A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
Timo-Leste is a Least Developed Country (LDC), a Small Island Developing State (SIDS) and a post-conflict nation. The country has experienced rapid population growth, urbanization, and environmental degradation. Climate systems have been devastated by a 25-year war of independence. It is ranked 131 out of 189 countries on the Human Development Index (HDI) and is one of the world’s least developed nations.

Climate change presents serious challenges to Timo-Leste's development, including increased climate-related risks to lives, livelihoods, biodiversity, food security, water supply and economic growth. The country is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to its small size, low-lying coastal areas, high temperatures, warming and rising seas, ocean acidification* and desertification*, less predictable and more extreme weather events, insect outbreaks, and increased frequency of natural disasters*. Climate variability and change has direct effects on the health and well-being of Timo-Leste's population; for example, climate variability can lead to food insecurity, malnutrition, water scarcity, and reduced access to clean drinking water. Climate variability and change has direct effects on the health and well-being of Timo-Leste's population; for example, climate variability can lead to food insecurity, malnutrition, water scarcity, and reduced access to clean drinking water.

The vulnerability of Timo-Leste is evident from the devastating impacts of the recent tropical cyclone, heavy rains, flooding and landslides that occurred between 29th March and April 2021, and which affected over

In addition to causing considerable human and economic loss, the flooding and landslides significantly undermined COVID-19 response efforts as Timor-Leste struggled to contain the virus' second wave. Heavy flooding in the National Laboratory and medical storage facility led to the destruction of a large number of COVID-19 test kits and other essential supplies.

Moreover, displaced people sheltered in improvised evacuation centres and in the homes of relatives, which lack basic sanitation facilities, increased their vulnerability to COVID-19 transmission. The confluence of natural disasters and the COVID-19 pandemic provide demonstrable evidence of the potential for a repeat of the "triple disaster" scenario, warned of by the UNDRR Asia-Pacific (UNDRR Asia-Pacific Brief No. 48/2020), following the devastating loss of life in Timor-Leste in April and May 2015.

The Timor-Leste Joint Appeal and Flood Response Plan, issued in May 2021, states:

- The dual challenge of climate change and a global pandemic highlights the urgency required to improve disaster risk management systems.
- The COVID-19 crisis can also be an opportunity for us to look at how to build greater resilience into the system.

The Timor-Leste Joint Appeal and Flood Response Plan identifies four inter-related components:

1. Strengthened delivery model and legislation for climate information and multi-hazard early warning services
2. Strengthened observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts
3. Improved dissemination and communication of risk information and early warning

# Enhanced climate risk management capacity

The Project will create a paradigm shift to evidence-based planning and early action for climate resilience through accurate, timely and actionable climate information, impact-based forecasting, people-centered multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS) and Forecast-based Financing (FbF). The Project will build the capacity of Timor-Leste’s National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD), the UN Environment Programme (UNEP) as well as other key national partners to underpin science-based transformational planning and programming, de-risk investments, and facilitate timely decision-making for climate resilience. This will be achieved by strengthening early warning systems that protect ecosystems that sustain them. The interventions are designed to demonstrate the value of climate data at all levels of Timor-Leste’s economy – from government policies to the decision-making of subsistence farmers in remote communities.

The Project will also build the capacity of national and local governments, civil society organizations, and communities to identify risks, vulnerabilities, and opportunities for adaptation. The project will support the development of a climate risk management framework that identifies risks at all levels of Timor-Leste’s economy from national to community level are identified and funded before a climate shock and become an integral component of disaster risk management and disaster risk reduction in Timor-Leste.

Cost-benefit analysis shows that the Project is economically viable. At 10% discount rate, the discounted economic net present value (NPV) is positive ($US56.01 million). The economic internal rate of return (ERR) exceeds the discount rate making the proposed investment economically viable* by reducing the risk of climate-related disasters and increasing resilience to climate change impacts and extreme weather events.

The Project’s impact potential is high, with an estimated 1,034,485 direct beneficiaries (90% of whom are women), 276,000 indirect beneficiaries (80% of whom are women) and 1.3 million total beneficiaries* of improved climate risk knowledge and effective systems for early warning of extreme events.

The Project Results will strengthen implementation of the WMO Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS) and the UNFCCC’s Strategic Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction, including: i) Early Warning Systems (MHEWS) prepared by the partners of the International Network for MHEWS; ii) Decision-making tools to support early warning systems; iii) Strengthening of institutional capacity and disaster risk management capabilities; iv) Awareness raising and communication; v) Preparedness response capabilities. Furthermore, The Project will directly contribute to the attainment of selected targets and indicators of the Paris Agreement (Article 4), the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, SDG 13 on Climate Action, and SDG 3 on Good Health and Well-Being and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction.

The Project has been developed at the request of and with the support of the National Designated Authority for Timor-Leste. The Project is aligned with national priorities and will contribute to the achievement of goals identified in major national strategic and policy documents, including the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) of Timor-Leste under the Paris Agreement. Ownership and implementation ownership will be ensured through strong stakeholder and community engagement. At the request of the NDA of Timor-Leste, the UN Environment Programme (UNEP) will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project. The AE will work in close collaboration with the National Designated Authority (NDA), the Implementing Entity (IE) alongside a range of technical partners and national service providers, including Timor-Leste Meteorological Service (BMKG), International Centre for Theoretical Physics (ICTP), World Meteorological Organization (WMO), Badan Meteorologi, Klimatologi dan Geofisika – BMKG), International Centre for Theoretical Physics (ICTP) and the World Meteorological Organization (WMO).

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Contextualisation and context (max: 1500 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### Background

Timor-Leste is a Least Developed Country (LDC) and Small Island Developing State (SIDS) situated at the eastern end of the Lesser Sunda Islands of the Indonesian archipelago, about km north-west of Australia. It comprises the eastern half of Timor island, the western part of Atauro island on the northern side of Timor, and the islands of Atauco and Jaco. The topography is dominated by a massive central mountainous chain rising to heights in excess of 2000 m above sea level.

Timor-Leste has a monsoon climate, which is characterised by a wet season from December to March and a very marked dry season from May to October – except on the coast and the southern slopes, where the wet season extends into April. The timing of the onset and retreat of the wet season varies annually. These year-to-year variations in Timor-Leste’s climate are related to El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD). El Niño generally brings drier conditions, while La Niña tends to bring above-normal rainfall in the dry season and below-normal rainfall in the wet season. The frequency of extreme ENSO events has increased as a result of global warming due to increasingly negative effects on climate variability.

#### Historic climate trends and expected future climate change

##### Temperature

In the period 1901–2005, temperatures in Timor-Leste increased by 0.5 – 0.8 °C. Data from 1979-2005 suggest a decadeal increase of 0.3 – 0.7°C with a mid-century acceleration over the later decades. There is an increasing trend in higher temperatures, which has been observed since 1961. The period 1981–2005 was warmer than 1901-2005 and there are indications that this warming will continue into the future.

Surface air temperature and sea surface temperature projections are expected to continue to increase. Under all RCPs, projected increases in surface air temperatures for the period up until around 2030 is a growing difference in warming between each RCP scenario. For example, by 2090 a warming of 2.4 – 2.7°C is projected for RCP8.5 and an increase of 0.3 – 1.2°C is projected for RCP2.6. Sea surface temperature increases are also projected to increase.

Increasing temperatures in the tropical Pacific will likely impact ENSO, which has resulted in a recent increase in the frequency of extreme El Niño events. This project is expected to double one event every 10 years under global warming in the period up until around 2030 and beyond. The frequency of La Niña events may also increase.

This indicates a positive (reinforcing) feedback mechanism between ENSO and climate change.


---
**Page 10**
---

Key climate hazards associated with increasing temperatures are outlined below:

* **Heatwaves** are projected to increase in frequency. More extreme temperatures could result in increased incidence of heat stress and death in local communities, which will particularly affect the young, elderly and infirm. The incidence of vector-borne diseases such as malaria and dengue fever is also likely to increase.

* **Wildfire hazard levels** are classified as high "in almost all of Timor-Leste", meaning that there is more than one fire per year in most areas. Wildfires can cause significant loss of life, property damage, and destruction of livelihoods, with a potential for loss of life and property in any given year. During the dry season, the vegetation dries, leaving significant highly combustible biomass. Ragged terrain and steep topography and a contrasting bimodal rainfall pattern are also conducive to wildfires. The frequency and severity of wildfires is projected to increase as well, with fire events peaking towards the end of the dry season in October and November. VIIRS imagery shows an increasing trend in the number of fires per year since 2014.

* **Increased air pollution:** Air quality is strongly dependent on weather and is therefore sensitive to climate change. Increasing temperatures and extreme heat can increase the concentration of fine particulate matter (PM2.5) and ground-level ozone (O3). Coupled GCM-CTM studies find that climate change alone will not significantly alter PM2.5 concentrations in Timor-Leste, but it is projected to have a significant effect on PM10 levels with the largest effects in urban areas and during pollution episodes. As highlighted above, the projected increase in frequency and severity of wildfires is also expected to lead to increased concentrations of particulate matter.

# Rainfall

Since the mid-1960s, annual mean rainfall in Timor-Leste decreased (relative to 1953–1960). Long-term seasonal analysis shows a consistently increasing trend in rainfall during the rainy season (7.6 mm/decade in December and January) while there is no significant change in rainfall trends in other months of the dry season, with a particular dominant decrease in rainfall over the last two decades. These long-term trends are indicative of climate change.

Short-term inter-annual variability is associated with annual increases or decreases in rainfall that lead to possible extreme climate events such as drought and floods. The onset of the wet and dry seasons also shows strong inter-year variability. Short-term inter-annual variability is linked to climate drivers such as El Niño, La Niña, and ENSO. These climate drivers are associated with changes in precipitation variability, particularly with regard to changes in intensity or location of rainfall during El Niño.

Long-term average rainfall is expected to remain similar in Timor-Leste, but extreme rainfall events are projected to increase by 2050. The frequency of heavy rainfall events will increase by up to 14 days per year, while dry season onset may be delayed by as much as 11 days. Projected changes in the spatial patterns of rainfall suggest that the wet season will become more concentrated over the western and central parts of Timor-Leste, with consequent increases in the total area of generally drier land areas (north and south-west). This suggests that heavy rainfall will be concentrated on smaller total land areas in the future. These changes in both spatial patterns and frequency have significant impacts on the water balance.

Key climate hazards related to rainfall are outlined below:

* **Floods:** One of the most frequently occurring extreme events in Timor-Leste, resulting from a combination of heavy rainfall, poor drainage systems, urbanization, rapid population growth, and widespread deforestation. Natural climatic drivers (i.e., ENSO) also affect floods. La Niña years are associated with above-normal rainfall leading to increased flooding. Future projections indicate that the frequency of extreme rainfall events will increase by up to 14 days per year, which is expected to cause increased risk of flooding and associated impacts for infrastructure, food, health, tourism and biodiversity.

* **Landslides:** Induced by flooding are reported to be one of the most common disasters in Timor-Leste, with the eastern half of the country particularly prone to landslides. A combination of climatic (extreme rainfall events) and geographic (steep slopes, high weathering rates and slope material with low shear resistance or high clay content) factors are the main precursors for landslides.


---
**Page 12**
---

# Drought

Tropical-Late experiences periodic extreme drought events associated with El Niño. Future projections indicate that the overall proportion of time spent in drought is expected to decrease slightly under RCP8.5 and stay approximately the same under all other scenarios, although there is low confidence in these projections.

### Tropical cyclones and storms

Seven tropical cyclones developed within or passed through the Timor-Leste EEZ since the period 1960–2010, all of which were Category 3. Tropical storms (wind speed of 35–117 km/hour) affect Timor-Leste annually and can be as devastating as cyclonic activity due to the deposition of extremely high amounts of rainfall in a short time period.

* An increase in the average maximum wind speed of cyclones by 2–11% and an increase in number of tropical storms by up to 30% are projected under RCP8.5.
* The frequency of extreme storm surges increases exponentially with wind speed, therefore an increase in the proportion of severe cyclones is expected to cause greater flood loss and damage. Increased incidence of extreme storm-related loss of critical habitats is also anticipated.

### Sea level rise

Sea levels have risen near Timor-Leste by about 89 mm per year since 1953. This rate is two times faster than their global average. Mean sea level is projected to continue to rise over the course of the 21st century.
* CMIP5 models simulate a rise of between approximately 8–16 m by 2030, with increases of 20–40 cm indicated for the period up to 2100. The rate of sea level rise is expected to increase over time due to melting ice sheets and glacier mass loss due to warming.
* Impacts of rising sea levels include the increased risks of coastal inundation that damages critical infrastructure and degrades coastal habitats, loss and damage to arable land due to saltwater intrusion, and freshwater sources.

### Ocean acidification and deoxygenation

Ocean acidification has been increasing in Timor-Leste waters and is projected to continue to increase through the 21st century. The rate of ocean acidification is driven primarily by increased oceanic uptake of carbon dioxide, in response to rising atmospheric carbon dioxide concentrations.
* In parallel to acidification, deoxygenation of oceans has occurred due to the decreased capacity of warmer waters to dissolve oxygen.
* Decreased oxygen levels can have a significant impact on marine ecosystems and biodiversity. Rising ocean temperatures, increasing acidification and reduced oxygenation of sea water will reduce the availability of fish for marine fisheries.

# Vulnerability of Timor-Leste to climate hazards and climate change

Timor-Leste is highly vulnerable to climate change and climate-induced hazards, and experiences recurrent disasters associated with droughts, floods, and landslides, resulting from climate drivers such as extreme rainfall, temperature fluctuations, and sea-level rise. These events are exacerbated by the country's topography, which promotes widespread deforestation increase climate risks. Socio-economic development is limited by the inability to respond appropriately to climate hazards, which in turn increases vulnerability.

About 70% of Timor-Leste’s population lives below the poverty line (2015), and most people rely on climate sensitive rain-fed agriculture as their main source of income, with very limited access to financial services. Over 40% of the population lives below the national poverty line. Low agricultural production combined with a lack of access to markets and infrastructure results in food insecurity for many households. The majority of Timor-Leste’s rural population suffers moderate and severe food insecurity, which is exacerbated by extreme climate events such as droughts and floods. In 2019, Timor-Leste was ranked the second most vulnerable country globally, after Syria, with the highest in the world. In 2010, Timor-Leste was ranked 110th out of 117 countries on the Global Hunger Index (GHI), with the GHI indicating a “serious level of hunger.”

Climate change will have significant impacts on human health and well-being due to increased exposure from extreme weather events, cardiovascular and respiratory diseases, infectious and vector-borne diseases and malnutrition) as well as under-water food and water supplies, infrastructure, health systems and social protection systems. Air pollution poses a major threat to public health in Timor-Leste. The country’s air quality is currently worse than the “safe” maximum of 10 µg/m³ recommended by the World Health Organization (WHO). Climate change can affect air quality through increased emissions from burning fossil fuels, deforestation and land use changes. Rising temperatures and extreme heat can increase the concentration of fine particulate matter (PM2.5) and ground-level ozone (O3), which are both harmful to human health. Both PM2.5 and O3 also contribute to increased levels of PM2.5. Both of these pollutants are detrimental to human health – increasing the risk of all-cause-and cardiovascular and respiratory mortality – which increases the burden of climate change on public health.

The majority of climate-related disasters in Timor-Leste are localised and periodic, with resultant significant impacts on local communities and sectors. A socio (village) level vulnerability assessment found that 61 out of the 442 villages were highly vulnerable to climate hazards. The assessment also found that more than one-fifth of the sucos can be categorised as less or not vulnerable. Around 80 percent of the population has poor access to health services, with the majority living in remote areas and/or isolated communities. Factors being those isolated in terms of accessibility and communications, and those with low capacity to prepare for and respond to climate risks. Table 1 summarises the main impacts of climate change on the five priority sectors of Timor-Leste’s National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) as identified by the NAPA process.

## Vulnerability due to COVID-19 and interconnectivity with climate change

As of 24 May 2021, COVID-19 cases continued to increase daily, particularly in Dili, with 5,637 confirmed cases as of that date. The rate of new infections has been high, particularly amongst the temporarily displaced.[*] In response to the COVID-19 pandemic, a State of Emergency was declared on 28 March 2020, which was extended until 30 June 2021. The country is currently in its third wave of the pandemic with an outbreak[*]. However, "the country's dependence on agricultural production, high levels of multidimensional poverty, unemployment and chronic malnutrition, lack of basic infrastructure, dependence on exports, and urbanization make it particularly vulnerable to the economic impact of COVID-19. The impact will be severe." The impact of COVID-19 is forecast to contribute to the sharpest fall in gross domestic product (GDP) since independence in 2002.[*] The World Bank estimates that GDP per capita could fall by up to 35% in 2021. May 2021 has seen Timor-Leste report its highest daily rate of infections since the pandemic began[*] and the CDC entered its third tiered lockdown due to concern that it could be facing its local first outbreak.[*] Restrictions were lifted on 4 June, but a new lockdown was announced for Dili and other areas in the country from 10 June. The impact on COVID-19 prevention and response efforts can be severe: the national medical storage facility has been temporarily evacuated due to the pandemic; the National Institute of Health's laboratory that provides the COVID-19 isolation facility had to be temporarily evacuated and the national laboratory was also affected.[*]

UNDRR has asserted that as countries in the Asia-Pacific region enter their cyclone, drought, heatwave or flood seasons, they will face a double challenge from climate change and COVID-19. The 2020 UNDRR pandemic highlights how risks rise significantly with compounding extreme events, with social and economic impacts of both pandemics and climate change being felt simultaneously.[*] Despite the increase in greenhouse gas emissions as a result of the pandemic, SIDS continue to be affected by existing climate change impacts and must address these simultaneously to recovering from the pandemic[*]. There is an urgent need to "focus on a multi-hazard integrated disaster risk management approach that includes high levels of disaster preparedness and accelerated disaster

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 12 OF 116

## Exposure to climate hazards and climate-related loss and damage

### Timor-Leste's geographical context, topography and lack of coping and adaptive capacity and strategies makes it the 15th most disaster-prone country in the world according to the World Risk Index (2019). About 80% of Timor-Leste’s population lives below the poverty line. The country has experienced a series of natural disasters that have caused over 80% of all disaster-related deaths since 1992.* Timor-Leste estimates its total estimated value of exposure to climate change and extreme weather events at US$46 billion (5% of GDP), transitory SDR $3.7 billion, commercial SDR $10.9 billion, education SDR 3.7 billion, industrial SDR 1.6 billion, health SDR 8.6 cents and crops SDR 0.7%.

### The Government of Timor-Leste has identified climate change and natural disasters as a major source of risk for the country. In response to this, the National Disaster Risk Management Act (2005) established the National Disaster Risk Management Policy (2008) elevated Disaster Risk Management to national priority.

### Using official government data, the UNDRR Desinfecting report 87 deaths due to meteorological events in 2019. The average annual mortality rate is estimated at 64 per 100,000 people and the average annual mortality rate of 9 deaths per year. However, casualties are likely to be underestimated because not all deaths are reported. In 2018, Timor-Leste experienced a series of natural disasters including floods, landslides, and droughts affecting most of the fatalities (86%). Based on the Pacific Catastrophe Risk Assessment and Financing Initiative (PCRAFI) (2017) modeling, Average Annual Losses (AAL) for Timor-Leste are estimated at US$3.1 million including direct economic losses and indirect economic losses. The total economic loss is estimated to be US$46 billion with a potential loss equivalent to 6.7% of combined gross domestic product (GDP) per year by mid-century due to climate change impacts is expected.* The damage costs of climate change are therefore significant for Timor-Leste.

## Current status of climate information and early warning systems

### Basic weather and climate services are currently provided by the Timor-Leste Direcção Nacional Meteorológica e Geofísica (DNMG) as a public good, funded from very limited public sector resources and with the support of neighboring countries of Indonesia and Australia, and other external agencies. DNMG is a WMO Category 1**, basic level meteorological service. It is poorly resourced and has no capacity to provide any advanced weather or climate services beyond the provision of basic weather services, which many communities are currently unable to access. As a result, Timor-Leste’s observation network and resulting data quality is rudimentary.

### The country does not have an operational meteorological observation network, all of which require calibration and maintenance. None of the stations are currently reporting to the WMO Integrated Global Observing System (WIGOS) and none are compliant with the WMO Global Basic Observing Network

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 105 OF 116

**(GCB)** has technical requirements. The Dili airport weather station is operated with the assistance of the Australian Bureau of Meteorology (BoM), which collects, processes and analyses the data for forecasting and for airport operations purposes. ConcorDilipos, an American multinational energy corporation, also operates a weather station at Dili Airport, but the data from this station are not yet available to BoM and therefore are not analyzed.

The features of the stations and their condition vary widely. The recent severe flooding in April 2021 has caused significant damage to infrastructure in Timor-Leste**, which is likely to have also impacted weather station operations.

### Cyclone tracking and early warning information

Cyclone tracking and early warning information is available from the Australian Bureau of Meteorology (BoM) and other international bodies calculating the frequencies of cyclones events.

Local weather stations are not able to provide reliable data. The data used for Timor-Leste comes from low resolution satellite sources, unverified by sea surface observations. Given that there is no tide gauge data available for Timor-Leste, the Wyndham (Western Australia, since 1984) record is used as the closest available gauge.

Maintaining adequate observation networks is very challenging, both financially and physically. Whilst DNMIS is responsible for collecting all the information from the country’s weather stations in a database**, it has not been able to collect data on a consistent basis due to limited resources and the lack of reliable ground scale weather information; if verification at ground level is impossible then timely and reliable local forecasts cannot be produced.

There is no end-to-end early warning system (EWS) in place in Timor-Leste and disaster risk management is fragmented. Lack of ocean data and sparse land-based data add to these difficulties. While informal coordination and sharing of information exists between the BoM, DNMIS and other agencies, there are concerns about understanding EWS being lacking in Timor-Leste amongst disaster risk reduction (DRR) actors working in Community Based Disaster Risk Reduction. In the absence of a functional end-to-end early warning system in Timor-Leste, it is difficult to assess whether or not the country has adequate capacity to respond to disasters; these efforts have not been coordinated or integrated into a broader national system. Therefore, Timor-Leste’s Strategic Development Plan (SDP) 2015-2024 includes an early warning system as one of its priority areas.

The important functions of civil protection, along with the protection of State assets, will be prioritised according to a management plan. This function will involve establishing, where possible, an early warning system for natural disasters and developing disaster response plans to protect our people.

A detailed assessment of the baseline needs, completed and existing projects, and priorities is provided in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

The National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) is responsible for providing disaster risk management coordination and technical support to the government and communities in Timor-Leste, including responsibility for preparedness and early warnings. The NDMD is composed of the National Disaster Operation Centre (NDOC), the National Emergency Operations Centre (NEOC), and disaster management committees at district, sub-district and village (sucu) levels. It works in support of the National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (2015-2030). The NDMD’s main functions are to provide guidance and knowledge; it develops strategies in disaster risk reduction including preparedness, response plans and protection of ecosystems and biodiversity; it provides early warning monitoring in the region and interprets and provides warnings and strategic planning; as well as its act on the contact points for climate change adaptation and disasters in conjunction with the NDOC. Within each village, the Suco Chief and village leaders are responsible for disaster risk management.

### Current finances for climate services and disaster risk management

The National Directorate of Meteorology and Geophysics (DMMG), under the Ministry of Transport and Communication, is responsible for the provision of climate services in Timor-Leste. In 2015, the annual state budget for DMMG was USD 67,000 on salaries and wages. In 2018, the total annual state budget for DMMS was USD 165,000. As with the national budget for climate services, the budget for disaster risk management varies from year to year. In 2018, the total annual state budget for NDMD was USD 743,000. Of this amount, the National Disaster Management Fund received USD 1.5 million from the annual state budget; the majority of which (USD 847,000) was allocated to disaster risk management and climate change adaptation projects in 2018, with the remaining funds distributed among 12 municipalities.

Timor-Leste also allocates funding for disaster relief and response to other sectoral ministries, namely agriculture, health, education and public works.


### External financing

External finance is limited due to the government’s climate change programme and disaster risk management on account of its highly limited financial capacity as a newly independent country. Lead Developed Country and Small Island Developing State (SIDS) donors have provided significant funding for climate services in Timor-Leste, with ownership over climate funding is limited as most financial flow is ‘off-budget’ via development partners and NGOs. A 2018 study by WaterAid highlighted that there is a lack of clarity and transparency in climate finance disbursement to the government. The government has also been criticized for its lack of coordination around the issues, with different ministries and working groups in coordinating climate change responses across government.

Despite these challenges, the government has made progress on disaster risk management support, as triggering important discussions about procedural changes that might facilitate climate-related fund disbursements through the national budget.


### Barriers to the delivery of effective climate information and early warnings

Timor-Leste starts from a low institutional base and lacks the weather and climate observation infrastructure and human resources required to generate reliable data on climate and population and impact-based multi-hazard early warning that can be easily accessed. The effective delivery of climate services, including climate information and early warnings is essential to support evidence-based policy, planning and early actions, and enable sectors such as agriculture, water management and health to better anticipate the impacts of climate change and take action to reduce their vulnerability to climate change and climate-related disasters. The main barriers to be overcome are as follows:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 5 OF 110

## Lack of legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks for climate services and disaster risk management:
### i) Inadequate observation networks and sector-specific climate information (e.g., health, agriculture, disaster reduction, environmental management)
### ii) Limited communication and use of climate hazard and risk information, including related health risks;
### iv) Limited capacity and funding to prepare for and manage climate risks.

**Problem statement:**
The national economy and most people's livelihoods in Timor-Leste depend heavily on climate-sensitive sectors such as agriculture and fisheries. Changes in precipitation patterns are already having devastating effects on the availability of fresh water, agricultural yields and access to arable land. Fisheries are under threat from the loss of coral reefs and other marine habitats that provide breeding grounds for fish species. Coastal areas and parts of the sea lives, livelihoods, assets and infrastructure are threatened by climate-related hazards, particularly drought, floods and landslides, but also coastal inundation. Air pollution, which is expected to worsen with climate change, will have a significant impact on human health.

Climate information and early warning services are essential to Timor-Leste's economic growth and sustainable development, but they cannot be consistently provided. Weather, climate, water and oceanic information services are not reliable or consistent in their delivery of data. The government does not have the capacity to collect and reliably reaching the people who need it. These services are crucial to enhancing resilience and reducing the vulnerability of communities and livelihoods.

The Government needs support in integrating climate information into its planning and management processes. The staff of the DMNG are constrained by a lack of human, financial and technical resources in their capacity to develop, implement and maintain climate services. The quality of data collected is poor due to inadequate equipment and monitoring equipment is inadequate. A value chain from data collection to delivery of services and response by users is lacking; communication and outreach systems, especially to remote island communities, are poorly developed or non-existent. The DMNG's technical capacity for developing and implementing early warning forecasting systems do not yet exist. Timor-Leste's capacity to protect its population from climate-related hazards is compromised due to a lack of ability to plan for the mitigation of the long-term impacts of climate change.

**Without the Project? Scenario:**
Without the proposed project, the DMNG will continue to provide basic weather forecasts with the help of external partners, but its information will be based on incomplete data for large areas of the country and for ocean areas, and it is not reliable. The capacity of the DMNG's technical staff responsible for disaster preparedness and warnings remains often unclear and their response actions reactive with delayed financing. Data critical to adaptation planning, early action and public health management will not be available in a timely manner. The development of climate services and early warning systems based on reliable forecasting systems has been decimated by decades of conflict and it is already less reliable as climate change affects the country's weather patterns. The DMNG's technical capacity for developing and implementing early warning forecasting systems do not yet exist. The DMNG's technically worded information difficult to understand and apply, and many communities will not reliably receive any usable information.

The DMNG's external development partners will continue their support, addressing some of the obstacles to effective adaptation, but underlying barriers will remain, undermining the sustainability of efforts. Severe hardship and loss of lives, assets and livelihoods will escalate as extreme events become more common and less predictable. The project is not likely to be sustainable or underfunded.

**Proposed actions for reducing vulnerability:**
The Project will enable a paradigm shift to evidence-based planning and early action through timely and actionable climate information, impact-based forecasting, people-centred multi-hazard early warning systems

#### (MNEWHS) and forecast-based Financing. This will result in a transformation to increased resilience to climate change threats, improved human health, enhanced livelihoods, and a reduction in the damage and losses incurred due to the increased frequency or intensity of climate-induced disasters, such as flooding and extreme droughts. The project will also provide access to early warning systems for communities, including an array of improved forecasts, early warning and early action. Social benefits will include improved public safety due to early warnings and better preparedness by communities.

The Project will be implemented in a way that ensures the use of information that is accurate, timely and actionable climate information is used in policy, planning and response actions, and sectors and communities in Timor-Leste are enabled to adapt and reduce their vulnerability to increasing climate variability and change. This will be achieved through the following results:

- **Result 1** will provide an enabling environment for adaptation and reduced vulnerability by developing legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks for services climate and disaster risk management, including a framework for forecast-based financing. By establishing a strong institutional architecture of intersectoral collaboration and multistakeholder partnerships for climate action, the Project will reduce the risk of maladaptation resulting from failure to account for multiple interactions and feedbacks between different groups.

- **Result 2** addresses the need for observation networks that provide the critical data underpinning weather and climate forecasts, which in turn facilitate timely warning of climate risks and hazards that enable communities to take early action. The Project will establish a national network of weather stations and other monitoring infrastructure, and integrate these into an information system that provides real-time data into sector-specific climate information for use by climate-sensitive sectors (e.g., health, agriculture and disaster risk management) in formulating appropriate responses and actions that reduce vulnerability and increase resilience.

- **Result 3** addresses the need to enhance communication and dissemination of climate risk and hazard information. The Project will establish a national network of weather stations and other monitoring infrastructure, and integrate these into an information system that provides real-time data into sector-specific climate information for use by climate-sensitive sectors (e.g., health, agriculture and disaster risk management) in formulating appropriate responses and actions that reduce vulnerability and increase resilience.

- **Result 4** addresses the need to increase the capacity of Timor-Leste—from national to community level—to better prepare for and manage climate risks, which in turn will reduce the impact of climate-related hazards. The Project will establish a national network of weather stations and other monitoring infrastructure, and integrate these into an information system that provides real-time data into sector-specific climate information for use by climate-sensitive sectors (e.g., health, agriculture and disaster risk management) in formulating appropriate responses and actions that reduce vulnerability and increase resilience.

The proposed interventions are designed to address the key barriers to delivering sustainable climate services and MNEWHS that provide the information and knowledge exchange underpinning effective risk reduction and adaptation. The Project will also support the development of a national framework for long-term climate resilience through the integration of climate services and MNEWHS into key policies, strategies, plans and programs at all levels of government. This will be achieved by increasing decision-making and planning in key sectors, such as health, agriculture, water and disaster risk reduction.

The integration of hydrology and hydromatics information is an important element of the project-based approach to climate services and MNEWHS delivery. To ensure engineering and hydraulics throughout the Project takes into account the low baseline capacity in Timor-Leste and has been carefully designed to complement the existing GCF-funded UNDP-led project "Safeguarding communities

Critical elements of hydrology and hydraulics are already covered in FP109, which includes flood risk mapping, flood modeling and forecasting for major river basins. The Project will build on these outputs and address the critical gap of integrating the observed and predicted climate information into a coherent framework to support decision-making by relevant agencies including, but not limited to, the National Directorate for Meteorology and Geophysics (DGM), the National Disaster Management Agency (NEMA), the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MENL), the Agriculture Information System (ALGIS) and the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries (MAF). The proposed Climate Data Informatics System (CDIS) for UNDP will host all hydrological data along with weather and climate data in a single platform.

Moreover, a sustainable delivery model for climate services will be established based on a value chain approach. This will include opportunities to develop value-added products and services and facilitate private sector engagement. The Project will highlight the importance of building capacity through training, knowledge management and learning. In this regard, the Project will specifically focus on maintaining climate resilience in Timor-Leste by strengthening national capacities for climate monitoring, local capacity building and targeted multi-channel communications to reach the most vulnerable populations.

B.2. Theory of change (max 1000 words; approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The Project interventions aim to shift away from the current paradigm, characterised by lack of national capacity to access and use climate information for decision-making, reliance on external inputs and standards, and to implement proactive and effective climate risk management and responses. In this new paradigm, the Timor-Leste Meteorological Service (TLMET) will be able to provide a wide range of climate services that are based on the best available science, including climate information and impact-based multi-hazard early warnings for use in policy, planning and early actions, and enable sectors (including health, agriculture, disaster risk reduction, water and environmental management) and communities to better manage their exposure to climate-related hazards.

In order to effect this transformation, the following key barriers will have to be overcome:

(i) Lack of legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks for climate services and disaster risk management in Timor-Leste. This is addressed by SDG 13.1, which aims to “Strengthen resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters” and SDG 17.5, which states the need for “Strengthening scientific knowledge on climates, including research, systematic observation of the climate system and early warning systems, in a manner that informs policies and strategies.”

(ii) Limited communication and use of climate hazard and risk information, including related health risks, as also recognised by the Sendai Framework on Disaster Risk Reduction, which highlights in its 14 “That Parties should enhance their capacity to monitor, assess and understand disaster risk and sharing such information...and enhancing capacities for early warning systems.” This is addressed by SDG 3.5, which aims to “Strengthen the implementation of national strategies for education, information and communication on climate change mitigation and management of global health risks.”

(iii) Limited capacity and tools to prepare for projected extreme climate risks, as also recognised in the Paris Agreement Article 1.2, which emphasizes the need to “enhance the capacity and ability of developing country Parties” and facilitate “access to climate finance, relevant aspects of education,

## Project Results

### i) Strengthened delivery model and integration for integrated climate information and multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS) in Timor-Leste
The project will establish a national climate service (NCS) based on the GFCC to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for the development of an NCS. The NCS will be established as a focal point for the delivery of the multi-hazard business model of the national meteorological and hydrological service (DMG). Moreover, it will instigate a best practice approach to data governance and advance the integration of climate, health, agriculture, disaster risk reduction, water and environmental management sectors in Timor-Leste.

### ii) Strengthened observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts
Amongst others, the project will establish an integrated climate service (ICS) based on the GFCC to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for the development of an ICS. The proposal will develop integrated climate information services covering oceans and island-based MHEWS in Timor-Leste, in alignment with the five components of the Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS): i) Observations and Monitoring (through Activity 2.1), ii) Climate Information System (through Activities 2.3-2.5), iii) User Access and Use (through Activity 2.6), iv) User Engagement (through Activity 1.f), and v) Capacity Development (building national capacity to deliver climate services will be a focus through all Activities).

The GFCS is based on the value-chain approach to climate services delivery, with an end-to-end early warning system that incorporates the four main elements of efficient, people-centred MHEWS: i) Disaster risk knowledge based on climate information; ii) Early warning systems (i.e., Detection, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of hazards and possible consequences); iii) Dissemination and communication of timely accurate and actionable impact-based warnings; and iv) Preparedness for disaster response at all levels.

There is a need to strengthen the resilience of Timor-Leste’s population to climate change impacts and climate-related hazards through the following measures:

Result 1: Strengthened delivery model and legislation for climate information and multi-hazard early warning services

The project will support Timor-Leste to establish a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS), which will structure the science-policy interface so that sector agencies have ready access to sound scientific data and useable information. It will support Timor-Leste’s Meteorological Service (DNMG) to create coordination mechanisms between climate services, disaster risk management, water resources management, agriculture and environmental management – to facilitate the integration of climate information and disaster risk knowledge into national planning processes. The project will also support the development of a National Early Warning Act and a National Meteorological Strategy. A systematic User Interface Platform will be established for continuing, regular and reliable access to climate services. This will ensure that all users have access to relevant climate information which will enhance coherence and complementarity, and inform the development of tailored, accessible products and services that serve the practical needs of end-users and ensure that climate information reaches the last mile.

Long-term sustainability of climate service delivery mechanisms will be achieved through the active maintenance and strengthening of institutional and stakeholder partnerships, identifying opportunities for future private sector engagement and investment as part of the development of a financial framework and business plan. The project will also support the Government in developing a national strategy to increase the allocation of public funding for water, weather and climate data collection, analysis and adaptation planning and options for other sources of revenue are explored based on the value-chain approach captured in the figure below.

Furthermore, this Result will instigate a best practice approach to data governance, initially for climate data, by strengthening DMNG's climate data management system. ### Activity 1.1: Establish institutional and policy frameworks, legislation and delivery models for climate services

#### Subactivity 1.1.1: Establish a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS)

The Project will develop a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for enhanced use of climate information and provision of climate services. The NFCS will be developed through the following steps: i) Conduct an international strategic consultation workshops on climate services to bring together the key stakeholders who link climate knowledge to adaptation action; ii) Identify the key actors and their roles in the climate service delivery chain; iii) Develop a national strategic plan and a costed action plan and timeline to implement the NFCS and improve existing services; iv) Convene a meeting with national stakeholders to endorse national strategic plan and the action plan; v) Establish an institutional framework for the NFCS, including roles and responsibilities of key actors in climate monitoring and evaluation; decisions will be revisited each year during the National Climate Outlook Forum and multi-stakeholder meetings and hearings. This process will be repeated in the second, third and fourth years with the private sector, NGOs and community representatives respectively and in an evaluation workshop with all stakeholders in the fifth year.

Once established, the NFCS will serve the following functions:

A platform for institutional coordination, collaboration & co-production amongst relevant technical departments across line ministries at national and sub-national levels, DNMG, and technical experts to develop and deliver user-oriented climate services. A framework for collaboration at the national level to generate and share user-oriented climate services for use by the relevant social and economic sectors. This will help stakeholders to identify and agree on specific climate information products and services that are needed, including those related to extreme weather variability and climate change impacts.

The platform will continually identify user needs for climate services, communicate available climate products and services to users in the relevant sectors, and obtain feedback from users on climate products and services.

A vehicle for a continuous monitoring to monitor the state of the climate at the national level and disseminate climate knowledge outputs for policymaker actions based on scientific evidence through the Climate Services Delivery System. A multi-stakeholder working group comprising representatives from government agencies (including technical departments) for the Five Global Framework for Climate Services priority sectors – agriculture, health, disaster risk reduction, water and energy) to identify and articulate how they need climate information products and services in their sector.

A vehicle for a continuous monitoring of climate science to the development of national adaptation plans, disaster risk reduction, Sustainable Development Goals and national development policies by enhancing the integration of climate information and products into decision-making as well as into national policies.


In order to clarify and formalise the mandate of DNMG and its relationship with other national institutions and sectors. This sub-activity will also support Timor-Leste to develop a Meteorology Act for approval by the Council of Ministers. The Act will provide legal basis for DNMG to perform its functions, including the responsibilities of the DNMG to provide weather and climate services (including flood forecasting) and formalise the DNMG's role in monitoring and analysing changes, advising on adaptation, contributing to warnings, and being a focal point for international cooperation. This sub-activity will also support Timor-Leste in areas such as including the National Directorate of Water Resources Management (DNIGRA), which has the mandate for hydrological services, to develop a Meteorology Act for approval by the Council of Ministers during budget scrutiny. The Draft National Meteorological Strategy will document the process by which DNMG will put its Meteorology Act into operation.


Sub-Activity 1.1.2 - Establish a User Interface Platform

A user interface platform (UIP) will be established as a forum for interaction between DNMG – as the climate services provider – and its stakeholders and end users of climate information services. The UI is a key pillar of the National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS), to be developed under Sub-Activity 1.1.1, and will focus on achievement of outcomes in four priority areas: Feedback, Dialogue, Outreach and Monitoring and Evaluation.

The National Climate Outlook / Monsoon Forum (NCOF) will be a critical component of the UfP and will be held twice each year, in October before the wet season and in March before the dry season. The NCOF will facilitate delivery of the intended outcomes of the UfP, as outlined below:

* Feedback from user communities to DMNG to obtain feedback on how effectively its products and services are meeting the needs of user communities.
* The Forums will enable DMNG to respond to forecast information requests from users.
* Elicitation of decision makers' information requirements for climate risk management.

### Dialogue
The NCOF will build dialogue between climate service users – including government agencies, NGOs, private sector representatives, civil society organisations, women’s groups and communities at large. The NCOF will also facilitate the exchange of knowledge among climate and information system pillars of the NfCS – in particular, DMNG. The Forums will provide a venue for DMNG to present its products and services, including climate risk management tools, in response to changing conditions and tropical cyclone incidence. The NCOF will include sessions on preparation planning, which will help to enhance users’ capacity to interpret and integrate forecast information of various time-scales.

### Outreach
The NCOFs will support improved climate literacy in the user community and literacy of the climate community in user needs. They will provide platform for knowledge sharing between climate service providers and users, including government agencies, NGOs, private sector representatives, civil society organisations, women’s groups and communities at large. The NCOF will also facilitate dialogue on the role of climate information in climate forecasting and the meaning and use of probability rankings about likely climate risks.

### Evaluation
The NCOFs will provide a mechanism to monitor and evaluate the development, delivery and impact of DMNG products and services. This will be done through an iterative process that meets the needs of stakeholders. This will facilitate that the NfCS promotes evidence-based decision-making and planning processes in the water sector. The evaluation framework (Sub-Activity 1.1.6) will include the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) plan and action plan (Sub-Activity 1.1.7) to be undertaken, which will provide feedback on the performance programme of DMNG. The M&E plan and action plan will also be used as a tool for continuous improvement in the NfCS using a results-based approach that is roughly based on the Logical Framework Approach.

The UfP will also serve as an opportunity to strengthen the climate service delivery framework (in this case, the NfCS) by delineating a hierarchy of Activities (How), Outputs (what), Purpose (Why) and Goals (greater why) that collectively lead to accomplishment of the greater Goal of the Framework.

The Water Sector – a priority area of the Global Framework for Climate Services – will be strongly engaged through the NfCS and DMNG, recognising the critical importance of ongoing climate data for the assessment of future water availability and quality (e.g. droughts and floods) as well as for effective Integrated Water Resource Management. Increased dialogue and joint action between DMNG and the water sector stakeholders is expected to lead to improved understanding of user needs and improved applications of climate information for the water sector. Moreover, dialogue facilitated through the NCOF will also help to better understand the needs of the wider and climate service community, enabling DMNG to better understand the decision-making context of water managers.

The NCOF will also be an opportunity to establish partnerships for implementing the Early Action Protocols (EAPs) developed in Activity 4.2. The sector workshops held in each year of the Project will also form part of the UfP, as well outreach work on making climate information more accessible to communities.

# Sub-Activity 1.1 - Enhance climate data management and governance

This sub-activity will support the Government of Timor-Leste and DMNG in managing and using climate data and information in conjunction with data from other sectors in order to mainstream climate considerations into the work of government, private sector, civil society and communities. This will be achieved by strengthening water and fisheries. Although some ministries have made significant progress on data collection, in most sectors, decisions are being made based on sub-optimal evidence. In addition, there is an immediate need for stronger inter-sectoral coordination to ensure that climate information is used effectively at all levels of government – at both the national and international levels. The Government of Timor-Leste wants to be able to combine data from different sectors in order to make better decisions on how best to manage water resources and the implementation of multilateral environmental agreements such as the Paris Agreement and the UNFCCC is general. As such, the following will be concluded:

- Develop a Climate Data Strategy through a series of consultative workshops with key stakeholders. A Climate Data Strategy and Action Plan for DMNG and the Statistics Directorate will be drafted to support improved climate-related data management, governance and enhanced inter-sectoral data coordination, particularly relating to water resources and fisheries.
- Strengthened data sharing and coordination among DMNG and other key stakeholders, including DGCS and the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR), to ensure that climate information is used effectively at all levels of government – at both the national and international levels. The Government of Timor-Leste wants to be able to combine data from different sectors in order to make better decisions on how best to manage water resources and the implementation of multilateral environmental agreements such as the Paris Agreement and the UNFCCC is general.
- Develop a Climate Data Strategy through a series of consultative workshops with key stakeholders. A Climate Data Strategy and Action Plan for DMNG and the Statistics Directorate will be drafted to support improved climate-related data management, governance and enhanced inter-sectoral data coordination, particularly relating to water resources and fisheries.

# Sub-Activity 1.2 - Develop a Climate Decision Support System

The project will implement a Climate Decision Support System (CDSS), which will provide an integrated platform for climate and scientific data analysis and visualization, as well as linkage to sectoral decision-making tools. As part of the installation of CDSS, DMNG will be provided with training in the use of the system. The CDSS will be seamlessly integrated into DMNG’s systems. The CDSS will also host all hydrological data (along with weather and climate data) to provide a single source for all relevant climate information. The CDSS will also provide access to other related products. As an intergovernmental entity, RMES will not charge Timor-Leste any licensing or authoring fees for the use of the CDSS. The CDSS will be hosted on RMES servers and will provide free ongoing technical support both during and after the life project lifespan. Installation of CDSS will enable the establishment and operationalisation of the national Forecasting Centre (Sub-Activity 2.2.1).

# Sub-Activity 1.4 - Mainstream climate risk knowledge into weather, agriculture, disaster risk reduction and other sectors

The Project will support a systematic five-year process of integrating climate considerations into the decision making and planning of government, private and community sectors by supporting them to understand and appreciate the value of climate information and how it can be used in their work. The Climate Science Access and Communication Plan (CSACP) will be co-developed with key stakeholders in each of the five GFCS priority sector areas. This plan will identify the specific needs for climate information, as well as the communications from the CSACP and sector-specific data and information products that will be used to conduct sector specific training by DMNG for the five GFCS priority sector agencies on climate basics and the logistics of integrating climate information into their work.

# Sub-Activity 1.5 - Establish a financial framework and business model for sustainable climate services

Once foundational capacity building for climate services is initiated, the Project will start to demonstrate the value, reliability and utility of climate products and services and build an expectation in government from different sectors on how best to utilise the information. This will address the ongoing needs for climate information (which are generally recognised and outlined in Section 6 of the Feasibility Study – Annex 2).

In order to prepare the ground for private sector engagement and mobilisation opportunities in the longer term (beyond the Project’s duration), this sub-activity will further scope viable opportunities for sectors and businesses

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE OF 119

## Result 2: Strengthened observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts

This result will strengthen the technical capacity and modernise Timor-Leste's national meteorological service (DMAG), enabling it to collect higher quality data at higher resolution and from a wider geographical range, including its coasts, and use the extra data. This will be achieved through the installation of new and upgraded infrastructure, including weather stations, radar systems, and satellite communication equipment; training and support for observations, monitoring, modelling, prediction, and through training in maintenance.

The Project will upgrade and expand the surface-based observations and monitoring network in Timor-Leste to enable the country to meet its obligations under the Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) technical standard approach for the international exchange of the most essential surface-based observational data and will facilitate access to GGBP (2020). The value of Surface-Based Meteorological Observation Data: Costs and benefits of the Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) technical standard approach, using the case study of Timor-Leste. Available at: https://opus.wahl.de/record/168439-human-quality-and-human-health-priority-action items. Further observations will enhance extreme weather monitoring and early warning systems, and support validation of the climate change vulnerability assessment for Timor-Leste. The Project will also provide the three critical elements of telecommunications, data management and forecasting – supported by technical training and capacity building to ensure that DMAG has the ability to translate weather, water, climate variability and change information into actionable advice for decision-makers in the public sector. The Project will also build national capacity to implement innovative and cost-saving technologies and practices, such as the deployment of a network of Timor-Leste Next Generation Weather Stations (TNGWS).

One of the key activities of this Project will be to establish climate services for health. Climate change is increasingly affecting public health and well-being – for example, deaths from extreme weather events, wider spread of vector-borne diseases such as malaria, dengue fever and chikungunya, increased risk from climate variability and change expected to magnify exposure to particulate matter, ozone and other pollutants. As such, there is an increasing demand for relevant, timely and usable information about weather and climate variables that can be used by health professionals in their daily work to help them make decisions – to take appropriate action to keep people safe and healthy. The Project will address this critical need through several activities: the development of a national climate change vulnerability assessment for Timor-Leste; establishment of an hybrid air quality monitoring system, air pollution forecasting and health impact advisory, and the delivery of tailored forecasting and decision-support systems for the health sector.

### Activity 2.1: Enhance infrastructure and technical support for observations and monitoring

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1: Expand and upgrade the meteorological observation network to GBON standards

The Project will expand and upgrade the surface-based observational network in Timor-Leste by implementing a new and upgraded weather station network in alignment with the draft WMO Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) technical standard approach, using the case study of Timor-Leste. The Project will also implement innovative technologies to address gaps identified in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2), by increasing the number of Automatic Weather Stations (AWS) and Automated


---
**Page 35**

# Weather Observing Systems (AROS) and building in-country capacity to ensure sustainable operations and data delivery

The following interventions will contribute to Timor-Leste's achievement of WMO Category 2 (Essential) status for climate services:

- **Enhance the network of surface-based observation stations** measuring atmospheric pressure, temperature, humidity, horizontal wind and precipitation, as a horizontal resolution of 200 km or better with an accuracy of ±1.5 hPa, ±0.3°C, ±4% RH, ±0.6 m/s, and ±8 mm/day, respectively, to meet GSON requirements, which will be finalized at the WMO Congress in 2021.[^1] The Project will install new climate monitoring stations: nine automatic weather stations (AWS) – including, amongst others, automatic rain gauges with tipping-bucket sensors – and one surface-based observation station. These AWS will be linked to a global Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP). Based on the Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFF), Timor-Leste will also install two additional AWS in 2021 (one at Dili Airport, with an elevation of 200 (+200 m) above sea level) to obtain the number of surface-based observing stations required. Timor-Leste (area ~15,000 km²) requires only one automatic weather station (AWS) to comply with GSON. Given that the maximum distance between AWS is approximately 300 km and the elevation difference is less than 200 m, the proposed equipment installations surpass the GSON requirements for resolution. All newly upgraded stations will report data on an hourly basis, thus ensuring compliance with GSON requirements for frequency.

- **Increase observation station density** based on national requirements and guided by the observation network design of the World Meteorological Organization (WMO). This will include the installation of a marine buoy, which will enable marine forecasts to be produced.

- **Introduce weather radar** for severe weather and climate monitoring through upgrading the network of three AWS. The radar will provide high-resolution, real-time, and continuous monitoring and remote support to build in-country capacity for radar operations, maintenance and data applications for weather and climate monitoring and analyses. Establishment of a network of low-cost, high temporal resolution, short-range radar stations is planned as an alternative solution to overcome the limitations of the steep mountainous topography challenges the effectiveness of more advanced long-range radar systems due to increased signal attenuation. The radar will provide real-time data for severe weather and climate monitoring, precipitation measurements by radar provide area rainfall determination, and thus complement point rain gauge measurements. Radar provides spatial coverage that is not possible with point rain gauges, which are grid-based models. Radar data also provide real-time data availability and the ability to track approaching storms before they reach the boundary catchment of interest.[^1] The blending of radar data with other sources will allow for a more accurate representation of precipitation in mountainous areas, advancing enabling prediction of rain cell development and decay as well as trajectory.[^1]

- **Improve observations** through compliance with WMO Integrated Global Observing System (WIGOS) requirements. This includes the establishment of an operational quality control system for operations and maintenance schedule, procurement of spare parts and calibration equipment, and iterative training on calibrations and maintenance with development partners.

- **Build capacity to use Information and Communication Technology (ICT)** for the collection and exchange of hydrometeorological observations, required for analysis, monitoring and forecasting and the

### Sub-Activity 2.1: Initiate an Internet of Things (IoT) Approach

The Project will support the DNMG in advancing towards an Internet of Things (IoT) approach to collecting data and delivering services. The Project will contribute equipment and training to support the development of innovative and cost-saving technologies for observations, modelling and prediction, with special focus on the application of IoT technology. This will be achieved through a project visit by the Project team to the International Centre for Theoretical Physics (ICTP)179, on the use of wireless connectivity and IoT for climate services and disaster risk management.

Workshop: 2023

The Project will implement a pilot study consisting of 20 low-cost weather stations based on IoT technology. The aim of the pilot will be to develop and demonstrate the potential of diverse, low-cost sensors to provide weather data. This will be achieved through the integration of three technologies into a common platform.

* LoRaWAN™-enabled weather stations will be deployed to send data to a solar-powered, GSM180-compatible gateway.
* Infrastructure will be set up to enable remote data collection and will be available for future extensions (water level monitoring, air quality, landslide detection, drought monitoring, etc.).
* A local server will collect data from TTN™ and will enable user-friendly visualisation of weather data.

### Activity 2: Strengthen climate modeling and impact-based forecasting

#### Sub-Activity 2.1.1: Establish a National Forecasting Centre

The Project will establish a Forecasting Centre with hardware, software, installation/set-up and training of DNMG staff in the use of the new equipment. This is crucial to strengthening DNMG’s capacity to deliver multi-hazard early warning products and services. The Centre will provide the three critical elements of telecommunications, data management and forecasting—including numerous databases and model/analytic engines for short, medium and long-range modeling and forecasting, flood monitoring and forecasting.

## Sub-Activity 2.2.2 - Enhance climate change risk modeling and prediction

The Government of Timor-Leste has recommended that academic institutions such as the National University of Timor-Leste (NUTL) and the University of Adelaide develop apps UNEPCC climate change scenarios to establish or improve drought and floods predictions in support of the DMNG. Specifically, for the Centre for Climate Change Adaptation (CCC) in the Faculty of Agriculture of NUTL, this project will provide some basic climate change related training to relevant stakeholders. The CCC will also develop a DSAT as a decision support system for agriculture technology.

This project will support the CCCB access to climate change models such as CMIP5 (Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5) and provide related training on risk models and output interpretation. The project will also utilise a training of trainers (ToT) approach to provide intensive technical training on climate models and outputs to relevant stakeholders in Year 1 so that they can enhance the accuracy of their trainings to relevant ministries and stakeholders.

### Sub-Activity 2.2.3 - Establish impact-based forecasting and decision-support systems for agriculture, disaster risk reduction and marine sector

The Government of Timor-Leste will establish a multi-hazard impact-based forecasting approach that translates information on weather and climate-related hazards into context-specific location-based impacts. It will provide technical support and capacity building to generate hazard sub-forecasts such as 3-weeks and 5-weeks forecasts of wave height, wind speed, direction, period, sea surface current temperature, and wind speed with associated confidence intervals. The project will also include some parameter ranges in its forecasting system. Concurrently, it will support DMNG and key stakeholders to translate hydrometeorological, oceanographic and socio-economic information into context-specific decision-support systems (DSs) for agriculture, disaster risk reduction (DRR), and marine sector to enhance preparedness planning, early warning and information dissemination.

### References

127 DSAT is a web-based system for generation of advisories based on location-specific ocean state forecast information. The system disseminates 3-day forecasts of wave size and wind speed, height, direction, period, sea surface current temperature, and wind speed with associated confidence intervals. It also provides some basic climate change related training to relevant stakeholders in Year 1.
