#### B

Within the scope of the Project, the aim will be to establish in-country capacity to integrate hazard forecasts produced by DNMG with vulnerability curves to generate potential impacts of forecasted hazards. Simultaneously, the Project will develop overarching DSS tools containing the data, models, analytics and visualization capabilities required for decision-making under uncertainty. The DSS will support four main activities: i) access and visualize climate information and advisories. In developing the sector-specific DSSs, technical partners will work intensively with DNMG to ensure that the DSSs are able to integrate hazard forecasts with vulnerability curves using existing data; ii) identify hazard thresholds and vulnerability curves (where available); and iii) develop impact assessments based on these hazard thresholds and vulnerability curves. The DSSs will be designed to support decision-making under different forecast-hazard scenarios. The DSSs will be developed through a partnership process to support risk-informed decision making of operational managers and users in climate-sensitive sectors. A key priority will be the provision of early warning information and advisories for farmers, pastoralists and other vulnerable groups. Enhanced cooperation in generating and sharing the derived products. Therefore, it will add value to existing projects such as DSSs, which are already being implemented in some countries. The DSSs will also support hazard assessments and flood-risk maps (descriptive and predictive analytics) for use in generating the analytics required for the impact-based forecasting.

The overall aim of this sub-activity is to complement and improve efficiency in the dissemination of forecasts, early warning and response advisories. It will provide a means for decision makers and disaster managers to correlate between scientific parameters and potential impacts by visualising information in a user-friendly, understandable and accessible manner. The DSSs will be designed with an emphasis on providing actionable information. Whilst enhanced risk knowledge can contribute to strengthened adaptive capacity in the longer term, it should be noted that the implementation of concrete adaptation and disaster risk reduction actions is not within the scope of the Project.

The sector-specific decision support systems to be developed under this sub-activity are detailed below:

* **Agriculture stress index system (ASIS):** This global system assesses the severity (intensity, duration) and spatial extent of agricultural stress. The ASIS will provide a daily updated map of agricultural stress, including the possibility for comparison with the agricultural statistics of the country. This sub-activity will calibrate ASIS for Tanzania using data from the existing climate monitoring systems in the country. It will also include the streaming of high-resolution satellite imagery (20 m resolution Sentinel 2 imagery) into ASIS and the training of ALGEC staff on the operation of the ASIS tool. It will train MAF staff on the analysis protocol for the ASIS, including the use of the ASIS to generate early warning information and advisories and triggers for early actions. The intervention will include both global and regional climate information and forecasts, as well as local weather data from meteorological stations and rainfall gauges. The ASIS will be used in monitoring field visits in municipalities—covering main agriculture production areas—to validate ASIS early warning products. A systematic drought analysis will be conducted at the maize and rice production areas. This will include an assessment of the performance of the existing climate monitoring system, analysis of drought vulnerabilities and risks to agricultural dependent livelihoods, drawing on the existing climate information and forecasts, as well as local weather data from meteorological stations and rainfall gauges. The ASIS will be used in conjunction with each sub-forecasted and action-oriented early warnings for dissemination to municipalities and villages.

* **Early Warning for Agriculture:** The information will feed into the Specialized Expert System for Agroclimatic Information (SEASI) which is currently being developed by DNMG. SEASI will address some of the constraints faced by farmers by giving them access to location- and crop-specific agrometeorological advisories. It will be designed as a web-based system that integrates data from high-resolution baseline information (cropland cover, soil etc.), hazard information (drought), and crop-specific information (sunlight and temperature requirements, pest and fertiliser information, input sources, etc.). The SEASI will provide early warning of the onset of droughts and other hazards. The existing baseline information will be integrated into the DSS to i) strengthen risk monitoring with agreed indicators and data sources; ii) support the development and dissemination of agrometeorological advisories

## Early Warning for Disaster Risk Management: This intervention will work with DNMG and the National Disaster Management Agency (NEMA) to develop a Decision Support System (DSS) called SMART—Smart Pre-Assessment and Emergency Response Tracking (SMART) DSS—a digital, multi-hazard, impact-based forecast and advisory platform that integrates all four elements of people-centric, multi-hazard early warning systems: hazard monitoring, risk assessment, impact assessment, and communication and resources, and will overlay forecasts from DNMG in order to generate real-time forecasts, impact assessments, and advisories for disaster risks related to extreme weather events, water and climate hazards from DNMG’s MAFAILIGIS and RIME/SUNDAS flood forecast model outputs. Data on historical disasters will be secured via NDMG’s disaster loss database. The SMART system will empower local communities to better understand the risks they face in their homes and communities to manage disaster risks holistically, by transforming generic weather forecast data into actionable information for decision-making at all levels of society, from individuals to communities through various channels—including SMS, mobile app and social media.

The development of their app versions will increase redundancies in information delivery to the disaster management sector, agriculture, and seawater sectors. ### Activity 2.3: Establish climate services for health

#### Sub-Acitivity 2.3-1: Establish a national Climate and Health Working Group

This activity will establish a group of experts and collaboration between the climate and health communities in Timor-Leste. It will convene and provide technical advisory to experts in meteorology and climatology (from DNMG), public health and epidemiology through the establishment of a National Climate and Health Working Group. Given that climate change is expected to have significant impacts on human health, it is important to determine the key climate-related health risks for Timor-Leste; identify climate and weather information and services needed by the health sector; and develop an appropriate climate service delivery system to meet the capacity of DNMG to meet the specific needs of the health sector. Multi-stakeholder task groups will be organized to improve knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts, images between health and climate sectors, and the development of the diagnosticclimate-related health services. The group will identify sources and methods to access, analyze and interpret both epidemiological and climate data. Representatives from health-relevant partners, disaster risk management actors, and agencies delivering health services at the community level will be involved in this activity.

level will also be invited to participate. The Working Group will also support development of the National Climate Sector Action and Communication Plan for Health (Sub-Activity 1.1.4).

### Sub-Activity 2.3.2 — Establish an air quality monitoring framework

This sub-activity will build in-country capacity to better understand, manage and reduce the impacts of poor air quality on human and environmental health in Timor-Leste through monitoring, forecasting, early warning and emergency response systems. The project will also support development of a national air quality index (AQI).

Climate change can affect air pollution both physically and chemically in a manner that reduces air quality. In particular, climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of heatwaves and droughts, which can lead to increased emissions of particulate matter (PM2.5) and ground-level ozone (O3). PM2.5 is a significant contributor to ill health (including cardio- and cerebrovascular disease, respiratory diseases, and cancer), while O3 is the most common air pollutant in the tropics with the highest priority pollutant for air quality monitoring. Nitrogen dioxide (NO2) is a precursor for PM2.0 and O3, of which exposure to high concentrations is associated with increased mortality due to respiratory and cardiovascular diseases.

The Project will establish a hybrid ambient air quality monitoring system consisting of low-cost sensors for fine particulate matter (PM2.5 / PM10), NOx, diffusion tubes and reference grade particle counters covered with MAAP-3000A filters. The project will also use satellite data from the MODIS sensor on board satellites provided from NASA® and Copernicus global products. The combined use of surface-based and satellite observations will enable the identification of hotspots of air pollution in the country, which can be used to target interventions.

Establishment of the monitoring system will be facilitated by development of an air quality monitoring framework — including a national AQI, data management plan, and communication strategy. In addition to the establishment and application of the new information and datasets, the Project will deliver in-country technical training and capacity building for the use of the new tools.

The data outputs will feed into a customised mobile application that can disseminate hyperlocal air pollution forecasts, warning alerts and health impact advisories and advocacy. Local stakeholder engagement workshops will be held to identify key stakeholders (e.g., local government officials, community leaders, and others such as vehicle emissions and burning of household waste), related health risks and appropriate actions to reduce impacts on health and well-being — including impacts on biodiversity and ecosystems health.

### Sub-Activity 2.3.3 — Co-develop tailored forecasting and decision support for health

This sub-activity will develop a national air quality index (AQI) and associated early warning system that will provide decision-makers with timely and relevant information to proactively manage weather and climate-related health risks. The Project will work with DNMG and the Ministry of Health to integrate weather, climate, epidemiological and other relevant data into an AQI forecasting model. This will be done in collaboration with the National Climate and Health Working Group (Sub-Activity 2.3.1) to ensure that the DSS is responsive and tailored to the most

**Result 3:** Improved dissemination and communication of risk information and early warnings as a key element of a people-centred impact-based Multi-hazard Early Warning Systems (MHEWS). The Project will focus on disseminating and communicating critical, actionable, and timely hazard-specific information, which are critical to enable appropriate preparedness and response actions that can safeguard lives and livelihoods. Engagement with “last-mile” communities to integrate Traditional Knowledge into MHEWS products and services will be a key intervention in this regard. ### Sub-Activity 3.1.2: Co-develop socially inclusive and gender-responsive localised communication strategies

The Project will engage in multi-stakeholder dialogue to analyse the effectiveness of existing warning communications, including those used by CVTL. This analysis will consider how different population vulnerabilities impact on the accessibility, reach and understanding of early warnings. This will be facilitated through a series of focus group discussions with key stakeholders (including CVTL staff, local government and socal council representatives) and community members – with a proactive focus on meaningful engagement with traditionally excluded groups such as women, youth, elderly people and people with disabilities, and including with indigenous peoples.

Based on the initial consultations, the Project will coordinate with the EWS working group (Sub-Activity 3.1.1) and communicate with key stakeholders to develop locally appropriate communication strategies and localised warning strategies based on understanding of last-mile connectivity (which population groups can be reached by different communication channels) and tailored to the differential vulnerabilities and needs of specific population groups.

The Project will also work closely with CVTL to ensure that the EWs are designed in a way which enhances the understanding and reach of science-based knowledge-bases communications. An important focus of this strategy development is on ensuring that the EWs are inclusive, accessible and relevant for all segments of the population in Timor-Leste. The Climate and Health Working Group (Sub-Activity 2.3.1) will be engaged in this regard to provide inputs and ensure coherence and complementarity with local public health messaging.

The dissemination of early warning messages should also consider how to address other barriers – such as social exclusion factors – to build trust between stakeholders and ensuring that communication and dissemination strategies are inclusive, accessible and relevant for all segments of the population. This will be done through Sub-Activity 4.1.1.

### Sub-Activity 3.1.3: Enhance community-based early warning systems

This sub-activity will work with The Timor-Leste Red Cross Society (CVTL) to establish community-based early warning systems (EWs). With trained volunteers at the municipal level and well-established community volunteer teams, CVTL will be able to effectively disseminate early warnings in a timely manner. This will ensure that early warnings are understood and translated into action. To this end, CVTL will train municipal and community-based volunteers to understand, monitor and communicate available forecasts, which will then be relayed to the wider community.

In addition, the Project will install flood/sealevel markers, megaphone systems, information signboards, and solar panels to detect landslides – a key hazard for which EWs are needed. Localised EWS frameworks will be developed in collaboration with CVTL and local communities to ensure that these tools are used effectively.

The Project will also work closely with CVTL to ensure that the EWs are inclusive, accessible and relevant operational and effective. These interventions will further complement Sub-Activity 4.1.1 building to enhance disaster preparedness under Sub-Acitivity 4.1.1.

### Result 4: Enhanced climate risk management capacity

The value of climate information and early warning services in supporting disaster risk reduction (DRR) is clearly acknowledged in the resilience agenda of post-2015 international agreements and frameworks [1] – in particular, the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030. The project will contribute to the achievement of Goal under Priority 4: Enhancing disaster preparedness for effective response and to “Build Back Better” in recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction. This Result is therefore designed to increase coherence and mutual reinforcement between climate information and early warning services (CIES) and DRR activities, including – and climate information and early warning services, which will be strengthened under Results 1 – 3.

Result 4 focuses on a capacity building to prepare for climate risks and hazards and the introduction of Forecast-based Financing (FbF) and Early Warning Early Action (EWEA). Both terms have become popular in recent years as a means of addressing climate-related disasters. The FbF approach is based on the idea that early warning systems can be used to trigger financial support for disaster preparedness measures, which will build capacity of institutions and people of Timsi-Leste to act at an early when warnings are received.

The outcomes of this Result will complement focused training on disaster risk management to be undertaken by DNMG staff through the GCF UNDP Project, Safeguarding Rural Communities. It will also put in practice the Disaster Risk Management (DRM) strategy and national DRM plan to reduce the impacts of natural disasters in the country, particularly from landslides, floods and strong winds.

### Activity 4.1: Build capacity to prepare for and respond to climate risks and hazards

#### Sub-Activity 4.1.1 – Enhance disaster preparedness capabilities from national to community level


---
**Page 46**
---

The Project will work with Cruz Vermelha de Timor-Leste (CVTL – Timor-Leste Red Cross Society) and the National Directorate for Climate Change (NDCC) to enhance disaster preparedness capacities from national to municipal to community level for effective action in response to climate variability and change.

At its core, this project will build on the momentum of the 2013-2015 Green Climate Fund (GCF)-funded Timor-Leste National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the 2016-2018 GCF-funded Second National Adaptation Plan (SNAP), as well as the outcomes from the two most recent Climate Change Conferences over the term of the Project, which will aim at enhancing country ownership and abilities to better prepare for and manage climate-related risks. In addition, the Project will support regular Community-Based Early Warning Systems (CB-EWS) workshops in all 13 municipalities of Timor-Leste, building on an existing coherent strategy for disaster preparedness, including plans and standard operating procedures. It will also build on the work done by CVTL and NDCC to develop a National Disaster Management Strategy (NDMS), as well as existing early warning processes and optimise their effectiveness in relation to preparedness capacities, which in turn is likely to result in early action at the local level. This will be complemented by targeted capacity building for disaster risk management (DRM) staff.

At the community level, the Project will use participatory approaches such as risk mapping and focus group discussions to support vulnerable population groups to assess their own exposure to and capacity to deal with climate-related hazards. The CB-EWS workshops will also provide an opportunity to discuss community-based preparedness and contributes to the development of community-based initiatives that are mutually supportive and complementary to national DRM strategies, including the National Disaster Management Strategy (NDMS) and the co-development of Community Action Plans (CAPs) to improve preparedness for early action in relation to climate-related hazards. The CB-EWS workshops will also provide an opportunity to discuss and identify suitable evacuation sites and routes, identifying strategies for protecting household assets and conducting simulation exercises.

In addition, this sub-activity will conduct community-based workshops across Timor-Leste in each year of the Project. The workshops will train community members on the use of climate forecasts to support contingency planning and decision-making processes. Climate change concepts and early warning systems (EWS) and climate change concepts and information will also be introduced. The overall aim of the workshops will be to build local capacity for understanding, interpreting and using EWS and climate change concepts and information in relation to climate-related hazards and providing an entry point to build trust with national knowledge systems and demonstrate their complementarity with local knowledge.

Sub-Acitivity 1.2.2 - Build capability of the National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) for EWS

This sub-activity will support the NDMD in building capacity to build the technological, technical and operational capacity of NDMD to utilise EWS information for performing its mandated functions in disaster preparedness. The Project will work with NDMD to co-develop Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) for disaster risk management and climate change adaptation. This will include the development of a national early warning system that facilitates a constant state of preparedness and, in the longer term, reduce disaster risks.

The Project will ensure that staff at all 13 Municipal Disaster Management Committees (MDCMs) of Timor-Leste have access to EWS information through the use of mobile devices such as smartphones and tablets (BDOTL™), and that the MDCMs have the requisite knowledge to oversee appropriate data collection by the Suco (village) Disaster Management Committees, for which they are responsible. Improving disaster loss data and enhancing the capacity of local communities will also be supported through the use of mobile devices in Timor-Leste and will provide valuable inputs to the impact-based forecasting and decision-support systems developed under sub-activities 2.2.3 and 2.3.3.

In addition, the subactivity will facilitate equipment maintenance and upgrade to ensure reliability of communication channels and early warning system hardware, supported by technical training and IT expertise for long-term sustainability. Sub-Activity 4.1.3 - Increase public awareness and education on climate hazards, related health risks and early warning

Despite the prevalence of disasters in communities around Timor-Leste, there is relatively little awareness of risk, and limited understanding or knowledge on how to use early warnings or weather information. As a fully functional end-to-end early warning system (EWS) will be established by the end of 2018, it is important that communities are able to implement a broader awareness and education campaign to ensure that communities across Timor-Leste better understand climate hazards, risks, the early warning services available, and appropriate actions to take to reduce their vulnerability. This will include targeted awareness campaigns for women, youth, elderly people and people with disabilities) and participate in disaster risk education workshops.

The project will establish local women’s networks – through linkages with women's groups and local NGOs/CSOs – that will be trained on climate change and early warning systems, and will seek to educate women on how to use risk information to take actions at the household and individual levels to protect themselves from climate hazards. The women’s networks will also serve as multipliers for broader disaster risk awareness and education campaigns to other communities, thereby developing local educators and enhancing sustainability beyond the Project implementation period. These interventions will support implementation of gender-inclusive and gender-responsive localised communication strategies developed under Sub-Activity 3.1.2.

Sub-Activity 4.1.4 - Conduct a targeted disaster risk awareness and education campaign for women

This sub-activity will focus on empowering women through enhanced awareness and understanding of disaster risks, climate change impacts and early warning systems, and decision-making for climate resilience. The Project will establish local women’s networks – through linkages with women's groups and local NGOs/CSOs – that will be trained on climate change and early warning systems, and will seek to educate women on how to use risk information to take actions at the household and individual levels to protect themselves from climate hazards.

The women’s networks will also serve as multipliers for broader disaster risk awareness and education campaigns to other communities, thereby developing local educators and enhancing sustainability beyond the Project implementation period. These interventions will support implementation of gender-inclusive and gender-responsive localised communication strategies developed under Sub-Activity 3.1.2.


### Activity 4:2 Establish Forecast-based Financing (FbF)

The project will introduce a Forecast-based Financing (FbF), also known as Early Warning Early Action (EWEA) – in which, on an indicative basis, timely early-preparesness actions at community and government level are pre-planned based on in-depth forecast and risk analysis, and resources are automatically allocated when a specific threshold is reached. Fb-EWA has been shown to enhance preparedness,

## Figure 5. Forecast-based Financing conceptual framework (Source: Adapted from IFRC)

The approach taken in the Project follows the precedent set by the approved GCF project "Enhancing Climate Information and Knowledge Services for resilience in 3 island countries of the Pacific Ocean" (GCF.17), for which a roadmap was developed to guide the implementation of an innovative mechanism that would improve disaster preparedness and enable more efficient management of government budgets. The International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC) has been involved in forecast-based financing spearheading FbF, and has designed this activity based on their experience in developing FbF in low-capacity countries and will lead its implementation together with Timor-Leste's National Red Cross Society (CVTL).

### Sub-Ac2.1: Establish a Roadmap for FbF

This document outlines the approach to forecast-based financing in Timor-Leste, which will identify the early warning information – based on forecast attributes (magnitude, probability and lead time) – and decision criteria that can trigger worthwhile action. The Roadmap will include the following components:

1. World Food Programme. 2015. Forecast-based Financing: (FbF) - Anticipatory actions for food security.
2. IFRC. 2017. Forecast-based Financing: Early effective actions to reduce flood impacts.
3. IFRC. 2020. Forecast-based Action (FbA) by the DREF.
4. Lopez, Coughlan de Perez, Bacci, Suarez Van den Hout, Van Aalst. 2018. Bridging forecast verification and humanitarian decisions: A review of existing approaches.
5. IFRC. 2021. FbF Practitioners Manual.


---
**Page 49**
---

# Stakeholder Identification – The Roadmap will identify the key stakeholders to be involved in the development and implementation of FfS, including international, national, regional and local actors and lead agencies.

## Risk Assessment - The Assessment will utilize a participatory approach to analyze risk factors, key hazards, past impact, exposure and vulnerability. Based on the analysis, the priority impacts to be addressed will be identified. The assessment will provide an overview of the different types of early actions that could be implemented (e.g., early warning systems, capacity building, disaster preparedness, water, health, etc.).

### Impact-Based Forecasting (Triggers) – The Trigger is the specific threshold value, based on weather and other indicators, that dictates when an early action is initiated. In line with an impact-based forecasting approach, the trigger model will be developed based on detailed risk analysis, and analysis of exposure and vulnerability.

## Financial Mechanisms - The Roadmap will identify a country-driven, scalable financial mechanism for FfS that will provide a sustainable source of funding for early action suitable for the context of Timor-Leste.

The first phase in development of the Roadmap will comprise a scoping study that will cover feasible hazards to target with FfS, forecasting capability, and the institutional landscape in Timor-Leste. For FfS to be a sustainable and effective tool, it must be embedded in national institutions, which have roles and responsibilities for taking early action.

The scope study will involve key stakeholders (government, development partners, private sector, civil society) and enter into a dialogue with them about the potential for FfS, its effectiveness, and added value to existing disaster risk management systems.

Following the scoping phase, the second phase of Roadmap development will consist of collaborative consultations with institutional stakeholders and local communities to delineate three key elements that would enable FfS:

- Defined triggers, based on improved forecasts, for preparedness actions aiming at avoiding losses and damage if an extreme event materializes;
- A "menu" of the available early warning information derived from forecasts that can trigger the early action;
- A potential framework of country-level technical working groups and institutional ownership for FfS, including funding mechanisms when necessary. A lead national agency in which to embed EAP will be identified.

Forecast-based Financing is only as strong as the National Society supporting it [162]. Accordingly, the Project will undertake extensive capacity development to ensure that CIVIL has the requisite knowledge and skills to develop and implement FfS. The Project will also seek to align the principles of FfS with existing national and sub-national strategic mechanisms and/or priorities to improve coordination and enhance country ownership. In addition, the Project will identify opportunities for scaling up FfS in Timor-Leste and the principles and activities embodied in other areas of the Project will be identified – for example, collaboration with the national Climate and Health Working Group (Sub-Activity 2.3.1) to identify opportunities to implement forecast-based financing.

Building on the scoping study, the Project will identify a financial mechanism to ensure that key stakeholders have access to immediate, reliable and sustainable funding in the event of Early Action Protocol (EAP) activation. The Project will initially explore existing financial mechanisms currently operational in Timor-Leste, and hence

### B

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND**

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 80 OF 116

assess a variety of potential financing tools for early action – including dedicated funds, specific windows in existing response mechanisms (e.g. IFRC’s Disaster Relief Emergency Fund™), and direct links to regular resource allocation processes, including ring-fenced budgets. This scoping and assessment will identify whether efforts are needed to establish new funding mechanisms or to enhance existing ones as a complementary source of funding for FIFG. Given the existing baseline, the financial mechanism is expected to utilise public sector resources, with some additional contributions from private sector actors. The project aims to build a foundation for mobilising and integrating private sector resources in the longer term.

Finally, the project will develop the process of Early Action Protocol (EAP) development. The EAP is a tool to guide the timely and effective implementation of early actions based on impact-based early warning information. It outlines the steps required to trigger, execute, and monitor early actions, as well as the decision-making processes for the implementation of early actions once the trigger has been hit. It is to identify what should be in the EAP. The team will convene a technical working group, engaging stakeholders at all levels – including community representatives, disaster risk management officials, government officials, and other relevant actors such as local authorities, national departments, NGOs and private sector actors. The technical working group will build on the consultations in the second phase of the project to identify early actions that are most likely to be effective. It is expected that they will jointly identify and select the most appropriate forecast-based early actions. The draft EAP will be presented to the identified key Stakeholders for validation and refinement. It should be noted that actual implementation of the EAP will be a separate activity.

**Sub-Activity 4.2.2 – Develop capacity for Early Warning Early Action (EWEA) in agriculture**

This sub-activity will build capacity of national, sub-national and community-based partners to utilise impact-based forecasts and early warning information for FIFG/EWEA in agriculture – given the importance of this sector for food security and livelihoods.

The Project will work with DMCIC, NDMO and the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries (MAF) to enhance mutual understanding of the value of forecast-based early action for the agriculture sector and facilitate collaboration to develop a national Early Warning Early Action System. This will be done through the establishment of Sectoral Committees (SCMs), MAF local staff, agriculture extensionists and local NGOs will be engaged to enhance the focus on resilience and, by building on the existing framework of Transfers of Tonnage (ToT) – to develop capacity of key stakeholders in utilising improved forecasts, EWS and on-the-ground information. This is supported under Results 1–3) to identify early actions with the highest potential to reduce the identified impacts.

#### B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

**Accredited Entity**

UNEP will be the Accredited Entity for the Project and will be responsible for managing the implementation, financial planning, evaluation, reporting and closure of the activities under the Project. UNEP will monitor and supervise the execution of the Project and ensure the proper management and application of GCF Grant Proceeds in accordance with the terms of the Grant Agreement and the Procurement Procedures as set out in accordance with the terms of the Funded Activity Agreement to be entered into between GCF and UNEP and the Accreditation Master Agreement. UNEP will also assume a limited role as Executing Entity, as described in the relevant section below.

UNEP brings more than 25 years’ experience working on climate change issues and is an established GCF Accredited Entity. It brings a comprehensive approach to climate change mitigation and adaptation that is grounded in both natural science and economics and is led to the environmental and development concerns of


---
**Page 51**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 47 OF 116

#### B

**countries. Based on its core science-based mandate, one of UNEP's seven sub-programmes is entirely dedicated to keeping the world environment under review.**

Through its Science Division, UNEP has longstanding expertise in environmental and climate change information management systems, including those related with GEF and GCF funding it is currently supporting over 50 countries in establishing or strengthening their environmental information management systems and using them for reporting progress on SDGs and MEAs. Its Science Division manages the CLMWRN and Country Level Environmental Information Management Systems (CLMWRN and CLEIMS) for the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF). UNEP also convenes and facilitates regional environmental information networks and the world environment outlook process, which provides a comprehensive assessment of global environmental trends and foresight. UNEP enhances stakeholders to respond to the latest emerging issues related to environment and climate change. A concrete example is the UNEP-led initiative on "Enhancing Climate Information and Knowledge for Pacific Island Countries" (ECIKPIC) in support of the Pacific Community.

UNEP is also a key player in the "One Health" approach - a cross-cutting and systematic approach to health that recognises the interdependence of human, animal and environmental health as critical for addressing the three pillars of sustainable development. UNEP is a member of the One Health High-Level Expert Council. UNEP is supporting the collection, distribution and publication of data on zoonotic diseases in collaboration with the World Health Organization (WHO) and other partners.

In addition, the United Nations Country Team (UNCT) works to ensure inter-agency coordination and decision-making at country level. The UNCT provides a framework for cooperation among UN agencies, funds and programmes in support of national development priorities. The UNCT is already participating in the UNCT for Timor-Leste. The work of the United Nations in Timor-Leste is guided by the UN Sustainable Development Cooperation Framework (UNSDCF) 2021 – 2025, which outlines the actions needed to support the Government of Timor-Leste as it works towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals and its national development priorities. The UNSDCF was developed in close consultation with the Government of Timor-Leste, civil society organizations, UN agencies, funds and development partners. This Project will specifically support national efforts in the UNSDCF Strategic Priority 6, which aims at achieving enhanced resilience to climate change impacts and natural hazards.

UNEP and UNEP-UNDP have a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) that provides for collaboration, cooperation and complementarity with the UNDP GCF-funded Project F090. To this end, and UNEP and UNDP have a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) to increase collaboration and joint activities in support of internationally agreed environmental goals and objectives. The MoU is based on existing collaborative arrangements and lessons learned seeking to further enhance effectiveness and avoid duplication.

Enhancing the UNEP-UNDPF strategic partnership is a priority of both organisations to deliver synergies, demonstrate clear added-value and show that multi-lateral action is more than the sum of its respective parts. The two organizations are committed to work together as equals, out from the Project and both organisations are committed to continuing collaboration long beyond the project.

**Project Management Unit (PMU)**

At the request of the NDAF, UNEP will set up and manage a Project Management Unit (PMU) drawing on its Global Support Team and in close cooperation with UN Office for Project Services (UNOPS), where necessary. The PMU will provide management and support to the national implementation of the Project through coordination by the national Executive Unit (SE); and Technical Partners involved in Project implementation, in line with their


---
**Page 52**
---

obligations under the respective legal instruments and will coordinate to ensure that reports are received. The PMU will collate all half-year progress reports and quarterly financial reports, including on-going financial reports and annual audit reports, from the EE and Technical Partners and submit these to the AE. The PMU will provide guidance and support to the EE and Technical Partners in addressing any issues related to project implementation and technical issues. It will establish contact with other development partners working with Timor-Leste to ensure that adequate arrangements are made for collaboration. The PMU will also provide secretariat services to the Project Steering Committee (PSC).

The PMU will be staffed by three full-time staff reporting to UNEP in its EE capacity: a Project Manager (PM); a Fund Administration Assistant (FAA) and a Financial Management and Procurement Officer (FMMPO). The FMMPO and FAA will report directly to the PM. All staff will be recruited in line with UN regulations, rules and policies. The PMU will be overall responsible for the day-to-day management of the Project. This role will include an emphasis on:

* Coordinating the Project Steering Committee (PSC);
* Managing the Project in line with the budget and workplans, and in accordance with GCF and UNEP policies;
* Being responsible for financial management and disbursements;
* Coordinating the national EE and Technical Partners to manage the Project effectively;
* Coordinating the provision of technical support and guidance to the EE and Technical Partners to report to the AE; and
* Ensuring exchange of information and knowledge across the EE and Technical Partners.

To achieve the targets of the proposed Project the PM will, inter alia:

* Acquire on-the-ground information to inform UNEP progress reports;
* Engage with Project stakeholders;
* Arrange and participate in other meetings;
* Provide technical support, including measures to address challenges to Project implementation;
* Participate in training activities;
* Write technical reports;
* Facilitate project-related activities; and
* Establish the necessary operational linkages with the UNDP team managing FP109 in Timor-Leste as part of the overall UNEP-UNDP Strategic Partnership.

Additionally, the PM will liaise with members of the PSC, technical experts, government staff and stakeholders involved in the implementation of the project to ensure that:

* A range of equipment (i.e., AWS, AWOS, weather radars, etc.) and communication infrastructure upgrades will be procured to strengthen services provided by Timor-Leste, based on the priorities identified in the Feasibility Study [Annex 1] and as required for the ground-based meteorological services. The PMU will provide guidance and support to the EE and technical partners on procurement in line with the provisions of the respective Project Cooperation Agreements (PCAs). The PMU will also ensure compliance with UN rules and regulations related to procurement.

**Executive Units (EUs)**

The Secretary of State for the Environment (SSE) in Timor-Leste will serve as the national Executive Unit (EE). The SSE will be accountable to UNEP as AE for Project execution at the national level and for the effective and efficient use of resources.

## Project Steering Committee (PSC)

The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established comprising the NDA (also serving as national EE) and UNEP. It will meet at least once per year and will be co-chaired by the NDA and the AE (UNEP). The PSC will provide high-level oversight and guidance towards achieving Project objectives. The PSC is a consensus-based decision-making body that provides strategic direction, including policy advice on project implementation, technical direction and policy guidance to the Project team. Among other functions, the PSC will review and approve the annual work plan and budget for the Project, as well as any amendments thereto, subject to approval by EE.

The committee will also provide recommendations on Project approaches and participate in discussing general strategies and opportunities for project planning and implementation.

The functions of the Project Steering Committee are:

- Providing overall guidance for Project execution to the PMU, especially on cross-cutting issues which require consensus from the various stakeholders involved in the Project;
- Ensuring that recommended policy and institutional strengthening undertaken under the Project is consistent with national priorities;
- Ensuring full cooperation of various regional and national stakeholders under their jurisdictions to provide access and support to the Project team in carrying out their tasks;
- Representing the interests of civil society and communities derived from a regular formal dialogue between the PMU and EE;
- Reviewing and monitoring progress in Project execution; and
- Ensuring that complementarity with other GCF-funded projects (in particular, the UNDP-led FP10) is effectively operationalized.

The members of the Project Committee will be the NDA, the UNEP AE Representative and national EE representatives. Observers will include:

* The Project Manager;
* Representatives from the Technical Partner agencies involved in Project implementation – FAO, RIMES, FRC and others as appropriate;
* Representatives of additional entities involved in Project implementation, in particular DNMG, NDMD, DNGRA, MAF and community-based organisations with experience in disaster risk management;
* Representatives of civil society and women’s organisations; and
* Representatives of the private sector.

and capacity building for NDMO to plan, deploy and maintain wireless infrastructure.

B. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Timor-Leste is a Least Developed Country (LDC), a Small Island Developing State (SIDS) and a post-conflict newly independent country whose infrastructure and governmental systems have been devastated by a 25 year war of independence—between 1975 and 1998, in which about one-third of the population was killed [6]. In 1999, Timor-Leste gained its independence from Indonesia. The country has experienced significant economic growth since then, but it is still a fragile state with high levels of poverty and unemployment: 1400 Timorese were killed, 300,000 people moved to West Timor as refugees and the majority of the country’s infrastructure was destroyed [7]. The country has made significant progress in its economic development since 2006. Although it is now stable, in 2019 GDP per capita rank is estimated at 148th of 181 countries listed [8]. Timor-Leste faces many challenges including a lack of infrastructure and services, limited access to finance for developing private houses and public infrastructure and challenging the country’s economic development. Climate change is increasing these impacts and contributes to food insecurity, prompting further degradation of the environment and livelihoods.

Infrastructure, institutions and essential services in Timor-Leste are being rebuilt from a very low base and climate services must compete with more visible needs such as health, education and basic infrastructure. Direct revenue will be generated by the project through the sale of carbon credits and other market mechanisms, making financial feasibility. Although there is a clear need for climate information, there is currently no real market demand for climate services in Timor-Leste. The country has limited capacity to generate its own climate data, limited weather information services and little capacity to pay for it, given that the private sector is weak and primarily dependent on government contracts. The Project is expected to generate interest in using climate information products and markets in the future but this will be a long-term process. Timor-Leste has not yet developed any market mechanisms during the Project duration. Nonetheless, Result 1 includes the development of a financial framework and business model for DNMKs, which will pave the way for financial sustainability in the future (i.e., after the Project’s lifespan).

Early warning services are considered as a public good that can be expected to be provided as an outcome of budget approved by the Government. The government has not yet developed any system of Timor-Leste’s early warning services at this moment. The basic level of information being provided, its limited reach and lack of understanding about how to use it is often the reason why there are no demand for the associated public funding that would ensure its improvement in the future.

Although the Project is expected to generate interest in using climate information products and markets in the future but this will be a long-term process. Timor-Leste has not yet developed any market mechanisms during the Project duration. Nonetheless, Result 1 includes the development of a financial framework and business model for DNMKs, which will pave the way for financial sustainability in the future (i.e., after the Project’s lifespan).

Although the Project is expected to generate interest from the private sector in using climate information products and MHEWS, cost-recovery is not considered feasible during the 5-year term of the Project. Timor-Leste’s government has no capacity to provide public funding for climate services and it does not have a mechanism by which to pay for climate information services and so cost recovery for these essential services is not practicable.

Donor and bilateral support are also currently not available for the comprehensive approach to improving climate resilience in Timor-Leste. The lack of data on climate change impacts, vulnerability and adaptation, as well as relation private company or donor is currently likely to do so. However, strengthening and extending the observation network will provide valuable information that can be used by private companies and donors to invest in surface monitoring equipment. This data will be most valuable to future development partners in planning adaptation activities and de-risking investments, thereby avoiding waste and maladaptation. Specifically, it will contribute to the following SDGs: 13 (climate action), 2 (zero hunger) and 7 (affordable and clean energy).

Provision of grant funding is fully in line with the GCF Board Decision B.07/04 (ii) in reference to the initial results management framework of the Fund: section (ii) Project/programme level outcomes for adaptation calls for (6.0)

The Project is also closely aligned with key government policies and commitments – Timor-Leste’s UN Sustainable Development Agenda (SDA) 2011–2025, its Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs), its Initial National Communication to the UNFCCC (2014), the Environment Sector Strategy (ESS) 2013-2025, the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) 2016 and the National Disaster Risk Management Policy (2008). The highest priorities of key stakeholders in climate change adaptation are identified as: (i) improving access to information on Climate Change (2017); (ii) increasing awareness about climate change; and (iii) building capacity for disaster risk management. These priorities were identified during extensive consultations with in-country users and potential users of climate information.

### Key elements of the exit strategy, ensuring sustainability by country ownership and institutional capacity building:

* The project is closely aligned with key government policies and commitments – Timor-Leste’s UN Sustainable Development Agenda (SDA) 2011–2025, its Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs), its Initial National Communication to the UNFCCC (2014), the Environment Sector Strategy (ESS) 2013-2025, the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) 2016 and the National Disaster Risk Management Policy (2008). The highest priorities of key stakeholders in climate change adaptation are identified as: (i) improving access to information on Climate Change (2017); (ii) increasing awareness about climate change; and (iii) building capacity for disaster risk management. These priorities were identified during extensive consultations with in-country users and potential users of climate information.

* The Project is also aligned to the objectives of WMO’s Regional Area V (RAV), which covers South-East Asia and the Pacific. Partners in neighbouring Indonesia have committed to peer-to-peer South-East Asia-Pacific Climate Information Services (SEACIS) project, which will be implemented in the Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan 2011–2030, in particular through improving cooperation between government agencies to support vulnerable people impacted by natural disasters and building strong institutional capacity for climate change adaptation.


---
**Page 63**
---

B

The Project will strengthen the existing national hydrometeorological service (DMNG) and National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA) to provide a more comprehensive suite of climate services and disaster risk management. It will build on the DMNG's existing strengths and capacities and leverage the NDMA's institutional, operational infrastructure, human resources, systems and processes), and the achievement of DMNG certification will contribute to sustained operations and improved service delivery. The Project will also support the development and adoption of the National Framework for Climate Services, and its fine-tuning over the Project’s term, to ensure the effective integration of climate services into national planning and decision-making processes. It will progress from initial discussion of priorities and options, using feedback and review mechanisms, to developing a comprehensive framework that can be used by end-users at all levels. The EWS will be fully integrated into the national emergency management system, with information on climate risks cost transparent to the Government and its development partners.

The Project will adopt a broad multi-stakeholder approach and bring on board different ministries, agencies, departments, local governments, communities and others, representing both producers and users of climate information and early warning products. The National Climate Outlook Forum (NCOF) will be established as an inter-ministerial platform for the exchange of information held on mutual and complementarities, and leveraging synergies from existing stakeholders, networks and platforms. It will also serve to facilitate coordination among different actors in climate cooperation and maximise synergies between the multiple actors in water, weather and climate services in Timor-Leste— including the UNDP project team and national stakeholders included in the GCF project (FIP3). The NCOF will be a key mechanism for ensuring that climate information is used to inform policies and outcomes. Capacity development activities and awareness raising will enhance the capacity of all stakeholders involved, from producers to users, to better understand their roles and responsibilities within the impacts of climate change. The public outreach and demonstration outcomes will be documented, and benefits quantified, to generate evidence to convince policy- and decision-makers of the added value of climate information and early warning services supported by the Project and invest in its long-term maintenance.

The role of Result 1 is to establish a sustainable delivery model for climate services in Timor-Leste based on a value chain approach to service delivery. This will include establishment of a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration between national and international partners. The NFCS is based on the Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS), which is the principal framework for delivering climate services in support of sustainable development.

The NFCS will be supported by stakeholder coordination mechanisms – including establishment of a User Interface Platform and National Climate Sector Action and Communication Plan. This will ensure that climate services are tailored to meet the needs of different sectors, while risk-informed investment will be essential for technically sound, effective and sustainable delivery models. The benefits of investing in climate services far outweigh the costs; the losses they prevent will help establish a sustainable delivery model.

The Project will establish a new framework for financing climate services that includes: (i) public-private partnerships to provide funding for climate services and services; (ii) public-private partnerships in the generation, translation and transfer functions; and (iii) private sector investment and sponsorship. The Project will establish a new framework for financing climate services that includes: (i) public-private partnerships to provide funding for climate services and services; (ii) public-private partnerships in the generation, translation and transfer functions; and (iii) private sector investment and sponsorship.

The Project will introduce new and cost-effective hydro-meteorological equipment and tools for strengthening climate service delivery. This will include: (i) a mobile weather station; (ii) remote sensing equipment; and (iii) an automated weather station. The Project will also establish a low-cost weather radar network as an innovative means to complement GBON observations, which will be used in conjunction with the existing GBON network to provide more commercial value to climate-sensitive industries such as agriculture in the future.

## B

### Identification of a country-driven, scalable financial mechanism that will deliver predictable and sustainable funding: FfF/EWEA will be incorporated into the overarching financial frameworks for climate services to maximise coordination between the climate and disaster risk management sectors.

The Project will maintain climate resilience at the last mile through its emphasis on community engagement, capacity building, and targeted communications to reach vulnerable populations. The Project will engage with communities through targeted and gender-responsive approaches that are tailored to their needs and capacities. This will include co-development of community-based early warning systems, disaster risk management approaches, Community Action Plans (CAPs) and Early Action Protocols (EAPs) to build preparedness capabilities that can be sustained over time.

The Project has developed a comprehensive plan for Operations and Maintenance (O&M), which outlines how specific O&M needs and costs will be addressed and budgeted for both during and post implementation period. The draft plan provides as Annex 21 will be refined after the Project's inception and implementation. Furthermore, DNIAG has formally committed to sustaining some of these activities beyond the end of the Project (e.g., data collection, training, capacity building & Maintenance) after its implementation period. In addition, as a Technical Partner in this Project, the Regional Meteorological Centre (RMC) has also formally committed to continue supporting DNIAG after the end of the CFC project. This includes back-up support, technical assistance, and system upgrades to the Climate Data Information System (CDIS) as well as decision support systems for climate services and disaster risk management in Indonesia.

During implementation, the Project Manager will ensure that O&M records are included in annual national budget submissions to the Ministry of Finance and that all O&M costs are recorded on the project financial point in the year to ensure that they are included in budget preparations and to establish an accurate picture of the sustainability of the climate services beyond the end of the Project.

The Indonesian Meteorological, Climatological and Geophysical Agency (BMKG) will build the capacity of DNIAG to sustain equipment operations beyond the Project implementation. Moreover, through the Global Best Practices (GBP), BMKG will provide technical assistance in the areas of meteorology, hydrology and climate observations required for global Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP) are a public good to be freely available to all users.

The Indonesian National Climate Change Fund Facility (SCF) is being established as a priority of the Alliance for Hydromet Development, of which the GCF, GEF, Multilateral Development Banks and the AE among others are members. The SCF is envisaged to launch at the 2018 Climate Week in New York. It will provide financial resources during the Project implementation period; technical and financial assistance from the SCF is expected to be available for the operation of the climate services beyond the end of the Project. This will enable Timor-Leste to access on-demand O&M advisory services through tailored and hands-on peer-to-peer support via the WMO Country Support Initiative.

Donors supporting infrastructure, agriculture, fisheries, health, transport, tourism and water management activities need reliable data for climate-resilient sustainable development planning and implementation. The Project will contribute significantly to the observed value of improved datasets and regular reporting of these outcomes through Project communication channels. This will be achieved by the establishment and operation of the improved observation network, data management and new DNMS functions. The Project includes significant capacity building inputs across government bodies and civil society organizations to enhance their ability to respond effectively to climate variability and climate change. Transfer of appropriate technology will be effected through the contribution of modern, low emission technologies, as well as training in the use and maintenance thereof.

Over its five-year term, the Project will help build national capacity to provide the essential high-resolution data and climate information needed in planning all nationally and internationally funded development projects.

The Project interventions are designed to establish the value of climate data to all levels of Timor-Leste's economy from government policy- and decision-makers to subsector farmers, and to establish sustainable financing mechanisms for climate change adaptation. All four Project Results include capacity building activities. Result 1 will enable Timor-Leste to develop institutional and policy frameworks for strengthened climate information services, working with the National Disaster Management Office (NDMO) and other relevant government bodies and civil society organizations in sensitive sectors, including disaster risk reduction, water and health. A comprehensive National Framework for Climate Change Adaptation will be developed, which will provide a strategic framework for accessing climate information, building their capacity to implement effective adaptation policies and practices. In addition, the Project will build capacity in data governance by strengthening the NDMO's climate data management


---
**Page 69**
---

system with the support of RILMES, Timor-Leste’s Statistics Directorate will also be supported in using climate data and information with data from other sectors in order to mainstream climate considerations into the work of those sectors. Strengthened data sharing and coordination among DNGRA and other key stakeholders, including the National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) and the National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) and the Statistics Directorate, will maximise the value of existing datasets (including remote sensing observations) and advance the integration of multi-sectoral data for improved forecasting and early warning.

Result 2 will fund the procurement and installation of new technology and build DNGRA capacity in climate information services. This includes developing a Decision Support System (DSS) to support decision making, it will also develop the customised System for Multi-Hazard Potential Impact Assessment and Emergency Response TDecision Support System (SMART DSS) and enable relevant agencies to utilise impact-based decision-making tools. The project will also establish new technologies for health risk forecasting and decision support, including a mobile application for early warning of heatwaves and other extreme weather events.

The Project will also establish new technologies for health risk forecasting and decision support, including a mobile application for early warning of heatwaves and other extreme weather events. DNGRA, Ministry of Health, the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries, and other relevant authorities will ensure that the technology is used to improve disaster preparedness and response capacity in the short- and long term. Advancement in establishing Internet of Things (IoT) for innovative weather, water and climate applications will further contribute.

Result 3 will enhance DNGRA’s capacity to deliver effective Early Warning Systems (EWS) and work with agriculture extension officers, building capacity to understand climate data and its implications and to explain information to the farmers they advise. It will also work with communities to co-develop socially inclusive and gender-sensitive early warning systems and tools for disaster risk reduction and resilience through public awareness campaigns.

Result 4 will enhance disaster preparedness capacities from the national to community level through targeted training, workshops, awareness raising and education. It will upgrade the NDMD’s EWS infrastructure and access to the Internet and to consistent IT support, which will ensure that NDMD is able to effectively monitor and respond to extreme weather events in a timely manner.

Furthermore, agencies (Government ministries and directorates, local and international NGOs) with current responsibilities and experience in disaster preparedness will be supported to accelerate their capacity for forecasting and early warning of extreme weather events corresponding to extreme climate events, including through the development of Forecast-based Financing capacity.

This Project will deliver transformative impact in Timor-Leste. It will increase resilience and enhance livelihoods of the population of Timor-Leste through a paradigm shift to evidence-based decision making, and an integrated approach to climate change adaptation. The Project will build multi-hazard early warning systems for extreme weather events, including cyclones, floods, landslides, droughts, and heatwaves. It will also provide access to high-resolution data, information and risk knowledge. This includes 1,034,495 direct beneficiaries (80% of the total population), which is the estimated population most at risk from worsening climate-related hazards. The Project will also increase the resilience of coastal communities in Timor-Leste against extreme events. People are also affected by coastal inundation, storm surges and cyclones. Both coastal populations and those who depend on stand areas are increasingly at risk from loss of livelihoods and assets as well as injury and loss of life.

The cost-benefit analysis (CBA) for EVS in Timor-Leste shows that the internal rate of return (IRR) is 104% above the discount rate, making the proposed investment economically viable. Sensitivity analysis has been used to test the robustness of this result under different assumptions about the future costs and benefits of EIRR decreased with those simulated cost benefit changes, the IRR remains well above the 10% threshold in both cases (90.2% and 85.3% respectively). In addition, an even more pessimistic scenario considering a 40% increase in the cost of EVS interventions and a 60% decrease in the benefits of EIRR ($46.81 million vs 79.4%). The overall conclusion is that proposed EVS interventions will have a positive impact on Timor-Leste’s economy, environment and society. The CBA analysis provides positive quantitative justification for the Project, which is estimated to generate a benefit-cost ratio of 5.71. This suggests that each USD 10 invested will translate to benefits with a value of USD 57.

The Project will also reduce the vulnerability of Timor-Leste’s population and communities to extreme climate-related disasters and is expected to achieve an overall reduction in damages and losses through increasing the safeguarding of assets (such as fishing boats and equipment, agricultural land and properties) that are most at risk from climate change. The Project will also increase the resilience of communities affected by the Project. Actionable climate information products, particularly for agriculture, health, water and disaster management, will be provided to communities in Timor-Leste. This will enable them to better manage their losses and damage to crops and other assets. ### A.2 Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security

The Project will promote multi-stakeholder climate action, bringing together actors in sectors such as public health, agriculture, water resources management, disaster risk reduction, and others to enhance their capacity to provide actionable knowledge to enhance their resilience to increasing climate threats. Improved understanding of the impacts of climate change on these sectors will be achieved through a combination of research, development of sector-specific decision support systems together with targeted training, awareness-raising and climate-related health impact advisors who will enable decision-makers—from government leaders to individual citizens—to make informed decisions.

The Project will also address food security by increasing access to information about climate-sensitive water resources. This will be achieved through the engagement of multiple stakeholders across the services value chain – for example, through the reduction of barriers to the flow of information between farmers and service providers, prioritization of water security priorities and development of the most appropriate products and services to address these priorities. Moreover, the Project proposes to increase the proportion of households that have secure food and water supplies by 2025 in Timor-Leste through improved access to climate-sensitive information about events that threaten food production and water supplies.

A high proportion of rural households are known to be vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their reliance on rain-fed agriculture, limited access to modern climate technologies. Currently, Timor-Leste has insufficient capacity, data and infrastructure to provide adequate warnings for extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, heatwaves and tropical cyclones, including specific forecasts and early warnings to inform climate-resilient planning, and the Project will also address the need for early warnings of extreme events.

A key element of the NAPDRP—Safeguarding communities and their physical assets from climate-induced disasters in Timor-Leste (FP10) is addressing key elements of hydrology and hydraulics, which will contribute to enhancing water security. This includes flood risk mapping, flood modelling, and forecasting, as well as the development of a Decision Support System (DSS). The DSS will be based on integrating the observation data from monitoring stations (including hydrological and hydrological meteorological stations) with climate data in a single platform for easier access, quality control, and generation of derived products. The DSS will also include an early warning system to provide timely information to communities to account for low baseline capacity in Timor-Leste and has been carefully designed to complement FP109, which is focused on flood risk management and the development of a national flood risk management strategy allocation.

A multi-level institutional arrangement has been developed to facilitate establishment of synergy and coordination. The arrangement is based on the National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (Annex 2).

### A.6 Increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making

The Project will substantially expand the geographical coverage of weather and climate observations in Timor-Leste and its coastal waters by modernizing observation and communication equipment, refurbishing and upgrading existing stations, and establishing new ones where necessary to meet the requirements of the Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) requirements. Additional data will be made available as information to government sector agencies and other relevant platforms (such as cloud computing).

The Project will also support the development of a Decision Support System (DSS), which will provide climate-sensitive sectors and other relevant stakeholders, with a particular emphasis on the agriculture, health, water and disaster risk reduction sectors. It will support DNGK and key stakeholders to translate hydrometeorological data into actionable information for decision-making. The DSS will be based on forecasted information in turn will feed into sector-specific decision-support systems (DSS). The overall aim of the DSS will be to provide a means for decision makers and disaster managers to correlate between scientific parameters and potential impacts by visualising information in a user-friendly, understandable and actionable format.

---

The Project will establish a people-centric, improved multi-hazard early warning system (MHEWS), described in Annex D of the National Feasibility Study – Section 6) of the existing situation and in partnership with organisations active in disaster risk management. Result 4 addresses an integral element of climate change adaptation: building community capacity to better understand climate risks and hazards, equipping and empowering communities with the risk knowledge to make informed decisions to better prepare for and respond to climate-related hazards. The project will focus on climate risk reduction and strengthened resilience and adaptive capacity. The actual implementation of specific adaptation or “response” activities is beyond the scope of the Project.

The Project will build upon and strengthen the work of extension officers with substance and commercial farmers on restorative agriculture. It will provide training and awareness workshops to build community capacity to prepare for forecasted climate-related hazards. A targeted awareness and education campaign will be developed, implemented, and evaluated in collaboration with relevant stakeholders to support increased participation in disaster risk management and decision making for climate resilience. The Project will also introduce a new early warning system (EWS) for coastal communities to ensure that EWS information reaches the “last mile” in addition; the Project will introduce seasonal forecasting and generate data from ocean areas, which will support safety of life at sea, generate local-scale forecasts, and provide early warnings for coastal communities through robust, reliable technology.

The Project will work with communities and partners to develop basic understanding of climate processes and climate change, and related health and environmental risks. It will support the development of effective preparedness plans and strategies that include community participation in the acceptance of the DMNG’s warnings and advice, and contribute to saving lives, livelihoods, crops and assets.
