The GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 89 OF 110

The inclusion of activities that aim at building preparedness capabilities (namely Result 4) is in line with the approved GCF project FP147 and are included as a key element in a holistic climate information and early warning services (CIESW) project designed to deliver transformational impact to the last mile. Such activities provide an opportunity for the CIESW project to contribute to the achievement of SDG 13, by increasing the value of climate information and early warnings towards reducing the impact of climate-related hazards and promoting sustainable development in vulnerable communities. The inclusion of Result 4 is also aligned with local knowledge. The inclusion of Result 4 is in line with international best practices for delivering end-to-end, people-centred early warning systems (EWS) that can be used to reduce the exposure of people and communities threatened by hazards to a level at which they are able to respond in an appropriate manner to reduce the possibility of personal injury and illness, loss of life and damage to property, assets and the environment; and, where possible, to prevent or mitigate adverse impacts on livelihoods. The inclusion of Result 4 is also aligned with the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) Global Framework for Early Warning Systems. The need to ensure that financing covers all segments of the EWS value chain is also a key strategic recommendation in the WMO 2020 State of Climate Services report.

The Project will enable a paradigm shift to evidence-based planning and early action through accurate, timely and actionable climate information, impact-based forecasting, people-centred multi-hazard early warning systems (EWS), and improved decision-making. The project will build on lessons learned from the 2015-2016 El Niño and early actions demonstrated to save lives. This will result in a transformation to increased resilience to climate change threats and enhanced livelihoods, and a reduction in the damages and losses incurred through extreme events. Improved decision-making and evidence-based planning will also reduce economic costs and potential economic losses resulting from better preparedness to extreme events due to the use of improved forecasts, early warning and early action, with sustained engagement and involvement of end-users in disaster risk management.

Potential for scaling-up and replication

The Project is expected to achieve transformative change in the understanding and use of climate information at all levels, from national to community level. The National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) will be developed to enable delivery of best practice climate services through strengthened institutional capacity and improved coordination between government agencies, civil society organizations, private sector entities, and other stakeholders. This will include development of policies, strategies, and plans, providing a solid foundation for the uptake of climate information into decision-making and upscaling of Project interventions. This will be further complemented by development of a sustainable financing mechanism to ensure long-term sustainability of the project activities and the identification of cost-recovery options, and institutional and capacity building of DMAs. To sustain forecast-based action beyond the life of the project, it is envisaged that the NFCS will provide a framework for the development of climate products and services tailored to specific end-user needs with intrinsic replication potential, as does the development of methodologies, standard operating procedures (SOPs), and other tools and guidelines. The selection of priority activities and associated interventions will be selected to optimise the spatial distribution of activities and outcomes, thereby increasing the likelihood that climate resilient approaches (e.g., community-based disaster management and multi-hazard early warning systems) are adopted by communities in a sustainable manner with high potential for in-country scalability of community-based activities and South-South knowledge sharing (including with FP147). Supporting replication in other Pacific SIDS. Finally, the User Interface Platform


---
**Page 74**
---

will enable training, advice and lessons learned to be communicated to multiple stakeholders, which will facilitate the successful upscaling of Project activities across Timor-Leste.

### Potential for knowledge and learning

Timor-Leste’s circumstances mean that it will benefit from the lessons learnt by other SIDS in the transition towards a low-carbon economy. Established relationships with Pacific SIDS neighbours will be useful, particularly with countries experiencing similar constraints and implementing similar programs, through which Timor-Leste can learn about best practices in climate change adaptation, including procurement methodologies, technology option decision-making, the development of effective communication techniques and in identifying potential business models, drawing on the experience of neighboring countries.

The Project will promote robust knowledge management – including through targeted capacity building, training of trainers (ToT), mentoring, attachments and technical advisory visits – which will facilitate that information is shared effectively across the country. The Project will also engage with RAMES, the Indonesian meteorological service (BMKG) and the Indonesian Red Cross (Pembangunan). This will strengthen Timor-Leste’s capacity to share knowledge and lessons learned in relationships with neighbouring countries and establishing links between DNMG and nearby national meteorological services in more developed countries. Networking with Pacific Island countries engaged in similar projects, such as those supported by SIDS Platform, with both Timor-Leste and Pacific countries will foster sharing of lessons with particular reference to SIDS.

At the national and sub-national level, the National Climate Outlook Forum will serve as a knowledge management platform for climate change adaptation. The forum will facilitate the exchange of knowledge and lessons learned. The national Climate and Health Working Group will enhance this function within its existing structure. Knowledge management through capacity building, mentoring and learning will be a key focus of Project interventions – from technical training for agriculture extensionsists to community-based disaster risk reduction (DRR) officers. This will ensure that climate change is integrated into the traditional and scientific knowledge systems, and the critical importance of integrating such knowledge for effective climate resilience at the local level. Traditional knowledge provides observations and interpretations all relevant to climate change adaptation, including information on seasonal patterns and weather events that may be overlooked by climatic scientists.137 Engagement with communities to develop Localised Climate Information Systems (LCIS) will ensure that traditional knowledge is used effectively in decision-making and that the knowledge improves community understanding of climate science, and DNMG’s understanding of how traditional knowledge is used. Involvement of communities in the development and implementation of Project interventions will also ensure that the project is locally owned and that lessons learned are shared to ensure local appropriateness of international best practices.

Furthermore, the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan and regular activity monitoring processes will be used to review and report on progress throughout its 5-year term to ensure impact evaluation, with progress documented and published.


### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

An important element of the Project is to support the establishment of an enabling environment for long-term sustainability, which will be facilitated through the integration of climate services and MHEWS into key policy, strategies, plans and the national budget. This will provide a foundation for update of climate information systems and the development of seasonal climate outlooks throughout the Project’s duration. ## Forums, the Climate and Health Working Group and the new framework for financing climate services will be critical to this action.

Enhancing coordination and forging robust multi-stakeholder partnerships for climate services, early warning systems, disaster risk reduction, water resources management and public health will create an enabling environment for long-term resilience. This includes the development or strengthening of policy, legal and regulatory frameworks conducive to increased political commitment, and to awareness in public and private sectors on how to effectively use climate information services. The project will also support the allocation and mobilisation of the resources required to sustain resilient building. The Project will build capacity for early warning systems (EWS) and emergency weather and climate information services (EWICS) for agriculture and food security. In the longer term, this will facilitate that climate-resilient early actions from national to community level are identified and funded before a climate shock and become an integral part of development planning.

Furthermore, The Project contains several interventions that will emphasise and support private sector involvement. In particular, the User Interest Platform will provide a forum for dialogue and wide partnerships between the public and private sectors. This will help to identify opportunities for public-private partnerships and mutually beneficial cost-recovery mechanisms. The Project will identify opportunities for private sector investment in climate services and will develop a business case to catalyse private sector interest. Moreover, the new financial framework will emphasise private sector involvement by providing a clear mechanism for mobilising resources from the private sector. In developing the framework, The Project will identify suitable revenue streams and effective strategies to incentivise and mobilise private sector resources.

Continuation to the regulatory framework and policies

Timor-Leste has developed its environmental policy and regulatory framework in most sectors, since its achievement of independence in 2002, and of establishing national policies relating to environmental management. The country is also developing a National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) that will establish relationships among relevant agencies and with users of weather, water and climate data and information.

The Project will support Timor-Leste in building capacity to provide the essential high-resolution data and climate services required for national adaptation planning and decision-making. The project will also develop and establish sustainable support so that strengthened capacity can be maintained. Data from the monitoring network and vulnerability assessments will allow for better understanding and anticipation of the effects of hydro-meteorological events on communities, including through the development of a flood early warning project (FP109). The data will also support the future formulation of science-based adaptation policies, response plans for planning for adaptation to climate change, disaster risk reduction and public health management.

The integration of climate information services into key policy, decision-making and operational processes of national agencies is essential. This will be achieved by strengthening institutional capacity through establishing institutional policy, planning and regulatory frameworks to optimise coordination between DNMG and other governmental agencies. The National Sector Action and Communication Plans (e.g., the Water Resources Management Plan) are being developed in line with the Climate Change Act (2013). The Meteorology Act is being effectively applied at the national and sub-national level. Local level frameworks (e.g., Community Adaptation Plans, Community Resilience Plans (CRPs) and Early Action Protocols) will support proactive climate-resilient action at the community level.

Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways

Timor-Leste articulated its commitment to pursuing climate-resilient sustainable development in its Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC). Significant international support will be required to increase its adaptive capacity. This Project will support climate-resilient development in Timor-Leste from national level to the "last mile". ## Information on evidence-based planning and decision-making for climate resilience

Building capacity of the National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) in EWS will ensure that coordination and technical support is available to the Government, sectors and communities to effectively prepare for climate-related hazards and disasters. This will enable them to take timely action to reduce disaster risk and vulnerability, thereby minimising the social, economic and environmental costs of climate impacts so that they do not hinder progress towards development goals.

The development and dissemination of targeted and actionable climate information products will be transformational in building the climate resilience of key economic sectors, particularly agriculture, health and water management. The NDMD will develop these products through a suite of outreach, learning and knowledge management activities. Information products will catalyse more efficient and evidence-based approaches and response actions to climate risks and prevent/minimise the social, economic and environmental costs of climate impacts.

Climate information will enable early warning systems to be established in key sectors, not only to reduce risk from climate change, but also to inform immediate reduction of losses of assets and livelihoods caused by climate-related hazards.

Enhanced climate information will enable resilience planning for critical infrastructure impacted by climate change and climate-related hazards, through embedding tailored and actionable climate risk information into their design and operation. The NDMD will work with the relevant sectoral agencies to develop plans to build the capacity of communities to take appropriate preparedness actions and utilise climate information to inform decision-making in key sectors such as agriculture, health and water management.

The Project will also support the development of National Climate Change Action Plans; enhanced community-based early warning systems, particularly to support climate resilience in agriculture, health and water management; and the development of Forecast-based Financing mechanisms for disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation.

As described in Section D.1., The Project will complement the GCF-funded UNDP project FTF09A. A multi-level institutional arrangement has been developed to facilitate this (see Feasibility Study – Section 9). The Project will also build on the work of the NDMD under FTF09A by linking the UNDP-led vulnerability assessments and flood hazard maps with newly established near real-time hydrological and meteorological data. This will enable the development of climate information products, which will be based on the enhanced data availability facilitated by the Project will provide valuable data inputs for several activities implemented under FTF09.

The accuracy of the hydrological and meteorological models developed under FTF09 is dependent upon the quality of the input data. The Project will therefore support the development of outputs, which in turn will improve the inputs (namely the flood hazard maps) available for integration into the Project-developed decision-support system.


## 5.1.2 Project Outputs (maximum: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The Project will create long-term social, environmental and economic benefits as well as support gender-responsive development – as detailed below.

The Project is aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the Paris Agreement, the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction at international and national levels. With respect to the SDGs, the Project will contribute to Timor-Leste’s progress towards disaster risk reduction, ecosystem protection, climate change adaptation, health and sustainable economic development. Through the proposed activities, the Project will also support Timor-Leste in achieving the following Sustainable Development Goals:

- Good Health and Well-being: SDG 3
- Gender Equality: SDG 5
- Reduced Inequalities: SDG 10
- Climate Action: SDG 13

The Project is also fully aligned with Outcome 6 of the UN Sustainable Development Cooperation Framework for Timor-Leste on “Sustainable management of natural resources and resilience to climate change”, which identifies “Strengthening early warning system and capacities” as a specific priority.


---
**Page 77**
---

# Environment co-benefits

The long-term environmental co-benefits of the Project are expected to be significant. The increased availability and use of actionable climate information can enhance natural resource management, from climate change adaptation policies to the sustainable use of arable land.

The Project will support Timor-Leste's national meteorological service (DNMG) to generate and deliver impact-based forecasts, decision-support systems and advisories tailored to natural resource-dependent sectors. This will help reduce the vulnerability of communities in coastal areas to extreme weather events and climate hazards that pose environmental risks and consequently inform the safeguarding of natural resources and biodiversity. Strengthened data sharing and inter-institutional coordination with the Water Sector, including through the use of shared platforms for hydrological monitoring and forecasting and modeling, will contribute to improved water resource management and management of hydrological risks. In turn, this will support sustainable fisheries management and the management of coastal habitats and inform sustainable fisheries practices to minimise environmental impacts.

Air pollutants can have severe impacts on ecosystem function and contribute to biodiversity loss. For example, acid rain caused by air pollution can lead to soil nutrient depletion, which in turn leads to acidification and eutrophication of soil and water bodies respectively, which in turn affect productivity and plant growth. The Project will establish a high-resolution air quality monitoring and alerting framework that will provide early warning on the occurrence of air pollutants and help reduce climate change and air pollution impact through evidence-based mitigation policy and interventions.

With regard to the energy sector, detailed climate and air quality information is necessary for comprehensive environmental assessments of energy projects. This will enable energy project developers to select sites that reduce greenhouse gas emissions.

At the local level, the Project will raise awareness on water, weather and climate hazards and related risks, including floods, droughts, heatwaves and storms, and build preparedness capabilities for effective early action in response to hazard warnings. Moreover, through training on the use of climate forecasts, the Project will support communities to better manage their resources and livelihoods in anticipation of the impacts of climate-related hazards, including on natural resources, ecosystems and biodiversity. This will contribute to enhanced local ownership for environmental protection and resilience building of natural environments.

Moreover, the Project will promote sustainable transport alternatives to international travel when possible. Capacity building that can be undertaken by in-country staff or delivered remotely will be the preferred option. When international travel is necessary, the Project activities will seek to constrain the travel to avoid multiple emissions from air travel. This will include the use of public transportation and low-emission vehicles, when they are available, and plan workshops in central locations to avoid unnecessary travel.

# Social co-benefits including health impacts

The Project is expected to have significant social co-benefits, particularly with regard to positive impacts on health and population well-being. Climate and health are inextricably linked. There is an increasing demand for relevant, timely and usable information about weather and climate variability, change, risks and impacts, as well as the links between these factors and human health. The Project will help reduce adverse factors with air pollution – the world's largest single environmental health risk — and to enhance joint action between the meteorological, climate and health communities to understand and reduce the health risks of

### In the case of the health sector, the Project will create an enabling environment for coordinated and sustained collaboration between climate and health experts and decision-makers to enhance understanding and accelerate action on climate change impacts on health. This will be achieved through a number of actions:

- **Establishing a national Climate and Health Unit**: The Unit will work with partners to co-develop tailored forecasting and a sector-specific decision support system, together with a mobile app to extend the reach of health-related forecasts and advisories to the general public.
- **Continuous engagement with stakeholders**: Regular meetings with key stakeholders will enable identification of health priorities and development of the most appropriate services to address these priorities. This will include engaging with national and local governments, civil society organizations, build-in-country capacity; and align stakeholders' objectives and expectations.

In addition to these Project activities will create employment opportunities for national employees, particularly by hiring local consultants who will be employed by civil society organisations and government institutions. Local businesses will also benefit, as catering services, conference venues and interpreters will be provided by local enterprises. The Project will also contribute to the development of a national disaster risk management system that is fully integrated into the Project interventions, thus ensuring international best practices regarding safety and labour standards, particularly those involving small-scale construction.

The Project will contribute to reduced economic damage and loss in Timor-Leste through enhanced preparedness to extreme events and climate risk-informed decision-making and action. High-resolution baseline weather data will be used to improve early warning and decision-support systems to enhance the climate resilience of sectors and communities. Early warnings can reduce the impact of natural disasters by up to 30% (e.g., in the case of cyclones, the timing of an impending storm or heatwave can reduce the consequent damage by 30 percent). In addition, the Project will establish capacity for Forecast-based Financing (FbF) to enhance preparedness and ensure sustainable development. FbF is a risk management tool that allows governments to pre-finance implementing interventions and has been shown to minimise damage and loss caused by climate-related hazards.

Economic gains are expected from better management of resources—for example, due to the use of impact-based forecasts in agriculture and water resources. Studies in several countries have shown that increased agricultural productivity can be achieved through the use of weather data, with an estimated increase in agricultural production by up to 30%. In the water sector, the use of forecasts to optimise water system supply and demand management has been shown to reduce water stress in agriculture and other water-intensive industries; weather data can improve supply chain efficiencies while reducing emissions and fuel usage. Weather-informed routing has been shown to reduce shipping emissions by up to 4% and reduce fuel consumption by up to 10%. The use of seasonal forecasts for the planning of infrastructure projects (e.g., more strategic risk-informed local development planning) improved decisions for weather events (e.g. a forecast of heavy rainfall in the next week can be used to make informed decisions about when to take decisions to a seasonal forecast) are expected to result in smoothing consumption and lower prices for food. Indirect economic benefits will be derived by the Project’s contribution to food security and self-sufficiency.

It should be noted that the economic benefits for the Project (see Annex 3 – Economic Analysis) are underestimated, both due to analyses having been done following conservative assumptions and because there is a lack of data on the full range of potential impacts. The use of more accurate climate forecasts will reduce income uncertainty and contribute to smoothing consumption and lower prices for food. In addition, the Project will help to reduce exposure from dollar to dollars. In the longer term, climate-sensitive sectors will be facilitated to make better informed investments that will yield long-term economic benefits. Reducing uncertainty is also shown to have direct positive effects on investment decisions in agriculture, water resources, and energy. The Project will include – including decision theory, avoided cost calculations, partial equilibrium models, game theory, contingent

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 76 OF 110

## Gender-responsive development impact

The Project will facilitate gender-responsive development in Timor-Leste through the mainstreaming of gender equality into all aspects of project implementation, as outlined in the Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (Annex 8). This will ensure that gender concerns are addressed and that existing gender inequalities are not reinforced, thus maximizing the outcomes and impact of the Project.

The Project will aim to:

1. Raise gender awareness and increase understanding at national, sectoral and community level of the differential impacts of climate change on women and men.
2. Address the climate vulnerabilities of both women and men through meaningful stakeholder engagement in project planning and implementation, including warning services tailored to the specific needs of end-users.
3. Promote the empowerment of women through equal representation and participation in planning, decision-making, capacity building and skills development.

A summary of the gender-responsive strategies and actions to be incorporated and implemented in the Project is provided in section G.2.

## Impact of COVID-19 on the Global Observing System - a potential co-benefit of the Project

Any lack of observations over one area may impact the quality of forecast and analysis products globally, as the majority of the global observing system is either partly or fully automated, some parts have already been severely affected due to COVID-19. Arctic observations have drastically declined, with an average decrease in coverage from 80% to below 50%, and in some areas even down to 30% in the tropics and Southern Hemisphere, where there are already fewer observations available. The availability of observations over the oceans has also been severely impacted by COVID-19. A suspected cause is reliance on manned observing stations subject to lockdown. For ocean observations, the main impact has been on voluntary ship observations, which are down around 80% compared to the pre-pandemic period.

The Project will benefit from its support of the Global Observing System (GOS) and the Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFFI) – namely, the transition to automated observing systems. The Project will also contribute to the development of a global framework for sustainable climate services (Sub-Activity 1.1.5). The Project will engage with SOFFI to ensure that Timor-Leste can benefit from its support to sustain its systematic basic observations network as a global co-benefit.

## Impact of COVID-19 on lives, livelihoods and assets - a potential co-benefit of implementing Forecast-based Financing (FbF) and integrated disaster risk management

The vulnerability of Timor-Leste is evident from the devastating impacts of the recent tropical cyclone, heavy rains, flooding and landslides that occurred between 25th March and April 2021, and which affected over


---
**Page 81**
---

30,000 households and caused 48 fatalities. In addition to causing considerable human and economic loss, the flooding and landslides significantly undermined COVID-19 response efforts as Timor-Leste struggled to contain the virus' second wave. Heavy flooding in the National Laboratory and medical storage facilities forced the evacuation of staff and equipment. The floods also displaced 20,000 people who were already evacuated. Moreover, displaced people sheltered in improvised evacuation centres and in the homes of relatives and friends were at increased risk from COVID-19 transmission. The lessons learned from these hazard-induced disasters and the COVID-19 pandemic provide demonstrable evidence of the potential for a resilient disaster risk management system to reduce the impact of future shocks and stressors, including those in 2020, following the devastation of large areas of Vatucu by Tropical Cyclone Hato. In addition, the Brief emphasises "the need for countries to focus on a multi-hazard integrated disaster risk management approach that is inclusive of climate change and COVID-19 risks" (see section 3.4: "The Multi-Hazard Sector"). The dual challenge of climate change and a global pandemic highlights the relevance of the principles outlined in the Brief, which are equally applicable to other shocks and stressors, including disaster efforts and enhancement of multi-hazard early warning systems. Furthermore, when Forecast-based Financing is combined with early response to a climate shock and resilient or disaster risk reduction actions, it can be an effective tool for reducing vulnerability.

A 2018 return on investment study in Nepal on implementing the approach, found that USD $22 million can be saved when responding to an emerging of any size (175,000 affected people). Over 20 years, this would save 4.3 Gt of CO2 emissions, or saved per dollar invested, after deducting the investment cost.

A 2018 USAID study on Ethiopia, Kenya and Somalia indicates that early response to drought, combined with a forecast-based system, could over a 15-year period save USD $4.3 billion, or an average of USD $287 million per year.

A 2015 Cost-Benefit Analysis carried out in Sudan and Nigeria shows that investing in a forecast-based system can be cost-effective compared to other approaches. For example, the study found that the cost of a humanitarian response is five to seven times higher than multi-year resilience-building.

The above studies exemplify the economic co-benefits of implementing the Forecast-based Financing / Early Warning System (FBF/EWS) in disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation projects.

Timor-Leste gained independence in 2002. While elections have been held regularly and peacefully since independence, there are still significant challenges to democracy in Timor-Leste. Many aspects of Timor-Leste's development remain a challenge. Poverty levels remain high, with an estimated 42% of the population living in poverty in 2014, and the 2017 Human Capital Index standing at 0.43 (compared to the regional average of 0.61). ## Climate change in Timor-Leste

Timor-Leste is vulnerable to floods and landslides, earthquakes, tsunamis, and tropical cyclones, and it is an archipelagic country. The country has a mountainous topography, the isolation of many communities, the lack of infrastructure, and poor access to services.

The vulnerability of Timor-Leste is evident from the devastating impacts of the recent tropical cyclone, heavy rains, flooding, and landslides that occurred between 29th March and April 2021. The impact was felt across the country, with many communities isolated for several days as roads were cut off or washed away.

## D

**have small farms, have the lowest literacy rates and are more engaged in agricultural activity. To achieve fair and inclusive development, additional focus is required to support succes with lower living standards.** The Project includes specific interventions to develop local community-based early warning systems (EWs) for climate change impacts at the local level. These EWs will provide communities with preparedness measures to climate hazards. Around 70% of the population of Timor-Leste rely on climate-sensitive livelihoods and are therefore vulnerable to climate change. The Project aims to build the capacities of farmers to utilise climate information, including from crop advisors, sector-specific decision support systems (uCCS) and agriculture extension services.

### Water security

Water security is a key issue in relation to climate change due to existing inequalities and multi-dimensional social factors that influence their adaptive capacities, resilience and participation in climate action. Timor-Leste is classified as a Least Developed Country (LDC), Timor-Leste is also one of the countries with the lowest per capita water availability and highest aridity index. The Project area will and continue to adopt a gender mainstreaming approach throughout its development and implementation. This includes, but not limited to, ensuring that women are included in all aspects of the project, including training to DMAG staff; designated leadership roles for women's groups in early warning protocols; and ensuring that women have access to water resources.

Further details are provided in Section G.2. and in the Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex 8).

### D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Government of Timor-Leste has highlighted the critical importance of services relating to climate change, early warning systems for agriculture and country programme implementation. The project will be policy driven. These are outlined in the list below and detailed in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

Dialogue on this Project began in 2015 when Timor-Leste expressed interest in the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS). The GFCS is a multi-country programme designed to enhance capacity for operational climate services. A Concept Note was approved by GCF in 2015 for five countries—four Pacific island countries and Timor-Leste. WMO undertook an intensive consultation process with the Government of Timor-Leste, including a series of workshops and meetings, culminating in establishing EWSs that would reach people at the last mile with accurate, timely, actionable warnings and services. The GFCS project was formally endorsed by the Government of Timor-Leste in 2016. Further consultation with potential users and stakeholders in Timor-Leste, as reported in detail to the Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7). Timor-Leste's National Designated Authority (NDA) for implementing the GFCS project is the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR). Work has been undertaken further consultation with potential users and stakeholders in Timor-Leste, as reported in detail to the Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7). Timor-Leste's National Designated Authority (NDA) for implementing the GFCS project is the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR). Work has been undertaken further consultation with potential users and stakeholders in Timor-Leste, as reported in detail to the Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7). Timor-Leste's National Designated Authority (NDA) for implementing the GFCS project is the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR).

The Secretary of State for the Environment (SES) in Timor-Leste will serve as the lead executing Entity (LE) for this Project, and will therefore be responsible for establishing national project implementation. This modality will enhance country ownership of the Project and build-in-country capacity to lead on future climate resilience actions and initiatives.

The Project is being developed by leveraging the technical expertise of international partners, who have been engaged at the request of the country based on their track record and experience in Timor-Leste. In order to retain capacity in Timor-Leste beyond the Project implementation period, it should be noted that Timor-Leste is a Member State and representative of the Regional Integrated Multi-Hazard Early Warning System (RIMES).

Alignment with existing national policies addressing climate change

The proposed Project will contribute to the achievement of priorities articulated by the Government of Timor-Leste in the following documents:

- **Green Climate Fund Country Programme (2019)** - The Project will provide essential information for evidence-based decision making and planning for the national priorities outlined in the Country Programme, enhancing their efficacy and preventing maladaptation. The Country Programme also explicitly refers to RIMES as a key partner.
  
- **National Disaster Risk Management Policy (2006)** - The Project will directly address two key priorities of the Policy's objective relating to the prevention of natural disasters: i) Create early warning systems, ii) Develop and implement disaster risk reduction strategies. The Project will also contribute mechanisms to respond to natural disasters.

- **Timor-Leste National Adaptation and Program Action (NAPA) on Climate Change (2016)** - This NAPA identifies the need for climate change adaptation, increased monitoring and risk forecasting, and support for the adaptation of government policies and strategies to improve climate change resilience among vulnerable communities. The Project will contribute directly with communications or through mainstreaming climate services and knowledge into the work of other government agencies.

- **The Environment Law (2012)** - directs that "land-use development and planning of inland areas shall consider the need for integrated management of water resources bearing in mind the potential impacts these may have on coastal areas."

- The Timor-Leste National Communication (TCN) to the UNFCCC (2014) identifies some key adaptation actions relevant to Early Warning Systems: i) Strengthening capacity of national and local institutions as well as communities in managing climate risks through the development of an effective early warning system; ii) Development of a national disaster risk management information support tools system for policy makers; and iii) Research and development of technologies more appropriate to the country's needs. The Project will contribute directly with the achievement of the first action and will generate data and information needed for effective implementation of the second.

- **Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDC) Timor-Leste (2016)** - The Project will directly address the priority measures for adaptation to natural disasters identified in the INDC: i) Improve institutional and staff capacity in the disaster sector in relation to climate change induced disasters; ii) Strengthen early warning systems, including those related to tropical cyclones, floods and storms; and iii) Integrate climate risk information into traditional disaster risk reduction and management strategies. The Project will contribute directly with the achievement of each measure identified in the INDC, in particular relating to the promotion of "evidence-based decision making", taking into consideration of climate change and its adverse effects on diseases, particularly waterborne diseases.

- **Timor-Leste Second National Communication (SNC) to the UNFCCC (2016)** - The Project will directly address the following adaptation needs identified in the SNC: i) Increase the number of weather stations; ii) Improve early warning systems and iii) Education and public awareness. In addition, it will contribute to an enabling environment for integrating consideration of climate change to develop

## Timor-Leste's National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (2021-2030)
As noted above, the Project is identified as a priority in the NAP and the Timor-Leste National Adaptation Strategy (NLNAS). In addition to contributing to the achievement of the following priorities and outcomes: * Strengthening the capacity of national, local institutions and communities in managing climate risks; * Reducing the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts through increased resilience at all levels; * Enhancing institutional and community capacity to prepare for and respond to climate-induced natural disasters; * Improving disaster risk management systems and practices (including early warning); * Enhancing government and community strategies to respond to drought exacerbated by climate change; * Establishing surveillance for heat early warning systems and response mechanisms.

## UN Sustainable Development Cooperation Framework Timor-Leste (2021 - 2035)
The Project is fully aligned with Outcome 6 on "Sustainable management of natural resources and resilience to climate change", which identifies "Strengthening early warning system capability" as a specific priority. The Project has key agents, including the implementation of the Climate Trial disaster risk reduction and the National Disaster Risk Management Council as part of the proposed Project. This includes the National Directorate for Meteorology and Geophysics, the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries, the Ministry of Social Solidarity, the National Directorate for Climate Change, the Directorate General for Environment, the Directorate General for Habitat, Ornament and Environment, and the Ministry of Development and Institutional Reforms.

The Project is aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the Paris Agreement and the Sendai Framework.

* **SDG 13 advocates urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts. Target 13.1 advises countries to "Strengthen resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in all countries".**
* **SDG 3 advocates to ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages. Target 3.d aims to "Strengthen capacity of all countries, in particular developing countries, for early warning, risk reduction and management of natural disasters and other impacts of climate change".**
* **The Paris Agreement in Article 7, Sub-paragraph (7)c) calls for "strengthening scientific knowledge on climate, including research, systematic observation of the climate system and early warning systems, as well as the dissemination of information and awareness-raising activities".**

The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030[29] paragraph 3.3(b) stresses that it is important "To invest in, develop, maintain and strengthen people-centred multi-hazard, multisectoral early warning systems, including the development of appropriate communication mechanisms, social technologies and hazard-monitoring telecommunications systems; develop such systems through a participatory process; tailor them to the needs of users, including social and cultural groups that are particularly vulnerable to disasters; ensure their accessibility for all; deploy early warning equipment and tools; and broaden release channels for natural disaster early warning information". The Sendai Framework's Seven Global Targets calls for efforts to "substantially reduce disaster mortality and substantially decrease the number of people affected by disasters".

## Engagement with civil society organisations

The Project will support an existing group of Government ministries and UN agencies responsible for coordinating disaster risk management, early warning coordination, and disaster risk reduction, expanding the group to include NGOs/CBOs and enabling the group to convey its message. The following agencies will be invited to establish a national focal point for climate services: National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA) and IOM), international NGOs (including Mercy Corps, CARE, Oxfam, PLAN, World Vision, Caritas, and others), and other relevant organisations (CIVILIS*, NHFDC**, ASHRED***, FENAMET**** and others). Existing or ad-hoc early warning related work at the community level will be leveraged to support coordination and integration into a broader national system.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 10 points)

The proposed Project interventions will require requesting a GCF grant to enable the efficient and effective delivery of the proposed Project interventions to achieve the stated impact. The requested grant is considered the most appropriate financial instrument to enhance climate services, disaster risk reduction and MHEWS due to the established linkages with the NDMA and IOM, as well as the ability to address immediate needs.

The investment will directly address financial, technical, capacity and communication gaps in the delivery of climate services, disaster risk reduction and MHEWS. The comprehensive approach will ensure investment in all elements of the value chain for climate services. Although there are no direct commercial products or services, the Project will develop a suite of decision-making information products with commercial value that will enable cost recovery options and private sector engagement.

The Cost-Benefit Analysis (CBA) has identified and assessed the costs and benefits of the proposed interventions and has made inherent trade-offs explicit. The feasibility of the investments was determined by carrying out a CBA, which shows that the Project is financially viable with an internal rate of return greater than 10%. The discount rate has been applied. The CBA shows that, assuming a 10-year useful life of proposed interventions at a 10% discount rate, discounted NPV is positive (US $36.01 million). The economic internal rate of return (EIRR) is 104.2%, which is greater than the discount rate.

Sensitivity analysis has been used to test key parameters such as a decrease in benefits by 10% or an increase in costs by 10%. Although the EIRR decreased with these simulated cost benefit changes, the EIRR remained positive at 98.5% and 74%, respectively. The CBA also tested a more pessimistic scenario combining a decrease in benefits by 10% and an increase in costs by 10% still resulted in a positive NPV.

There is uncertainty in the findings of the CBA, with the total net benefits ranging from USD 63.76 million and USD 40.99 million, depending on various assumptions and parameters used. Nevertheless, the sensitivity analysis shows that the Project remains financially viable under all scenarios tested. The Project has a positive economic NPV and an EIRR greater than the discount rate, which are the criteria to determine the efficiency of the Project. However, the net benefits are likely to be underestimated due to conservative assumptions used in calculations as well as the omission of qualitative benefits excluded from the CBA.

Most importantly, effective early warning systems save lives, and this is arguably impossible to translate to dollar terms. As a result, the CBA (Annex 3) provides a positive quantitative justification for the Project, which is essential in order to secure funding under the GCF.

The benefits calculated in the CBA take the form of avoided economic damages and losses. Without the Project, these will increase as climate-related disasters become more frequent or intensity of climate-related disasters, such as flooding, drought and storm surge, will be exacerbated, particularly when considering the negative impacts on infrastructure and physical assets, particularly contents, and crops. Effective climate services and impact-based MHEWS developed by the Project have the potential to radically reduce the loss of contents and crop losses associated with these events. Other benefits include reduced costs for evacuation, as people will be able to evacuate more move content and harvest crops, are key contributors to these avoided damages. Avoided economic damages and losses are calculated in terms of US dollars.

The Project aims to increase sectoral and community resilience to climate-related hazards and extreme climatic events, resulting in improved productivity and avoided losses to agriculture, fisheries and other relevant sectors.

Considering the baseline low capacity to provide climate information and early warning services (CIEWS) in Timor-Leste, the Project will build on existing networks and strengths in Timor-Leste, such as the establishment of a National Framework for Climate Services and development of a financial framework and business case for climate services. The Project will also contribute to the national strategy for disaster risk management beyond the term of the Project. Climate services investments return benefits that are far greater than the losses they will help prevent, with an overall cost-benefit ratio of one to ten. Moreover, investment in climate services is a highly efficient use of public funds, as it provides a 10% return on investment and a return on investment of as much as 80 to one.

The implementation of an integrated value-chain approach to climate services will be critical for the success of the Project. The Project will also contribute to disaster preparedness and Forecast-based Financing (FbF) / Early Warning Action (EWA). The inclusion of Result 4, which focuses on enhancing risk management and preparedness capacities, is in line with the GCF’s Strategic Objective 2: “To support countries in building their capacity for climate change adaptation.”

The Project will leverage the specialised knowledge of regional and international technical partners to ensure that the most effective technologies and best practices for climate information services and disaster risk management are adopted. The Project will also contribute to the achievement of six Results with transformative impact beyond the term of the Project, including: i) Innovative and cost-efficient technologies for climate information services; ii) Development of a National Framework for Climate Services and disaster climate services and disaster risk management; iii) Operations and Management (O&M); iv) Enhancing institutional effectiveness through Quality Management Systems (QMS) and related certification; v) Ensuring long-term financial sustainability, including a funding mechanism for forecast-based early action; vi) Use of traditional knowledge in the development and coordination of the Project major output disaster early warning systems. The Project


---
**Page 88**
---

will also ensure that all infrastructure, systems and processes are compliant with the WMO Global Basic Observing Network (GBCON) and internationally recognised standards, as relevant.

Effectiveness of the Project will be further enhanced by its direct contribution to key national priorities and strategies, including the National Disaster Risk Management Policy (NDRMP) 2008-2013. Elevated Disaster Risk Management (DRM) to national priority generated political commitment, made the policy a multi-sector responsibility, and allocated the necessary resources for implementation. The Ministry of Social Solidarity (MoSS), acts as a lead organisation for DRM in the country and coordinates with all line-ministries, District Disaster Risk Management Committees, and other stakeholders.

Participatory implementation will enhance ownership through proper engagement of all relevant stakeholders, which will lead to efficiency and sustainability. Effectiveness is expected to increase as factors influencing project success become less of an obstacle - for example, access to credit, and lack of skills to interpret information received.


Further details can be found in the Economic Analysis (Annex 3).

### E.6 Activities

#### 1.1 – Establish international and policy frameworks, legislation and delivery mechanisms for climate services

The activity will establish comprehensive national and policy frameworks, legislation and delivery models for climate services in Timor-Leste, including the National Framework for Climate Services Act and Meteorological Strategy Act.

A National Climate Outlook Forum (NCOF) will be established to facilitate dialogue between key stakeholders and end-users of the Climate Services products and services.


---
**Page 97**
---

The NFCS will be supported by a systematic five-year process of integrating climate considerations into the decision-making and planning processes for public and community sectors through development of a National Climate Sector Action and Communication Plan (NSACP) and sector-specific training - including disaster risk reduction and disaster risk reduction.

The project will support Timor-Leste to manage and use information systems in conjunction with data from other regional sources, taking climate considerations as well as social and economic sector sources with climate data for national planning and national community on implementation of applicable provisions of the Paris Agreement and the UNFCCC in general.

Furthermore, a financial framework and financing model for sustainable climate services will be developed. This will enable justification of the value of climate information and funding for disaster risk management and contribute to the identification of long-term sources of funds.

| Strategy and Action Plan for 2016-2020 | Statistics Directorate – including climate change processes and systems integration to ensure security, integrity, reliability and technology migration |
| Climate System Data System (CSDS) providing an integrated platform for climate and scientific data analysis, visualization, with linkage to national decision-making tools |
| Technical assistance training on climate data management, inter- and intra-sectoral coordination, climate data collection, storage and backup |
| Assurance and compliance with GMDM QMS standards |
| Data quality management enhanced in the areas of spatial-time land, ocean and atmospheric variables of Essential Climate Variables (ECVs) exchanged freely for use in Regional Climate Centres (RCCs) at least on a twice-monthly basis |
| National Climate Sector Action and Communication Plan |
| Sector Specific Climate Training developed |
| Technical Officers (TO) workshop for 5 TOs |
| Sector specific training for five GPCS priority sectors |
| Comprehensive and tailored financial mechanisms to generate services in Timor-Leste based on value chain approach |

## E

### Strengthen climate modeling and improved forecasting

This Activity will establish a national Forecasting Centre in Timor-Leste, which will provide the necessary infrastructure for climate monitoring, management and forecasting that are currently missing. The aim is to build capacity of DMMS's capacity to deliver multi-hazard weather forecasts and services. A National Fire Management System (NFMS) will be established to facilitate fire risk assessment and management.

## 2.3 – Establish climate services for health

This Activity will address the importance of transparent and timely, usable information about climate variability, change, risks and impacts on human health to enable people to make appropriate actions that protect their health. This will include institutional strengthening through establishment of a National Climate and Health Working Group; establishment of national climate and health monitoring systems; development of a mobile application; capacity building for the delivery of tailored forecasting services and early warning systems, including a mobile application.

#### 2.3.2.2 Training on building out on quality monitoring systems

## E

This Activity will build the capacity of Timor-Leste to prepare for and respond to the impacts of climate change risks and health impacts. The Project will work with the Civil Protection Unit (CPL) - Timor- Leste, the National Directorate for Climate Change (DNCC), and the National Directorate for Disaster Management (NDM) to enhance disaster preparedness through capacity building at national level for early warning systems (EWS) and community-level for disaster risk management and co-ordination.

In addition, The Project will conduct a targeted disaster risk awareness and education campaign for women, which will focus on empowering women with the understanding and knowledge for improved disaster risk management and decision-making for climate resilience.

### 4.1 - Build capacity to prepare for and respond to risks and hazards

#### 4.1.1 Enhance disaster preparedness at national level
- National early warning systems (EWS)
- Community-based Early Warning Systems (CBiEWS)

#### 4.1.2 Build capacity of the Disaster Risk Management Committee (DRMC) for EWS
- Capacity building for DRMCs to enhance community-level disaster preparedness and response

#### 4.1.3 Conduct a targeted disaster risk awareness and education campaign for women
- Training and workshops on early warning systems, contingency plans, and disaster preparedness


---
**Page 103**
---

The National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) will continue providing disaster risk management information and technical support to the Government of the Republic of Timor-Leste, including responsibility for disaster preparedness and early warning systems. This will build the technological and institutional capacity of NDMD to utilise EWS information for predicting and managing functions in disaster preparedness.

* International Day for Disaster Risk Reduction (IDDRR) event and awareness campaign conducted annually
* Public awareness and education campaign focused on reducing hazards, related risks, and vulnerability; disaster risk reduction and early warning management actions conducted; community radio broadcast of information
* National Education and Education Commission (NEEC): national health-education campaigns; disaster education support; community-based disaster activities on health and safety; vulnerability assessment

4.2 Establish Forecast-Based Financing (FDF)

This article will introduce Forecasted Financing (FF) in Timor-Leste, whereby early actions are taken based on in-depth forecast and risk assessments that can be automatically allocated when a predefined threshold is reached.

* Mechanism for FF mechanisms with an emphasis on the development of early warning systems to improve disaster preparedness
* Financial mechanism for immediate post-disaster response by NDMD within 24 hours

The Project will establish a Roadmap for Timor-Leste, including the following components: (i) Risk Identification; (ii) Risk

E7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 508 words, approximately 1 page)

The Project will engage a Monitoring and Evaluation (MME) Advisor to design a performance monitoring and evaluation framework for the Project. The MME Advisor will be responsible for ensuring general responsiveness of Project implementation. Working closely with the Project Manager in the PMU under the oversight of the UNEP Task Manager, the MME Advisor will be responsible for continuously monitoring progress against the Project’s objectives and indicators. The overall performance of the Project will be achieved by (i) measuring the indicators to assess the progress of the Project; (ii) reporting the Project's performance on a regular basis through quarterly financial reports and annual performance audits; and (iii) conducting mid-term and end-of-project evaluations.

The MME Advisor will work closely with the PMU to develop a performance monitoring framework. The mid-performance, mid-point and end of Project (PMI) will coordinate evidence-gathering exercises to verify this progress. Mid-term evaluation results will be triangulated with baseline surveys that will be conducted in the Project’s first year. In addition to the indicators and data collected by the MME Advisor team, activities will be monitored by the EEs.

The MME Advisor will organize training for staff members of the EEs and Technical Partner in data collection methods, including the Project cycle, parallel monitoring, and reporting of activities. All training should take a strengths-based approach, both in the training process and in the principles and practices of monitoring and evaluation. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that all monitoring activities are collecting meaningful information and that the information is able to be used both for project management purposes as well as for reporting to UNEP and GCF.

The MME Advisor will prepare progress reports and quarterly financial statements to the PMU and the PMU will consolidate the reports and submit them to UNEP as the AE. In turn, UNEP will submit annual performance reports and semi-annual financial reports to GCF. The detailed reporting timelines are as follows:

Under the PCC, each EE is to report to UNEP as follows:
a. Progress reports: by 29 July for January to June;
b. Annual Performance Reports: by 1 February;
c. Quarterly financial reports by 15 January, 15 April, 15 July, and 15 October;
d. Annual audited statements by 30 April;

e. Final APR within six months of Project completion.

UNEP (AE) reports to the GCF:
a. Annual Performance Reports by 1 March;
b. Semi-annual financial reports: by 29 March and 30 September;
c. Mid-Term Evaluation report: halfway through Project;
d. Final APR: within 6 months of Project completion.
e. Terminal Evaluation report: within 12 months of Project completion.


---
**Page 105**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 181 OF 116

Monitoring will also be undertaken by the AE through supervision visits and field missions to track implementation progress and challenges and strategically plan the way forward. The Project reporting relationships, including frequency of reporting, between AE (UNEP) and EEs and other partners in the Project will be reviewed at the Mid-Term Evaluation (MTE). The MTE will also review the Project’s performance against the agreed indicators and targets set out in the Project Document and will lead to the Terminal Evaluation (TE). The Task Manager will oversee the process of hiring an external consultant to carry out the independent evaluation. The AE will be responsible for finalizing the draft TE report by mid-point. This will be a formative exercise and will cover whether the Project is on track, what problems and challenges are being faced by Project partners, and what corrective actions need to be taken so that the Project achieves its intended outcomes by Project completion in the most efficient and sustainable way. The Project Steering Committee (PSC) and the EEs will participate in the Mid-Term Evaluation process and contribute to the evaluation report. The PSC will review the draft TE report at mid-point and provide feedback which will monitor the implementation of agreed recommendations during the remainder of the Project’s operational life.

The independent Terminal Evaluation (TE) will be carried out by an external consultant appointed by the AE, with input from the Task Manager throughout the process. An independent assessment of Project performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, likelihood of impact and sustainability) will be made at TE. The independent evaluation consultant will provide a draft report to the AE which evaluation criterion will be rated using a six-point rating scheme and a weighted average will be determined to determine the overall rating for each criterion. The AE will review the draft report and provide ratings between the independent evaluation consultant and the Evaluation Office; a final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office when the Terminal Evaluation report is finalized. The draft TE report will be shared with the PSC, EEs, and other partners in the Project at mid-point for their comments. Final comments on the report will be shared by the Evaluation Office in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a recommendation compliance process.

The costs for results monitoring and performance evaluation are included in the Project budget.

## F.1 Risk Assessment and mitigation measures (see pages [3-4])

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) risks and the proposed risk mitigation measures to ensure that project objectives are achieved.

For probability: High has high possibility; Medium has moderate possibility; Low has negligible possibility

For impact: High has high impact; Medium has medium impact; Low has low impact

Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistleblowers or witnesses, as well as fraudulent, coercive, and abusive behavior.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Political instability that limits coordination and commitment between key partners and stakeholders

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The Project will ensure that interventions are relevant to and aligned with existing national and sectoral priorities, which will increase stakeholder buy-in and support to deliver on the outcomes. The inception workshop will further define stakeholder roles and responsibilities in the project implementation process. The Project will also ensure that the roles, responsibilities and capacities of national and local organisations. Throughout its implementation, the Project will employ a phased approach to ensure alignment with the National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) and the User Interface Platform – to ensure:

- Understanding and buy-in of the Project at the highest government level and sustained involvement of Finance Ministers, Vice-Presidents, and other senior officials.
- The establishment of the National Directorates for Climate Change (NDCC), the National Disaster Management Directorate (NMD), the National Directorate for Water Resources Development (NDWRD) and the National Meteorological Agency (NMA).
- Phases: and

- Relevant partners (Government ministries, agencies and technical partners) are clearly identified, are committed to delivering the Project outcomes, and are aware of their contribution to the Project.

Furthermore, The Project will support the establishment of an active and engaged Project Steering Committee (PSC) to provide high-level oversight for the project. The PSC will be responsible for ensuring that the project is on track by ensuring full coordination of regional and national stakeholders and addressing any issues or conflicts that arise. Should potential risks materialize, the PSC will ensure that appropriate mitigation measures are put in place.

- Ensure regular communication with relevant entities to anticipate and address potential impacts to Project delivery.
- If necessary, an emergency management / contingency plan will be developed and communicated to ensure full communication of Project risks and opportunities for its continuation

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 103 OF 116

The National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to be established in Timor-Leste will facilitate regular communication between DMAG and the Government agencies responsible for climate-sensitive sectors – including the five GFCS priority sectors of agriculture and food security, disaster risk reduction, energy, health and water – with decision-makers at all levels. The establishment of a national climate service will also provide a platform to mainstream basic climate science within the government. This will facilitate regular dialogue between DMAG and end-users of climate information to help inform DMAG better understand their needs for climate services and how best to meet them, including through the provision of early warnings.

The technical working group for EWS will provide an additional means of interaction between DMAG and end-users of climate information. The project will also include a biannual stakeholder engagement workshop with communication feedback mechanisms. In addition, biannual ocean data stakeholder engagement workshops will be conducted in collaboration with the Data Sharing and Advisory System (DSAS) to be developed by RMES. It will include a user feedback mechanism to validate forecasts.

The Project will also build capacity to conduct vulnerability and risk assessments, which will facilitate the dissemination of climate information to vulnerable groups. The project will support the development of a Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (Annex 8) details specific project interventions targeted to the needs of vulnerable groups – in particular, women, children, older people and people with disability. Efforts have been informed by comprehensive assessment of gender issues in Timor-Leste.

The Project will support DMAG to work more closely with, in particular, Timor-Leste Red Cross Society (CVTL) and MAF agencies. The project will also provide training for staff at DMAG to feed back directly into the climate services system as well as end-users have been extensively consulted during the Project design process and their contributions have very much informed the project’s approach. The project will also support the development of a Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (Annex 8) details specific project interventions targeted to the needs of vulnerable groups – in particular, women, children, older people and people with disability.

Description

Sectors and communities unwilling to instigate organisational or behavioural change

Mitigation Measure(s)

The paradigm shift to evidence-based planning and preparedness action is a key measure of Project success from national to local level. This will be achieved by focusing on empowering sectors and communities to increase their climate resilience - introduction of seasonal forecasting and its application to climate-sensitive sectors, including the development of new seasonal forecasting methods, Forecast-based Financing / Early Warning Early Action mechanisms and co-development of Early Action Protocols, and improved dissemination and communication of climate information and early warnings, with emphasis on vulnerable groups.

Organisational and behavioural change is very difficult to achieve and will progress incrementally over the Project lifetime. The PMU will ensure that the project team continues to work in a manner consistent with UNEP’s approach to sustainability beyond its five-year term will help to ensure that trust in scientific forecasting will continue, with benefits to lives, livelihoods and assets saved.

# Mitigation Measure(s)

The Project includes a dedicated intervention to develop a financial framework for sustainable climate services to ensure that DNMG has the means to sustain its operations beyond the Project lifespan. This will be supported by the development of a business model for climate services delivery, which will identify options for value-added products and opportunities.

In addition, the Project will consult and work with the Government of Timor-Leste and Technical Partners during its inception phase to ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities, including the identification of key institutional resources; and build capacity in agencies that will continue to receive Government support beyond the Project duration. Within this context, as part of the Project, we will identify a country-driven, scalable financial mechanism for forecast-based early action to ensure the availability of reliable and sustainable funding for Forecast-based Financing, which will be introduced under Activity 4.2.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Screening identifies the risk category of this Project. The scope and depth of the environmental and social assessment is proportional to the level of risks and impacts described in the ESS Screening. For this Project, the following categories have been identified: Most activities that have been identified are expected to have significant environmental or social consequences. The majority of the Project activities will be subject to a full Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA). However, there are some activities that will require low-level monitoring (see Safeguard Standards 1 and 4 below), the environmental and social impacts should be minimal. This Project can therefore be rated as Category C, as per the GCF’s Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) and the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) will not be required. As well, the initial screening performed by UNEP has confirmed that this Project is unlikely to have significant environmental or social impacts.

In the prospect a providing through overview of the potential environmental and social impacts each of the 7 UNDP Safeguards are addressed:

#### Safeguard Standard 1: Biodiversity conservation, natural habitats, and sustainable management of living resources

- The Project will likely have minimal to no impacts on biodiversity conservation, natural habitats, and sustainable management of living resources. Specific activities that have been flagged for monitoring in the implementation phase related are:
    - 2.1.1 Establish a meteorological observation network to GBOON standards
    - 2.1.2 Establish a national Forecasting centre (in an existing government building)

#### Safeguard Standard 2: Resource efficiency, pollution prevention and management of chemicals and wastes

- This Project will have minimal impact safeguard Standard 2; however, international travel should be minimised, where practicable, in order to limit GHG emissions, and national travel should use low-emission vehicles when possible.

#### Safeguard Standard 3: Safety of dams

- Dams are not being implemented as part of the Project’s activities.

#### Safeguard Standard 4: Involuntary resettlement

- Immobilisation of land is envisioned as a consequence of Project activities. The Government of Timor-Leste (through the Director of DNMG) has provided a formal letter indicating that all equipment and infrastructure will be removed and maintained on government-owned land. These include but are not limited to Sub-activities:
    - 2.1.1 Establish a meteorological observation network to GBOON standards
    - 2.1.2 Establish a national Forecasting centre (in an existing government building)

#### Safeguard Standard 5: Indigenous peoples

- In accordance with the analysis of international development banks and other approved GCF projects (e.g., the Timor-Leste Development Fund), development projects, essentially everywhere in Timor-Leste is considered indigenous, and thus no specific analyses related to indigenous peoples is required.

#### Safeguard Standard 6: Labour and working conditions

- Manual labour contracts are not envisioned as part of this contract. Contracts for national and international consultants will be executed in accordance with Timor-Leste’s Labour Code, Law 4/2012.

---

Safeguard Standard 7: Protection of tangible cultural heritage

* Tangible cultural heritage will likely not be affected by Project activities.

In addition, an Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) is provided in Annex 6.

GCF's Environmental and Social Policy states that Projects must provide a 'grievance redress mechanism to receive complaints and feedback.' Therefore, the Project has created a Stakeholder Response Mechanism (SRM). The SRM provides Stakeholders with a mechanism that ensures that Grievances filed in relation to the Project are addressed thoroughly and systematically. Annex 6 sets forth the procedures for the SRM in detail. These procedures consist of:

* Who can Submit a Complaint;
* How a Complaint is Submitted;
* What information should be included in a Complaint;
* Information related to Logging a Complaint, Acknowledgment, and Tracking;
* Information related to Maintaining Communication with Project-affected Communities and Stakeholders;
* The SRM's mandate to build consensus and propose a resolution;
* The right of Project-affected Communities to seek alternatives to the SRM.

As for the foregoing analysis, this Project has been assessed as Category C: Projects with activities with minimal environmental or social impacts. However, in accordance with GCF's Environmental and Social Policy, the following procedures are required with respect to preparing an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment or Environmental and Social Management Plan:

G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

This Funding Proposal includes a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex 8) that has been informed, analyzed and prepared according to the following methodology:

* Stakeholder engagement specifically directed at gender assessment and analysis. This included participation of women from an in-country stakeholder consultation workshop, interviews and questionnaire responses.
* Comprehensive desk review of existing literature; draft Gender Assessment Report prepared by WHA Consulting (2018); UNDP Country Programme Document (2017); country-specific reports from international organisations (United Nations Development Programme – UNDP, United States Agency for International Development – USAID, The Asia Foundation, etc.); multilateral development banks (World Bank, Asian Development Bank, Inter-American Development Bank), Risk Management, Geography Compass; Women's Studies International Forum, etc.) and grey literature.
* The stakeholder consultations, literature review, gender analysis of the Feasibility Study and analysis of the draft Gender Assessment Report provided useful data and information to inform the socioeconomic and gender-based political, legal and institutional environment in relation to gender priorities, and gender-specific vulnerabilities and adaptation needs in Timor-Leste.
