# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 108 OF 116

## Information detailed above, gender-responsive recommendation and interventions relevant to the Project are provided.

### The Gender Action Plan outlines specific indicators and targets for each Activity to ensure that gender mainstreaming is implemented, monitored and evaluated throughout the term of the Project.
This will be achieved by ensuring that the Project is fully aligned with international and national gender priorities in Timor-Leste, as detailed in annex 8.

#### Gender mainstreaming throughout the Project is essential to ensure that gender concerns are addressed, existing gender inequalities are reduced, and new gender inequalities are not created. This is particularly important for the entire population of Timor-Leste. Therefore, the Project will engage a dedicated full-time Gender Expert.
Full details of how the Project will deliver against the above objectives are provided in the annexed Gender Action Plan. The Gender Action Plan outlines the gender mainstreaming strategies that will be incorporated and implemented in the Project.

#### All staff engaged in the Project—including funding Entities, PMU, PSC and Technical Partner agencies—will receive training on gender equality and gender policies (including the Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan, 2011 UNEP Gender Policy[1] and GCF Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan[2]) as well as UNDP’s Gender Equality Policy principles and support gender mainstreaming throughout the Project. Where possible, training on gender equality will be provided to promote gender awareness and gender-responsive action.

#### Frameworks, strategies, plans and partner agreements will recognize and require gender mainstreaming in all aspects of project implementation, including through the application of UNEP’s Gender Equality Policy and the UNDP and GCF Gender Policies.

#### All stakeholder consultations, outreach forums and training processes to be undertaken as part of the Project will adopt a participatory approach. The Project will ensure equal representation of women and men; include gender-specific inputs and indicators; and promote the active participation of women in all aspects of project implementation.

#### All climate information and early warning products and services will be developed and delivered taking into account the gender-specific (and other) needs of the intended end-user. This will include addressing the different vulnerabilities faced by women, men, boys and girls as well as additional social factors that influence adaptive capacity, resilience and participation in climate action.

#### Targeted interventions focused on improving women’s access to early warning information and empowering women to participate in decision-making processes will be implemented under Results 3 and 4 of the Project, which will enhance the impact of GCF investments and gender mainstreaming approaches highlighted above.

#### Monitoring and evaluation will track progress towards the Project objectives in relation to gender equality against the indicators and targets detailed in the Gender Action Plan; and ensure the collection of sex-disaggregated data against each of the relevant indicators outlined in the Logical Framework (Section 4.3).

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP171

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths

- High impact potential: The project will directly increase the resilience to climate risks of over 20% of the population and remaining 20% indirectly, through improved climate information services.
- Comprehensive approach to climate risk, including early warning systems (EWS) going beyond the usual climate information focus in Timor-Leste to better respond to the challenges in Timor-Leste.

### Synergies with existing projects (e.g. FP170)

- Will benefit from the improved monitoring and early warning information (especially in rural and mountainous areas), better access to and stakeholder engagement channels, and mainstreaming adaptation into planning.


---
**Page 117**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

#### 4. Time-Leste is a least developed country (LDC) and an island small developing State (SIDS) regionally vulnerable to climate change impacts on ecosystems and infrastructure over past 25 years of conflict. Climate change—manifested through rising mean temperatures, warming and rising seas, ocean acidification, desertification, fires, droughts, and more extreme weather events such as cyclones, flooding, landslides, and storm surges—poses significant challenges to Timor-Leste’s development. Climate-related risks to lives, livelihoods, health, biodiversity, food security, water supply and sanitation, energy, transport, communications, and other critical infrastructure, extreme weather events, wildfires and emerging infectious diseases, with disproportionate impacts on women and children, are expected to increase in the future due to climate change, land use changes, desertification, associated biodiversity loss and worsening air quality. In 2019, the World Risk Index identified Timor-Leste as one of the most vulnerable countries globally to natural disasters, as a result of its location, geography and very limited capacity to prepare for and recover from climate-related hazards.

#### 5. As the effects of climate change manifest and the frequency and intensity of climate-related hazards increase, Timor-Leste needs accurate, timely and actionable information and early warning systems (EWS) that can help reduce the risks associated with climate change and environmental health. Investments in infrastructure and institutional frameworks, which have been the focus of previous development efforts, are not sufficient to be supported by high quality climate information and evidence-based advice on planning adaptation to impacts from climate change. Disaster risk and public health management must integrate impact-based forecasts and warnings into their decision-making processes.

#### 6. The project will help transform Timor-Leste’s climate information services early warning system (EWS) into a fully-fledged, integrated, multi-hazard EWS that includes all five MHIWSS elements prepared by the partners of the International Network for MHIWSS: disaster risk knowledge; detection, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of hazards and possible consequences; communication of hazard information to decision-makers; early warning and response capabilities. The system will be upgraded from a status where it is not able to provide basic services in terms of climate change impacts on agriculture, water resources, natural climate outlooks, climate monitoring and aviation and ocean services. In addition, the system will include an integrated public health component that provides evidence-based disaster and health impact forecasting, responding to critical impacts of climate change in Timor-Leste.

#### 7. The proposal provides an adequate discussion of key barriers to achieve this transformation: i) limited capacity for data collection and analysis; ii) limited capacity for climate services and DRM; iii) inadequate observation networks and limited sector-specific climate information; iv) lack of a national early warning system (EWS); v) lack of decision-making information; and vi) Limited capacity and funding to prepare for and manage climate risks. The four components of the project address each of these barriers, as described below.

#### 8. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the relevant services and risk management, at the levels of national and sub-national government, technical institutions and communities. It will also involve a wide range of stakeholders to ensure that the information generated and acted upon. UNEP, as executing entity (EE), will work with a network of regional and global technical partners, including the Regional Integrated Multi-Hazard Early Warning

### Project financing

#### Table 1: Project financing

| Source | Amount (USD) | Use | Amount (USD) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GCF | 20,980,722 (grant) | Delivery model and legislation for climate information and early warning systems | 491,279 (2.3%) |
| Government of Timor-Leste | 748,400 (in-kind) | Forecasting of climate impacts and its consequences | 12,772,742 (58.6%) |
| Total | **21,729,122** | **Total: Delivery model and legislation for climate information and early warnings Enhanced risk management capacity Project evaluation and contingencies** | **13,264,021 (61.7%)** |

#### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

##### Component 1: Strengthened delivery model and legislation for climate information and multi-hazard early warning services (total cost USD 9,580,729; 100% from GCF)

- **Objective:** To improve the capacity of Timor-Leste to provide climate information and reduced vulnerability by developing legislative, regulatory and policy frameworks for climate services and DRM, as well as a financial framework and business model for long-term sustainability. Enhanced delivery models will ensure that climate information is available in a timely manner.
- This component will help Timor-Leste reduce the risk of maladaptation in their economic development.

##### Component 2: Strengthened observation network, monitoring analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts (total cost USD 12,772,742; 58.6% from GCF)

- This component is at the core of the project, with 70% of the funding focused primarily on improved capacity for climate services and DRM in Timor-Leste through the development of ICT hardware and software and technical skills for DMN.
- The component will address the need for observation networks that provide the critical data underlying weather and climate information. It will also support the development of a national network to enable communities to take early action. On the other hand, the component will build capacity to translate climate information into actionable information (e.g., health, agriculture and disaster risk reduction) to inform appropriate preparedness and response actions.

##### The observation network will be upgraded and expanded, increasing station density and enhancing data management and ICT systems with an Internet of Things approach, thus enabling compliance with the WMO Global Basic Observing Network (GBOA). These weather


---
**Page 119**
---

# GCF.10/02/Add.05

Page 137

radars as well as a marine buoy will be installed and operational skills built to enhance extreme weather monitoring and early warning, including marine forecasts. A robust training programme will be delivered for DNMC staff to adapt to new processes and functions with the support of the GCF. The project will also provide technical assistance in developing information systems and regional organizations. RMES will provide further technical support after project completion.

Activity 2.2 will focus on strengthening climate modelling and impact-based forecasting capabilities through the establishment of a National Forecasting Centre, which would provide hardware and software for climate monitoring and early warning services including marine forecasting. Impact-based forecasting capabilities will be established, integrating hazard, exposure, vulnerability and risk information. This information will feed into an early warning support system for agriculture, disaster risk reduction and marine sectors that will allow for enhanced preparedness, early warning and rapid response. The National Forecasting Centre will also provide climate services to other national stakeholders.

Activity 2.3 will focus on the impacts of climate on health, targeting an often underrecognized set of consequences of climate change in the case of this project: dengue fever, malaria, cholera, food insecurity, and loss of biodiversity and wildlife. This will be delivered through: i) establishing a national Climate and Health Working Group with the participation of relevant stakeholders; ii) developing a framework for climate and health epidemiology, with the goal to define health risks, identify climate information needs of the health sector, and develop an early warning system for heatwaves, floods, droughts, etc. iii) developing a monitoring framework, with measurements for five fine particular matter, ground surface and NOx, to lead to better understanding of the relationship between air quality and climate change; iv) building forecasting and decision support system for health, as well as a mobile app for health advisors.

Component 3: Improved dissemination and communication of risk information and early warning (total cost USD 1.748 million; GCF co-cost USD 0.251 million), at 8% of total project cost.

The component will focus on building capacity for preparing and responding to climate risks and on the introduction of forecast-based financing (FbF), which will enable local and national authorities to take action in advance of extreme weather events, reducing disaster losses before a climate shock, reducing risks and enabling timely and effective responses. This approach will be implemented through the development of early warning systems for extreme weather events, including specific early warning information by training agriculture extension officers on DRRM and understanding forecasts and early warnings and providing agriculture DRM and climate change adaptation in their respective communities.

Component 4: Enhanced climate risk management capacity (total cost USD 3.718 million; GCF co-cost USD 0.521 million), at 8% of total project cost.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

The Project aims to comprehensively address the priority hazards of the country through an integrated and comprehensive approach that will significantly improve the resilience of communities in Timor-Leste, who are expected to benefit from the strengthening of nationwide climate services, multi-hazard early warning systems, and risk-informed decision-making. This includes 13,044 AWS (Automatic Weather Stations) being established across the country to provide real-time data on weather conditions and risk from worsening climate-related hazards, particularly from drought, flooding, and landslides that are expected to increase in frequency and intensity as climate change gathers pace.

The project will also establish a national early warning system for floods, landslides, and cyclones. It aims to reduce the number of deaths by 50% (from an average of 12 per year) and the number of people affected by disasters by 30%. The project is expected to reduce economic losses from climate-related hazards by 40%, with a reduction in loss of livelihoods and assets. Annual average losses estimated at USD 1.3bn with losses linked to climate change estimated to reach 6-7% of GDP by mid-century.

2. The proposal adequately articulates the impacts and benefits in terms of (i) health forecasts (including air pollution, heatwaves etc.) supporting public and environmental health, safety and well-being of the population, (ii) disaster risk reduction interventions reducing the vulnerability to floods and droughts and generating broader socio-economic benefits.
3. The proposed interventions have a high potential to maximize impacts on the ground.

### 2.2 Paradigm shift Scale: N/A

The proposal has adequately articulated the challenges, opportunities and the paradigm-shifting nature of addressing these challenges. Paradigm-shifting elements include:

- **Transformational planning and programming:** The proposal aims to optimize efficiency and effectiveness through quantitative and qualitative enhancement, coherence and complementarity between climate information services and disaster risk reduction interventions to maximize impacts on the ground. It has assembled some of the global authoritative bodies (e.g., WMO, UNFCCC, IPCC, World Bank, WHO, IREMS, FAO and UNESCO/ICTP) as partners and executing entities who will deliver specific activities through their respective mandates and capacities in close collaboration with partners and therefore demonstrating co-design and co-implementations. This articulated in Result 1: Strengthened delivery model and alignment for climate information and multi-hazard early warning services.

- **Catalyzing climate innovation:** The proposal has adopted some of the best available science and technology to catalyze climate innovation, including (i) climate change impact warnings and early warning systems. It proposes to develop an integrated and comprehensive climate information services covering oceans, land and the atmosphere and coupled with impact-based MHEWS in Timor-Leste, in alignment with the global authoritative bodies (e.g., WMO, UNFCCC, IPCC, World Bank, WHO, IREMS, FAO and UNESCO/ICTP). Observations and Monitoring (through Activity 2.1); ii) Climate Services Information System (through Activity 2.2); iii) Climate Change Impact Warning System (through Activity 2.3); iv) User Interface Platform (through Activity 1.1.1); and v) Capacity Development (building national capacity for climate information services).

- **The impact-based MHEWS has been designed based on the WMO/UNDRR/IPCC established standards and will strengthen the value chain by enhancing: i) disaster risk knowledge and understanding, ii) preparedness to reduce the likelihood of possible consequences, iii) warning dissemination and communication, iv) preparedness and response capability, v) capacity for early action and rapid response.**

- **The proposal will also establish anticipation actions (e.g., Forecast-based Financing) and enhance forecasting and decision-making mechanisms, timing and planning early actions and financing to reduce the impacts of climate hazards. It proposes three different approaches: i) before a hazard occurs (forecasts of climate hazards linked to in-depth analysis of the impact on human health and well-being, including heatwaves, air pollution or climate/weather hazards), enabling action to be triggered before the hazard occurs); ii) during and immediately after a hazard has occurred (e.g., early warning systems for immediate impacts and used to create a trigger to release finance for early action and early response); iii) across multiple time-scales where multiple sources of impact-based multi-hazard early warning information are combined to provide an integrated view of the climate hazards that could lead to disasters.

- In addition, it will pioneer additional climate services for the priority sectors of health, safety and well-being (environmental and public health) through health forecasts and air pollution monitoring. Through sub-Activity 2.3.1, it will establish a national Climate and Health Working Group to facilitate coordination and collaboration between the climate and health communities in Timor-Leste.


---
**Page 122**
---

28 In effect, the project seeks to introduce innovation in climate services using state-of-the-art frameworks, ICT infrastructure and services (Internet of Things, Cloud services, Bigdata analytics), etc) based on the Global Framework for Climate Services. Accurate, timely and actionable climate information and early warning services will be provided through demand-based financing. This will enable evidence-based planning, enhanced lead-times, and quicker responses. Financed by a combination of public and private sector funding sources, including donor funds and government budgets. These solutions will lead to reductions in operations and maintenance of infrastructure and delivery of services.

**Main project outputs:**

a. Market readiness for climate information and early warning services: Although the proposal targets the delivery of climate information and early warning as a public good, it proposes to establish a business delivery model that will ensure the sustainability of the service through market development for climate and early warning services in priority sectors and business segments during project implementation under risk mitigation measures. This includes the establishment of a market-based funding mechanism (e.g., budget allocation, WDM-GRON-SDFC, HIRC, DFID Disaster Risk and other sources).

b. Expansion and replication of knowledge: The project has recognized the very low capacity for climate information and early warning services in Tanzania and proposed capacity development across all the intervention areas. It has also acknowledged the need to enhance capacity at the national level through the establishment of a National Framework for Climate Services (NFC). This will be addressed under sub-activity 4.1.1: Technical support and capacity building for NCMO to plan, deploy and operate climate information services; sub-activity 4.1.2: Technical support and capacity building for NMIO to plan, deploy and operate early warning services (including Internet of Things [IoT] approaches); ### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: N/A

#### a)
The proposal demonstrates good return on investment. The proposed interventions have been demonstrated to significantly reduce operations and maintenance compared to traditional methods. A cost-benefit analysis (CBA) has been conducted, and the delivery model will be developed to enable effective coverage of operations and maintenance, sustainability, and replication.

#### b)
The proposed cost-benefit analysis (CBA) for the impact-based MHEWS in Timor-Leste shows a positive net present value (NPV) of USD 77.9 million at a 5% discount rate. The economic evaluation results indicate that the project is financially viable and economically justifying the viability of the intervention. A sensitivity analysis was conducted to test the robustness of this assessment, which showed that even with a 10% increase in costs or a 20% decrease in benefits, the ERR decreased with those simulated cost benefit changes, but the ERR remained well above the 10% threshold in both cases (90.4% and 91.5% respectively). Additionally, although more pessimistic assumptions were used for the discount rate (3%) and the project life span (20 years), the results show very high NPV and ERR (USD 81.7 million and 97.5%). In conclusion, the proposed intervention is financially viable.

#### c)
The level of co-financing is relatively low (3.4% of total financing). However, the project aims to develop and establish a sustainable business model under sub-activity 1.1.5 which will contribute towards co-financing from the Government and users for the sustainability of climate information services.


### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

##### a)
The project is expected to contribute to upgrading Timor-Leste's DWS, thereby improving the availability and use of limited data for climate risk management and enhancing the climate resilience of communities to weather-related disasters. The project focuses on strengthening the national meteorological service by including climate information and MHEWS. In addition, the project will modernize the national meteorological service by including climate information and MHEWS. The project is considered as a low-risk project, which is confirmed as being equivalent to GCF's category C classification by the Secretariat and within its ACR acceleration level.

##### b)
Among other activities in this component include capacity-building for DING, the national meteorological service, by strengthening operations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting capabilities. The project will also provide equipment for climate information and MHEWS. Equipment that will be provided by the project to upgrade and modernize the meteorological observation system will be installed, operated and maintained on existing government-owned


---
**Page 125**
---

land, thereby reducing costs and, importantly, avoiding social impacts associated with land acquisition or physical displacement. Potential minimal risks are mainly environmental, spatially and temporally restricted, and reversible. Examples may include vegetation clearing, sediment transport, and temporary changes in water quality. The project includes adequate safety risks to workers. The environmental and social safeguards report describes mitigation measures of the potential adverse effects on people and the environment as well as personal protective equipment for technicians installing hydrometeorological apparatus.

The AE considers that, as all Timorese are indigenous, special measures such as an indigenous peoples’ policy are not required. However, the project will ensure that Timorese benefit fully. Nevertheless, due to the variety of indigenous people in Timor-Leste and the prevalence of extreme poverty, care must be ensured to need the project is socially inclusive and responsive to the needs of all persons affected by the project, including all vulnerable groups. Importantly, the project includes targeted interventions based on the different needs of specific groups such as women, youth, and people with disabilities.

The project will ensure that Timorese benefit fully from adverse impacts and can fully benefit from the project. The feasibility study includes preliminary consultations with indigenous communities to identify their needs and concerns during the inception phase of the project. A beneficiary feedback mechanism will be established through the project implementation unit (PMU) to monitor the effectiveness of measures to be appropriate throughout project implementation and that the most vulnerable people are being reached.

The project will engage a specialist with an expertise in environmental and social safeguards to ensure the continuous screening of activities for risks and the implementation of mitigation measures. The AE will also provide technical assistance on environmental and social safeguard issues, including gender equality, through close coordination with contractors and the EE, the Secretary of State for the Environment (SSE).

Stakeholder consultations were undertaken during the project development stage in a series of workshops held between 2018 and 2019. The stakeholder engagement plan includes a report on consultations held with national authorities and non-governmental organizations, including indigenous communities, to identify their needs and concerns during the inception stage. Furthermore, the project has developed a stakeholder engagement plan for the implementation stage. The plan identifies stakeholders, including community groups, that will be engaged. The representation of these groups in the PMU will ensure that they are adequately represented who will be recruited as part of the project management unit (PMU). The project manager will also work closely with indigenous communities and other relevant stakeholders during the inception phase of the project and prior to commencing implementation, elements of the engagement plan will be reviewed and revised accordingly at this time.

The project will use its AE’s stakeholder response mechanism to receive and coordinate the results from the consultations held. The GCF Independent Redress Mechanism will be available to affected or potentially affected people and communities. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the AE will ensure that indigenous peoples are provided for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made. Details of how both mechanisms can be accessed is included in the environmental and social safeguards report. It is recommended that the AE ensures that information on both mechanisms is communicated to stakeholders prior to commencing implementation.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of GCF.


---
**Page 126**
---

The gender assessment discusses the enabling environment for the pursuit of gender equity and women’s empowerment in the context of project in Timor-Leste. The constitution of Timor-Leste protects against discrimination based on gender and calls for equality between men and women. Timor-Leste has ratified several international conventions to achieve gender equality and the empowerment of all women and girls. It also launched a National Action Plan for United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 on Women, Peace and Security in 2014. The Gender Equality Unit (GEU) within the Ministry of Finance is responsible for implementing this plan.

The Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan also includes gender aspects across policies, laws, processes, and programs, including women’s access to education and leadership, reproductive health and the reduction of gender-based violence (GBV).

The gender assessment was completed based on a desk review, primary data collection and stakeholder consultations. Despite a recently strengthened enabling environment, there continues to be significant barriers for women in Timor-Leste. These include limited access to land, credit, education as well as limited access to maternal and childcare services. Malnutrition, food insecurity and hunger are also major issues affecting women. Traditional cultural norms assign strict roles for men and women, which often lead to discriminatory practices against women. At the local level, women have been largely excluded from decision-making processes in government institutions. Women’s representation is low: only 10% of women are involved in parliamentary representation with 23 out of 68 seats being held by women. Women's access to credit and land is also limited due to traditional cultural norms that restrict their rights, which mostly exclude women. The lack of land rights among women perpetuates their inability to access resources for income generation.

Gender-based violence against women is widespread in Timor-Leste with almost 60 per cent of women having experienced some form of gender-based violence during the past year. Women’s access to justice against violence is hampered by a lack of awareness and understanding, as well as low levels of reporting. In 2014, only about one out of four cent report to police mainly because of fear of repercussions, family pressures, economic dependence and old beliefs.

The gender-sensitive sector includes agriculture and food security, disaster risk reduction, energy, health and water. The gender assessment has identified key barriers that need to be addressed in order to achieve gender equality. These include the fact that women are still gender gaps in productivity attributed to lower access to factors of production including hired labor, farming tools, limited investment in cash crop production and farmers’ groups. These are due to limited access to credit and land, as well as lack of information on market prices, household duties, time poverty and cultural norms. Lower income levels among women also have a considerable impact on their ability to participate fully in the economy. Women’s participation is further hampered by limited access and input in decision-making related to risk reduction, preparation and response to natural disasters. The lack of access to information and communication technologies, information sources and networks, and fewer assets to support resilience after climate change shocks are also major barriers for women.

The energy sector accounts for 90 per cent of the energy needs of Timor-Leste. This contributes to the time burden and time poverty that affect many women. Kerosene lamps and candles provide light for the majority of households, which is a significant barrier as it limits their ability to participate in their domestic duties. The high prices and fluctuation of kerosene prices place a significant burden on women who are responsible for purchasing this fuel. This also increases energy waste and saturation increase their exposure to health risks and also contribute to time poverty. The gender assessment has identified several key barriers that need to be addressed.

These include: men's primary positions in decision-making and information transmission; women’s less access to communication technologies; sociocultural norms in decision-making,

# 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

### 5.0 GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 20.98 million to finance climate information services and related activities in Timor-Leste.

#### 5.1 The government of Timor-Leste is providing USD 7.5 million in in-kind contribution. There is no co-financing from the AF.

### 5.2 Timor-Leste is an LDC and LSS. There is limited scope for the government to incur public debt, which could be a risk during the project implementation period. Thus, the government is seeking a grant from GCF. The project includes activities that are expected to generate revenue through the identification of long-term sources of funds and capitalization opportunities. While the project is expected to generate revenue in the medium term, the generation of revenue for FWS cost recovery and revenue generation are not considered feasible within five years of the project implementation period.


---
**Page 128**
---

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (medium-high risk)

- The AE, UNEP, has a track record in implementing projects related to climate change information management and EWS in SIDS.
- The AE will act as a co-EE for certain sub-activities under components 1.2 and 3.

#### SSE as co-EE
- It is noted that SSE is also the national designated authority (NDA).
- It has experience in implementing projects financed by external donors; however, most projects over the past five years have been implemented by UNEP.
- The AE's capacity assessment to be carried out by the AE is pending. The AE may need to support the EE during the implementation and strengthen the capacity of the EE.

### 4.3.3 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### Government approval
- The project will require the approval of the Council of Ministers, which is beyond the project scope.
- For example, the draft of the Meteorological Act (sub-activity 1.2.2) will require the approval of the Council of Ministers.

#### Budget security
- The budget security risk is important as it will formalize the role of UNMIG and provide it with budget security.

#### O&M risk
- The funding proposal identified O&M-related constraints as risks with high probability.
- These constraints are due to limited financing and human resources. The project will hire new technical staff with necessary skills, but these positions may not be filled immediately if the required personnel are not locally available; an international technician will need to train the staff.
- UNMIG has provided a letter of commitment for the provision of O&M support from its headquarters in Nairobi for up to 20 years. However, a detailed financing plan is not available.

#### Financial framework
- A financial framework for sustainable operation (sub-activity 1.6.1) will be developed during the project.

### 4.3.4 GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

#### Impact of proposal on GCF portfolio concentration in terms of financial materiality
- In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of financial materiality is not material.

### 4.3.5 Compliance risk (high risk)

#### Office of Risk Management and Compliance request
- The Office of Risk Management and Compliance has requested the AE to submit the final risk assessment of the counterparties on several occasions but, due to country-specific factors, the AE could not produce the same.
- Since O&M/Compliance cannot finalize its assessment of the project's compliance with the GCF rules, the AE is unable to provide a final risk assessment. The AE wishes to remind the AE of its obligations under clause 4.05 of the accreditation master agreement and will continue to work closely with O&M/Compliance to identify any necessary arrangements so that all applicable provisions of the AMA can be met before the funding proposal is submitted for financial review.

#### Rationale

The role of the AE is to improve the policy frameworks and to finance O&M cost will be critical to the sustainability of the project. The EE's capacity to implement the project will be supported by the technical assistance provided by UNEP. This will support the EE during the implementation and strengthen the capacity of the EE.

### Fiduciary

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

a. UNEP will be the AE for the project. SSE in Tiner-Losse will serve as the national RE- SSE will be accountable to UNEP for project execution at the national level and for the effective and efficient use of resources. At the request of the national designated authority, UNEP will undertake the role of the RE.

b. As the AE, UNEP will be responsible for managing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the activities under the project. UNEP will monitor the progress of the project in accordance with the approved work plan and the disbursement and application of GCF grant proceeds by the EE. UNEP will ensure that the grant proceeds are utilized in a manner consistent with the terms and conditions of the Project Agreement and in line between GCF and UNEP and the AMA. UNEP will also assume a limited role as RE.

c. UNEP will set up and manage a PMU, drawing on its Global Support Services Agreement with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). The PMU will provide management support and assist to the national implementation of the project—through the coordination of the activities of the EE and other service providers—and will oversee project implementation, in line with their obligations under the respective legal instruments—and will coordinate with the RE. The PMU will also be responsible for preparing and submitting progress reports and quarterly financial reports, including co-financing reports and annual audit reports, from the EE and technical partners and submit these to the AE. The PMU will provide support on project implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting, procurement and technical issues. It will establish contact with other development partners in the country and coordinate with them where their respective fields are complementary and to seek opportunities for collaboration. The PMU will also provide secretariat services to the RE.

d. UNEP will enter into an appropriate agreement (Project Cooperation Agreement or PCA) with SSE for the execution of the project. The PCA will establish clear roles and responsibilities for the implementation of the project, including the terms and conditions for the determination of the prevailing fiduciary duties; and the terms and conditions for arbitration in case of disputes. UNEP will be responsible for overseeing the national RE-EE project execution, financial management, personnel administration and reporting to UNEP. SSE will be responsible for implementing the project on behalf of the national EE and project implementation in a relevant part of the government administration to provide implementation guidance and support to national service providers and technical partners. The PCA will establish clear roles and responsibilities for establishing national project implementation in a relevant part of the government administration to provide implementation guidance and support to national service providers and technical partners, as well as UNEP's role in providing oversight and monitoring and implementation guidance and will facilitate cooperation and coordination among the national service providers and technical partners.

e. The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by United Nations financial regulations, rules and practices, as well as UNEP’s project manual.

### Results monitoring and reporting

As an adaptation initiative, the project aims to benefit about 1,293,119 beneficiaries including 1,018,495 direct beneficiaries. The intervention is expected to deliver direct benefits to 80% of the population in Timor-Leste, of whom women comprise 511,690 and 679,613 indirect beneficiaries. This project will be monitored using a combination of quantitative and qualitative indicators.

The project targets different types of adaptation activities which are expected to generate impact across two adaptation result areas: A1 - Most vulnerable people, communities and regions and A2 - Health and well-being, food and water security. This is property demonstrated in the logical framework and the theory of change, which have been understood to come about in the country. The goal statement is properly articulated, and stated outcomes are specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, time-bound (SMART) and directly support the immediate project goal.

The logical framework appropriately applies GEF Fundamental inputs and outcome) RMI/PMI indicators, and the project has been based on data collection with triangulation to inform progress reporting on expected results in addition to integrating project performance monitoring into the overall project management information system implementation.

The implementation timetable has been found to follow the required structure and format. The milestones and deliverables are well articulated and reporting timelines are provided as required.


### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 December 2016 ("the MA"), and it became effective on 29 February 2017.

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.


The Government of Timor-Leste is not a party to this Agreement, which means GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has been engaging with the Government of Timor-Leste and negotiations with the Government of Timor-Leste are ongoing as of 20 August 2019.

The funds of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, any legal actions against the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP171

## Proposal name: Enhancing early warning systems to build greater resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards in Timor-Leste

### Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

### Country/ies:
Democratic Republic of Timor-Leste

### Project/programme size:
Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential: Scale: High

#### 1.1.1 Timor-Leste is a post-conflict newly independent country in the Asia-Pacific region.
This small island developing State (SIDS) continues to be faced with hazards that are linked with climate change, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts and subsequent inundation, extreme rainfall events, flooding, landslides in hilly terrain and prolonged dry spells. These hazards are exacerbated by the El Niño/Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and other global scale upper air circulation and ENSO/Southern Oscillation. Most of the population in the country are poor and their livelihoods and well-being depend on nature-based production systems, such as agriculture, fishing, and forestry. These systems are vulnerable to climate change through climatic perturbation and associated hazards. In addition to the livelihoods of the people, their food security, water resources, and infrastructure are also at risk from these hazards.

#### 1.1.2 Globally available literature report on indications that the frequency and intensity of many tropical cyclones in the South Pacific islands of the Asia-Pacific region will only increase under climate change.
It is widely known that advisory notices and fairly accurate early warnings can be issued to communities living in coastal areas, but this is not possible for inland communities. The majority production-based livelihoods and have little or no formal education can safeguard their livelihoods from these hazards.

#### 1.1.3 With the advent of much better algorithms in computational models, the genesis and subsequent movement of life-threatening super cyclones and other weather fronts can be predicted and people can be warned to take appropriate action.
This technological breakthrough is available to only a handful of countries and, unfortunately, Timor-Leste does not have access to this technology. The cost of skilled human resources to apply advanced multi-disciplinary science, or the computational software are too costly, or perhaps there is no effort towards continuously monitoring and gathering physical data in order to make these predictions.

#### 1.1.4 The institutions having the national mandate to do all the above may not be duly supported with adequate financial and human resources.
The lack of a comprehensive early warning system for hydro-meteorological hazards, coupled with the effects of an advanced climate information system, the overwhelming weaknesses cannot be addressed by ensuring international support for certain aspects of dynamic development.


---
**Page 133**
---

6. In addition, the legal meaning of "climate information" may be missing, which is why such advanced climate information cannot be provided to the potential users. The usual meteorological description of temperature and rainfall amounts, which are often provided through weather forecasts, do not provide a complete picture of the climate event that prevents people from understanding the various dimensions of an impending weather event. In many meteorological services, the technical descriptions of climate events have not been written or implemented, which eventually becomes a barrier to disseminating early warning notices. Lack of finance for regular operation and maintenance (O&M) is another ongoing problem in many developing countries. The lack of adequate funding has led to deteriorating protocols so that they can raise finance from the advisory users and contribute sufficient finance for the maintenance of the equipment.

7. Timor-Leste is at least developed country that has not yet dealt with the generation and subsequent dissemination of advanced climate information. Its current institutional capacity is at a rudimentary level, which means that it cannot provide adequate support to its citizens in terms of early warning services. The country's development partners have provided financial support for equipment acquisition and installation but this has been done without considering human resources and the absence of planning; the facilities and equipment are all in a dislocated condition. Timor-Leste is not yet able to generate climate information, let alone disseminate it. However, such services are below the "basic" category described in the international standard set out by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO).

8. The country's development partners need to make significant capacity-building effort so that the country can make its citizens aware of any climate-driven hazard. If this is achieved, then Timor-Leste will be able to provide adequate support for its citizens such that people can integrate the information into their decision-making and planning frameworks. This capacity-building involves integrating between the meteorological services and other relevant national agencies in Timor-Leste's government and well-being. However, all of this cannot be achieved by Timor-Leste alone: it must achieve the advanced capacity through international collaboration with its development partners and other countries that have similar experience. Such collaboration is needed for aspects such as meteorology, oceanography, hydrology and hydrometeorology, climate change adaptation, etc., which are beyond Timor-Leste's capability. This should ideally be complemented with its country collaboration and coordination among various institutions.

9. In view of the growing toll on Timor-Leste in terms of loss and damage resulting from the increasing impacts of climate change, it is necessary to provide adequate support to the country so that it can better manage these risks. The project aims to build up the institutional capacity to deliver most of the necessary items for upgrading Timor-Leste's institutional capacity on climate information services. The project will be implemented by a designated implementing entity (IE) for the project. The Secretary of State of the Environment (SSE) is the executing entity and the Ministry of Natural Resources, Energy Technology and Geophysics (DMNG), and the latter is in charge of driving all deliverables under the project during the project period of five years and beyond. The IE, SSE and the DMNG will be supported by a host of international organizations and institutions in various capacities.

10. The project has four components:

(a) Establishing institutional and policy frameworks for climate information and multi-hazard early warning services;

(b) Strengthening observation, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts; and

(c) Improving dissemination and communication of risk information and early warning;

**Page 134**
---

## Enhancing climate risk management capacity

### A well-analyzed baseline situation is one of the key determining factors that enables a clear understanding of the impact of any targeted intervention in a project.

Following questions raised by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the proponents delivered a needs assessment study. This document provides an overview of the current state of climate risk management at the national level, including its strengths and weaknesses. DNMG is the overall custodian for climate information services in Timor-Leste, with a mandate to provide such services.

While DNMG has the legal mandate to develop and deliver climate information, there are other national organizations that also have mandates related to climate change adaptation. These include the National Disaster Management Directorate, the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries, the National Meteorological Service, the National Water Resources Agency, and the National Works and the National Directorate for Water Resources Management. In addition to these organizations, there are other organizations and agencies that work closely with community members and are included in this study.

### The needs assessment study states that the Government of Timor-Leste offers a category 1 level of climate service through the involvement of DNMG, as per the definitions provided by WMO. DNMG has recently undertaken several initiatives to improve its efforts to deliver useful climate information. However, among many deficiencies, the highly technical nature of climate change adaptation makes the delivery of reliable and useful climate information extremely difficult.

### Stakeholder consultations involving key actors and general stakeholders have found that there is a lack of adequate protocols for information dissemination in both meteorological and hydrological products. In addition, there is an national legal mandate calling for disaster risk reduction (DRR) and climate change adaptation (CCA). However, this mandate cannot currently be fulfilled because of organizational, human resources and financial-related barriers. The Government has not yet developed a comprehensive strategy into actions are scanty and uncertain. There is a lack of adequate protocols for information dissemination in both meteorological and hydrological products. This applies to all sectors and all sectors, public and private. There is a great need to enhance national capacities so that people's aspirations regarding climate information and DRR may be adequately addressed. This is why the Government has requested support from GEF to establish a system for collecting data and generating user-friendly climate services.

### To produce impact-based forecasts, information on meteorology and oceanography needs to be integrated with hydrological and hydrometeorological information as well as geology and hydraulics. The initial funding proposal had little indication of its modality to integrate information concerning hydrology and hydraulics. However, the AE explained to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) that this was not a problem because the project FP109 titled "Safeguarding communities from their physical assets from climate-induced disasters" is already addressing this issue. The AE also stated that the Government engaged in formal cooperation with the respective AE for FP109 so there is no shortage of hydrological as well as hydrometeorological information. However, the AE did not provide details on the project. The AE confirmed that, for the planned collaboration, the effort would not require any additional funding and would be based on the existing capacity to improve the performance of planned forecasting, especially for water-related hazards and disasters.

### The project focuses on establishing an impact-based forecasting early warning system (IBF-EWS) in order to provide a more reliable climate information service. This requires adequate justification. On the basis of the initial assessment of independent TAP and subsequent discussions with the AE, it is not clear how this project will contribute to mainstream information services, which need no further explanation on climate relevance of the project since there is little continuous longitudinal meteorological data in this war-torn country. Timor-Leste needs support from GEF to establish a system for collecting data and generating user-friendly climate services.


---
**Page 135**
---

friendly clients. This itself is a major driver towards seeking GCF financing for which no additional climate justification is required.

The project aims to reach the entire population of the country (about 1.3 million) with its climate information services. About 80 per cent (i.e., 1,054 million) of the population is considered male and about 20 per cent (i.e., 267 million) are female. The majority are male, while the rest are female. If meaningful and socially worthwhile forecasts and advisory notices are developed and disseminated in a manner that is understandable to all segments of society and if simple dissemination modalities are applied, achieving such a high target number for direct beneficiaries becomes possible. This is because the project will be implemented through local resources, technologies including modelling skills - all within five years following inception – appears feasible. The DNGC has already demonstrated its capacity in this regard. It acknowledges the extremely low capacity of the DNGC to deliver all those under the age of 15 years old. This is one of the objectives of the project. A well-coordinated all-out effort will be needed, involving all parties, in order to achieve the objective.

A total budget of USD 21.729 million is proposed, of which GCF contribution is sought amounting to USD 20.860 million as grant. Co-financing of USD 0.748 million is offered by the Government of India.

In view of the above discussions, the independent TAP is of the opinion that the overall impact potential of the project is high.


### Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium to high

The current provisions for making people (including farming communities) and members aware of climate/weather-induced hazardous phenomena are at a rudimentary state in Tharparkar. The lack of adequate infrastructure, limited human resources, inadequate human capacity - the skills and capacity of the staff to handle advanced data acquisition equipment, and the absence of an information system that can be used for real-time data analysis and decision making - all these factors have severely curtailed the country's capacity to utilize budgetary allocations. The annualized national funding committed to the climate information services sector is USD 10 million. This amount is not sufficient even to take the first stages to upgrade the capacity for acquiring real-time weather data could not be kept in a functional state for long.

An information assessment has been done via stakeholders' participation and by applying perceptions of different groups of people. In line with the current information system, the findings indicate that there are five major gaps: (a) lack of adequate infrastructure in terms of IT equipment for building a database on climatology and partly on oceanography; (b) climate/weather data acquisition and processing capacity; (c) inadequate human resource provisioning, human capacity and skills enhancement, monitoring and data acquisition frameworks, regulatory frameworks including operational protocols; (d) capacity-building for disaster risk management and early warning systems; and (e) lack of a framework for incorporating other provisions including hydrology and hydraulics) to establish multi-hazard forecasting and alert mechanisms. The project will address all these gaps [1] establishing a financial arrangement for achieving long-term sustainability of the system.

If all the above items are to be delivered, mostly through the overwhelming support of the international collaborating centres of excellence and potentially a GCF-financed project


---
**Page 136**
---

FP109. The design shift may be achieved. Such a shift would ensure that a transition is made from an ad-hoc/interim reactive response approach to an informed and planned responsive regime. The funding proposal indicates that the proposed project would achieve all the above and more within five years of inception. However, the independent TAP considers the timeline to be extremely challenging.

The challenge regarding a short timeline can be overcome, especially when the bulk of the project activities are expected to be carried out by the DNGC, which is currently heavily recruited international consultants. However, the intention for implementing capacity enhancement and institutional strengthening through training, mentoring, and on-the-job acquired skills, equipment and human resources appears overwhelmingly difficult, if not impossible, during the first two years of the duration of the project. More specifically, the weak capacity of the recipient institution (DNGC) is a major challenge regarding its ability to sustain its function following the departure of the large number of international consultants. The DNGC faces another significant challenge regarding its ability to sustain its function following the departure of the large number of international consultants.

The realization of project shift through the project relies heavily on successful establishment of complementarity, where integration of early forecasting aspects of hazards in relation to climate change and hydrological changes is a key element. The success of such collaboration largely depends on the respective national actors. In the case of FP109, the ministry responsible for water resources is not included as an executing or the decision-making body for the project management. In the absence of all such arrangements in the current funding proposal, the AE took the initiative and formally engaged with the AE for FP109 to sign a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on 25th May 2017. This MoU covers the process of data acquisition and continued integration regarding hydrology and hydraulics into the current project.

The project is expected to provide early warning and climate information for about 80 per cent of the population, which means that there is hardly any room for replication within Timor-Leste. The AE has already engaged with the DNGC to replicate the project. Although at the outset it appears that this replication potential is somewhat good for populations in the north and east of the country, the technical specifications and requirements are not strong enough to run the models and integrate multi-disciplinary sciences and various state-of-the-art technologies into a single system. The independent TAP recommends a regional SIDS-wide approach towards developing an IWRM EWS with adequate financing under a recipient body such as WMO, which might be more sustainable in the longer term.

The project intends to build with fit-for-purpose equipment and technologies that are already available on the market. However, there is little scope for innovation or capacity-building and upgrading the system is also common. Therefore, there is little scope for innovation at this stage; however, there is locally relevant innovative technology such as blending meteorological and hydrological data to generate DRR-related warnings and forecasts with extended lead time for people to react early. The design of best dissemination pathways for the project should be considered carefully. It is recommended that social media can be exploited to the fullest, keeping in view the actual present use of such technologies by Timor-Leste.


---
**Page 137**
---

28 The project is about capacity-building. Therefore, potential for knowledge and learning is at the center of most activities. There will be training events for trainers to acquire up-to-date knowledge on the use of advanced technologies. Those train-the-trainer events will be supplemented by a series of workshops for local government officials and other stakeholders to understand advisory notices and integrate into DRR and local-level planning. The local institutions will also be supported in their efforts to mainstream climate change into daily business, especially in dealing with DRR. The logical framework has presented tools to monitor and report on the progress of delivery.

29 The project is not ambitious for the project to be short for the creation of a market for climate information. Moreover, the AE notes that any future market-based demand on climate information will be driven by the private sector, which may be accomplished within the stipulated five years. Real market expansion might happen later.

30 There is no Standing Order on Disaster (SOD) document. However, the project proposes to use the SOD as a legal instrument for disaster risk reduction and not as a policy boundary. There is no placeholder in the proposal for conducting extensive advocacy at the national scale for the complete development of a legal instrument such as a SOD. The AE along with DNGC must stipulate that the project will be able to conduct extensive advocacy at the national level if legislation on advisory notices and forecasts could be problematic. It is up to the Government to simultaneously ensure that the project can conduct extensive advocacy at the national scale, while also having a legally binding regulatory framework. The policy environment is conducive to pushing this agenda. The project can push the agenda by reinforcing its own stakeholders and coordinate their activities.

31 In view of the above discussion, the independent TAP finds the paradigm shift potential medium to high.


### 1.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

The project is expected to contribute towards achieving the sustainable development goals (SDGs) in a number of ways. The most direct contribution will be to SDG12 as means of taking actions to combat climate change and its impacts. In any given set-up, the development of multi-functional agricultural systems that are resilient to climate change, while safeguarding people's livelihoods and food security, occasionally saves lives in the case of rapid-onset weather hazards, and significantly reduce the burden of loss and damage.

As a result of these direct and indirect elements, the proposed project would contribute (indirectly) to the following SDGs:

- **SDG 2 (zero hunger):** helping farming communities by providing forecasts with increased lead time to enable them to address uncertainties due to the occurrence of extreme weather events.
  
- **SDG 3 (good health and well-being):** giving people lead time to take precautionary measures before suffering livelihoods and assets from being adversely affected by climatic hazards.

- **SDG 5 (gender equality):** allowing women to participate equally in life-saving activities as well as contribute to DRR-related actions.

- **SDG 17 (partnership):** helping the recipient country to forge a healthy partnership regime on generation and contribution to a globally acceptable climate dataset for the use of global communities.


### Environmental co-benefits

The project proposal does not establish direct linkages between climate change and enhanced forest fire. The majority of fires in Timor-Leste, referred


---
**Page 138**
---

## Economic co-benefit: The project offers potential economic co-benefits. First, the farming community will be able to plant their crops earlier than usual due to regulatory notices and planning their crop rotations early, thereby safeguarding the standing crops from frost damage. Second, the project can reduce the likelihood of catastrophic destruction of infrastructure in case of a cyclonic storm surge or a potential landslide. The introduction of the new regulatory regime might create opportunities for additional steps towards reducing the vulnerability of the population to high wind events.

## Although it appears too optimistic that the implementation of forecast-based financing (FbF) could be completed within 12 months from now (as per the project implementation), once the background works have been accomplished, the business sector is likely to receive information suitable for its needs (which need to be established first), and the active participation of the private sector will be a key factor in ensuring the success of FbF in the long run. The project may not deliver an effective FbF regime, but it may pave the way for a future FbF initiative.

## Social co-benefit: The proposed EWS is likely to accrue social benefits. A life saved from drowning in an area as a result of the dissemination of an early warning has huge social implications. For example, the reduction in the number of deaths due to the El Niño/Southern Oscillation in the Pacific Ocean can safeguard production by thousands of farmers and fishermen. The proposed EWS is likely to reduce the number of people who notice can avoid the harm of millions of citizens. The socio-benefits likely to be accrued through the project include the reduction in the number of deaths due to flooding, the early integration of data and information on hydrology and hydraulics, in addition to climatology and oceanography. The involvement of the Ministry of Health (MoH) and other allied organizations will be of great help in achieving such co-benefits.

## The modeling-based forecasts can be quite useful in generating health advisory notices regarding the potential impact of climate change on public health. Once the health advisory notices are generated and disseminated, the health-care system can find ways to manage health risks, particularly at young ages.

## The proposed project is likely to integrate gender concerns in relation to accessing climate information and taking necessary response measures. If the stakeholder engagement is designed with a focus on women’s participation, it will be more effective than technology-driven early warnings. However, utmost care is necessary to design the last-mile dissemination of such information. In many cases, the last mile does not consider gender issues in the design and the actual dissemination occurs at times and in places where women are unlikely to receive the information; the ultimate objectives around gender equality will be defeated.

## The proposal intends to arrange a gender awareness programme and also proposes measures for ensuring that women’s voices are heard during the project implementation and stakeholder engagements. Moreover, the project intends to integrate gender concerns by incorporating equal representation of women and equal participation in planning, decision-making, capacity-building, and monitoring and evaluation activities. In this case one can look towards implementation of the gender action plan for the project.

## In view of the above considerations, the independent EAP is of the opinion that the sustainable development potential of the project is high.

**Page 139**

1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

The country has faced a war of independence that lasted for about a quarter of a century from 1975 to 2003 and devastated critical infrastructure and killed one third of the population. By the time the country began to rebuild, climate variability and change had begun to manifest itself in the form of more frequent and intense droughts and floods. The country has experienced a general rise in surface temperature and a seasonal decline in rainfall – which is expected to continue into the future. In addition, the country’s agricultural sector is facing challenges of loss of surface moisture during critical crop growing periods. Moreover, heat stress on livestock is also becoming more frequent. The country’s urban infrastructure was denuded during the war of independence and erratic extreme rainfall events are thought to be causing landslides in undulated terrains, further causing degradation of forest ecosystems and steep slopes. These factors have led to a decline in agricultural productivity.

Agriculture is a critically important sector in the country, providing a subsistence living for over 80 per cent of the country’s population. Slash-and-burn agricultural practices are rampant and contribute significantly to deforestation and soil erosion. The country is also being affected by affecting crop acreage and thereby people’s food security. Flooding due to incessant rainfall events has led to loss of crops, livestock and infrastructure. In addition, extreme weather events are believed to be adversely affecting the country’s development by destroying both public and private property, disrupting the economy, and disrupting essential services, including transport and health.

Under these circumstances, the country needs to develop and disseminate early warning systems that its citizens can plan and execute adaptive responses. However, the infrastructure has been partly destroyed, disrupting the collection of data – a critical input for climate change adaptation planning. The country has not invested in the past by development partners to help the country to partially rebuild the war-ravaged weather infrastructure. This has led to the loss of historical climate data and the destruction of some immeasurable, resulting in fragmentation of the data sets available for better understanding of the climate system. The lack of O&M can be partly explained by severe lack of investment in O&M, which is a critical component of any early warning system. In addition, there has been an overall minuscule budgetary allocation for the handoff of staff maintained by DNMG.

In order to ensure that the country’s weather and climate information services are functioning properly, the country and its relevant institutions need international support. First of all, the dilapidated and outdated instruments for weather and oceanographic data collection need to be replaced with modern equipment. Secondly, there is a need to strengthen the human resources and budgetary allocations so that the staff can embrace digital challenges in handling data and information. The country needs to invest in the development of an early warning system that will elevate its standing through a massive capacity-building effort, following a logical step-wise process, which will ensure the sustainability of the system in general and subsequently disseminating reliable climate information services.

However, given the annual level of public spending, it is beyond the current capacity of the Government of Timor-Leste to bear the full cost of maintaining a fully functional EWS. While


---
**Page 140**
---

## Lack of financing is certainly a barrier in the process. However, it is the near non-existing capacity of the DNING which is the largest barrier. There is no question whether the institution can absorb the additional capacity within the stipulated time frame. The more pressing issue is how far the institution can organize itself to absorb the added capacity within the stipulated time frame. This is a major concern, especially in view of the fact that the DNING has been functioning in an ad hoc manner, perhaps one little step at a time so that the newly recruited staff and technologists do not necessarily have the prerequisite of delivering high-quality climate information products within the project lifetime. The institutional collaboration agreement under the project needs to be extended for a few more years following project completion. If necessary, the DNING needs to arrange for additional capacity building support from centres of excellence until the locally trained actors are fully capable of running the programme.
45. Since the country is prone to climate hazards, integration of hydrology and hydraulics with meteorology and oceanography is extremely important, especially if the climate information services are to be used in a manner that would help reduce disaster risk. However, this cannot be fully realized if the Government does not streamline its allied regulatory framework. There is a need for an appropriate legal regime to ensure that the DNING has the necessary mandate to disseminate of disaster-related forecasts and advisory notices. The lack of an appropriate legal regime is one of the major barriers identified in the feasibility study report of the project to address the barrier and forge a mechanism to help the Government deliver the legal and regulatory regime.
46. From the above, the current barriers have been identified as the feasibility study report of the proposal and addressed in the proposal. However, questions remain as to whether all such barriers can be overcome within the stipulated time frame. The project will have to be started from scratch and there is hardly any prior experience of dealing with advanced technologies and equipment to deliver EWS in Timor-Leste.
47. The independent TAF finds the needs of the recipient to be high.

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: Medium to high

The alignment of the proposed project with national policy documents is commendable. A number of key decisions have been made by the Government that are aligned with the generation and dissemination of climate information services. The policy alignment is found in the following documents: (a) national adaptation plan of action (2010); (b) national disaster risk management strategy (2013); (c) National Climate Change Action Plan (2014); and (d) the nationally determined contributions to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (2014), and (e) the nationally determined contributions to the Paris Agreement (2015). The alignment of the project with these documents indicates a sense of utter necessity expressed by the recipient country. The objectives of the project clearly align with the national priorities.
48. UNEP is the AE for the project. UNEP also has the additional role of being an executing entity, perhaps a natural inclusion in view of the extremely poor capacity of both the other executing entities and the DNING to deliver the project within the stipulated time frame. However, the AE has also included a large number of allied international organizations to help bridge the current capacity gap. The team as a whole can deliver the project to any green country within the Asia-Pacific region.
