## Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

### As indicated in paragraph 15 above, the Government of Tente-Lente has offered a total amount of USD 748,000 as co-financing, which constitutes 3.44 per cent of the total budget. The total budget is USD 21,690,000 and the project proposal states that the GCF will provide USD 5,000,000 in grant funding to Tente-Lente. This amount is sufficient for the project as it requests GCF to provide access to the amount with full concessionality (i.e., no grant).

### While the financing plan is justified by each sub-component, the independent TAP cannot guarantee that all of these components will be implemented. The TAP has identified consultants who would offer capacity-building and other technical services. In this regard, the budgetary allocation for the project is sufficient to cover the costs associated with GCF standard; particularly for projects aiming at achieving climate information services.

### The AE states that the net present value (NPV) is USD 56.01 million and the economic interest rate (EIR) is 9 per cent, which falls within the 10 per cent threshold. The positive NPV and high EIR indicate that the project is likely to be economically viable. The economic feasibility of the project is therefore considered as a key element in the success of the project. It appears that if the benefits were reduced by 10 per cent, the resulting EIRR would be 96.2 per cent. Likewise, if the benefit streams are perturbed by a 10 per cent increase in project costs, the resulting EIRR would be 85.4 per cent. In both scenarios, the calculated present EIR falls within the 10 per cent threshold and therefore still exhibits the high economic feasibility of the project.


---
**Page 142**
---

## A further sensitivity analysis assumed a combination of increasing costs and declining benefits due to an input 10 per cent, which results in a revised NPV of USD 648 million and a revised EIRR of 79.4 per cent. Both these numbers indicate high economic returns even if all the uncertainties are considered. The sensitivity analysis also indicates that for every USD 10 invested in the project, a likely return will be in the order of USD 57, which again indicates an economically highly desirable project.

The project is well aligned with the priorities and strengths in the country.
However, the funding proposal illustrates that the current state of affairs of the network is rather poor. The project has not been able to secure any funds for the network yet. Once new financing will be dedicated to procuring weather data collecting equipment, and future O&M financing is assured, it is most likely that the data generating network will sustain and continue to deliver.

Initially, the project rightly focused on the equipment for data collection to support analysis of climate change impacts. However, the funding proposal submitted by the principal indicated the intention to integrate data from other projects (see FP109) and continue to gather data from other national institutions. The necessary institutional agreement has been forged, which provides a good basis for the project to be implemented in the manner proposed by the project.

The funding proposal is optimistic in terms of accommodating diversity and the volume of work within the stipulated timeframe. The inclusion of Component 4 is perhaps a demonstration of such optimism. Since Timor-Leste is starting almost from scratch on providing climate change information, it would be difficult to integrate data from other projects. Hence, the executing agency has to build capacity to integrate aspects related to information on hydrology and hydrometeorology in the project. This will be challenging but possible in the longer term. However, it would add challenges regarding overall implementation.

From the above analysis, the independent TAP is of the opinion that project efficiency and effectiveness is medium to high.
### Overall remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

On the basis of the above findings, the independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP171)

## Proposal name:
Enhancing early warning systems to build greater resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards in Timor-Leste

### Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

### Country/ies:
Democratic Republic of Timor-Leste

### Project/programme size:
Small

## Impact potential
UNEP welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment that the impact potential of the Project is high, which also highlights how its implementation will significantly respond to the climate information and early warning systems (CIWWS) needs and priorities of Timor-Leste identified in the baseline assessment and stakeholder consultations. This would in turn result in a more resilient society with better preparedness for climate change impacts.

The project aims to provide timely, accurate and actionable climate information for enhanced resilience to climate change and climate-related hazards. The project will also address the challenge in relation to the planned scope of work, the AEI of the opinion that the broad partnership approach is not sufficiently supported by a strong institutional framework at national institutions and beneficiaries to kick start implementation and deliver planned activities.

The transformative impact of the proposed comprehensive and integrated capacity building approach is expected to be significant. The project will build on UNEP’s regional technical expertise and complementing existing initiatives – is expected to accrue benefits to the entire population of Timor-Leste.

## Paradigm shift potential
The proposal takes a unique integrated technical, infrastructural, and institutional capacity building approach to establish multi-hazard, people-centered GEMS in Timor-Leste that will facilitate a transition to proactive, risk-informed planning, decision-making and policy transformation. The project will build on the successful implementation of climate-resilient community-based activities and South-South knowledge sharing with IP4T17, supporting replication and scaling up of these approaches.

Forecast-based Financing will provide an innovative mechanism for early action. UNEP notes that the concept is well aligned to the UNDP’s FBF pilot project in Timor-Leste, which was carefully designed where a financial framework specifically focused on the sustainable management of natural resources and climate change adaptation was established as a sustainable business model for delivery of continuous climate services by the DMCN. UNEP confirms that this approach is consistent with the UNDP’s FBF strategy and supports the UNEP/UNDP strategic partnership as a priority of both organizations to deliver synergies, demonstrate clear added value and ensure transformative multi-faceted action.

The GEF-funded programme “Enhancing Climate Information and Knowledge Services for resilience in 3 Island countries” (GEF-6) is currently under implementation. The project was approved at R2 on November 2019.


---
**Page 144**
---

# Sustainable development potential

UNEP agrees that the sustainable development potential of the proposed Project is high. The proposal offers significant environmental co-benefits. Actionable climate information would enhance the resilience and adaptive capacity of communities in Timor-Leste to conservation and addressing the climate-related drivers to biodiversity loss. Strengthened data availability, including through the use of remote sensing technologies, will improve water resource management. Community-based activities will have the overall aim of sensitizing communities on the importance of adaptation measures against climate-related hazards, including on environmental health, contributing to enhanced local ownership for environmental protection and resilient building of natural ecosystems.

# Needs of the recipient

UNEP agrees that the needs of the recipient are high relating to the provision of climate and early warning information. Timor-Leste is a vulnerable SIDS, LDC and newly independent country with limited capacity in terms of disaster risk management (DRM). The floods and landslides in April 2021, which affected over 35,000 households and claimed at least 41 lives. As the UNDP/UNFPA/UNICEF Joint Country Programme (JCP) 2018-2021 notes, “The exposure against climate-related hazards is greater than ever before.” The importance of improving national capacity for early warning systems and community capacity for disaster preparedness and response has been highlighted in the UNDP Timor-Leste National DRM Plan (May 2021). Result 1 addresses the urgent need for systemic institutional capacity building and will create a strong foundation for the implementation of the DRM Plan. It will also strengthen the intersectoral collaboration between the multiple actors in weather, water and climate services, as well as disaster risk reduction.

With regards to the ITAP concerns on how far DNICG would organize itself to absorb the added capacity within the project timeframe, please note that Timor-Leste is a RIMES Member State and will be able to benefit from the technical assistance provided by RIMES in providing ongoing technical and backup support to DNICG and all national stakeholders during and beyond project completion. Moreover, The Project will enable Timor-Leste to meet


---
**Page 145**
---

# Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) standards and therefore quality for ongoing assistance from the new Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFF)

## Country ownership

UNEPI agrees with the ITAP that Timor-Leste has expressed "inter necessity" for the Project, which is consistent with the country's National Adaptation Plan. The project has been identified as a priority project in Timor-Leste’s 2011 National Adaptation Plan. The project has been developed through a consultative process considering representatives and target beneficiaries from government ministries/departments, local communities, indigenous peoples, government ministries/departments, local NGOs, and international organizations. As such, UNFCCC and other relevant stakeholders have been involved in the development of the project's exit strategy, budgetary provisions and the institutional arrangements with reputable partners and ITAP would pave a way to sustain human resources and CIEWS in country.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

UNEPI agrees that the proposal indicates high economic feasibility. UNEPI considers that the approach taken leverages the technical expertise of regional and technical partners, who have been engaged at the request of the country based on their track record and expertise in Timor-Leste. The project will also leverage existing institutional capacity in Timor-Leste.

Considering the low baseline institutional, human and financial capacity to provide CIEWS in Timor-Leste, the Project employs a holistic capacity building approach, maximizing synergies and complementarities with other initiatives and projects already implemented in Timor-Leste (e.g. 4 in line with international best practices) and it is critical to ensure that CIEWS are effective to the fullest.

Given Timor-Leste’s high exposure and vulnerability to a range of climate-induced hazards (in the context of an LDC), the potential of the project to avert, minimize and address loss and damage from extreme weather events, including high VPH and ESRK, and application of best available technologies and/or best practices.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

UNEPI welcomes the ITAP’s overall positive assessment of the funding proposal and notes with gratitude that the panel has recommended approval for the project.

Further to the responses above, UNEPI reiterates the critical value to Timor-Leste of building national capacity to generate and use essential high resolution data and climate information that underpins effective decision-making. The project will provide a comprehensive package which would facilitate long-term resilience and adaptation to climate change, protecting both human lives as well as livelihoods in Timor-Leste. The project also provides an opportunity for Timor-Leste to play Timor-Leste plans for climate service information, early warning systems and disaster risk reduction mechanisms. The project provides a comprehensive and integrated package that aims at building-in country capacities from national to local level.

The SOFF is currently being operationalized and will be launched at COP24. It will provide ongoing technical and financial support to countries in the Global South.

Enhancing Early Warning Systems to build greater resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards in Timor-Leste

Annex B - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

1. Introduction: Relevance of Gender Mainstreaming for the Project

This Gender Assessment has been prepared as an input for the design of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding proposal by UN Environment Programme (UNEP), titled "Enhancing Early Warning Systems to build greater resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards in Timor-Leste". The project aims at building climate resilience and disaster preparedness capacities at national, sub-national and community level in Timor-Leste through the development of end-of-bend climate and oceans services and impact-based multi-hazard early warning systems. These systems will help communities that are most vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea level rise, increasing mean temperatures and extreme climatic events, including droughts, floods, landslides, and cyclones. The project also aims at reducing exposure to natural hazards such as flooding, droughts and landslides; dependence on climate-sensitive resources for livelihoods; and the vulnerability of women and girls in particular.

The Gender Development Index (GDI) is a direct measure of the gender gap in achievement of the three basic dimensions of human development: health, education, and standard of living. Timor-Leste is in GDI Group 5, which corresponds to countries with the lowest gender equality in human development. The country has one of the highest levels of malnutrition among children under five years old as measured by the Global Hunger Index (GHI), which indicates the prevalence of a serious level of hunger[1]. This is reflected in a very poor nutritional status of Timorese women and children.[2] In addition, physical, psychological, sexual and economic violence against women are widespread. These factors contribute to gender inequality across culture and class divisions.[3] Gender equality is a cross-cutting issue, with vulnerability to climate change intersecting power relations and other structural and place-based causes of inequality[4]. Furthermore, framing the problem as one of vulnerability rather than capacity can lead to an overemphasis on women's roles in building resilience of the population and achieving sustainable development.[5]

Gender inequalities have social, economic, political and cultural implications for individuals responding to climate change. These include unequal access to resources such as land, water, food, shelter and fuel; and the extent to which women and men are affected by and respond to climate change impacts[6]. Women and girls face multiple barriers in accessing information about climate change and its effects on their lives, factors including unequal power relations and structures, discriminatory laws and customs, and unequal access to a wide range of resources. Such factors influence adaptive capacity, resilience and participation in climate action.[7]

The IPCC Fifth Assessment Report highlights that women are not inherently vulnerable to climate change because they are more resilient than men[8]. However, the report also notes that gender intersects power relations and other structural and place-based causes of inequality[9]. Furthermore, framing the problem as one of vulnerability rather than capacity can lead to an overemphasis on women's roles in building resilience of the population and achieving sustainable development.[10] The IPCC Fifth Assessment Report highlights that women are not inherently vulnerable to climate change because they are more resilient than men. However, the report also notes that gender intersects power relations and other structural and place-based causes of inequality[9]. Furthermore, framing the problem as one of vulnerability rather than capacity can lead to an overemphasis on women's roles in building resilience of the population and achieving sustainable development.[10]

Without a full understanding of how climate change will affect different groups, the proposed design process would not adequately address the full range of vulnerabilities and issues present within a target population, resulting

# Methodology

## Gender Assessment

The gender assessment describes the gender context, gender issues (gender equality and gender equity), and gender vulnerabilities (gender relations, access and power) relevant to the project and gender opportunities (recommendations). The Assessment was informed by the following methodological steps:

- **Primary data collection**: Focus group discussions, key informant interviews, workshops, and questionnaire responses. Further details are provided in Appendix 1.
- **Comprehensive desk review of existing literature**, draft Gender Assessment Report prepared by WRI, and other relevant documents (e.g., World Bank Country Strategy Paper, Timor-Leste Project Document).
- The literature review was conducted on climate change adaptation, climate information and early warning systems, disaster risk reduction, and hydrometeorological systems in relation to gender and the role of women in decision-making. It also included reports from international organisations (United Nations Development Programme – UNDP; United States Agency for International Development – USAID; Asian Development Bank – ADB), journals (Climate Risk Management, Geography Compass, Journal of International Forum, etc.), and grey literature (government reports, policies and plans).
- The stakeholder consultations. literature review: gender analysis of the Feasibility Study and analysis of the project’s potential impact on women in relation to their access to resources; the socioeconomic and gender baseline (Section 3), the political, legal and institutional environment in relation to gender priorities (Section 4), gender roles, gaps and challenges in the five GEF thematic areas (Section 5); and an assessment of the project’s vulnerability to climate change vulnerabilities and adaptation needs (Section 6) in Timor-Leste.
- Based on the literature review and previous assessments, non-gender-responsive recommendations and interventions are to be incorporated with the intent to ensure that gender concerns are addressed, existing gender inequalities are not reinforced, and that greater resilience is achieved for women in Timor-Leste.

## Socioeconomic and Gender Baseline in Timor-Leste

### 3.1 Background

Timor-Leste is one of the world’s newest countries, having achieved independence in 2002 after a long history of colonial rule by Portugal (1769–1975) and Indonesia (1975–1999). The country has a total population of 1.324 million (51.8% male and 48.2% female), with the majority of the population living in rural areas (89.4%). There is a significant geographical divide, with rural men and women having lower education, employment, higher poverty, and worse living standards.


---
**Page 150**
---

The average annual population growth rate for 2015-2020 was 1.94%, which is the highest growth rate in South-East Asia (1.05% average) and significantly higher than the average for SIDS (0.91%). The reality of this growth is reflected in increased demands, including those related to demand for food, water, sanitation, and health education services.

In 2018, GNI per capita was reported as US$3,257 in GDP and was USD $2,681 billion with a population of just over 9.4 million people. The proportion of the population living below the poverty line has fallen from 50.4% in 2007 to 41.8% in 2014. However, considerable spatial and gender disparities exist. For example, 80% of the population lives in rural areas with a higher proportion of people in lower socioeconomic context; 56% of men participate in paid employment compared with 27% of women.

### Composite Indices

- **Human Development Index (HDI)**

The HDI is a composite measure to assess long-term progress in three basic dimensions of human development: a long and healthy life, access to knowledge and a decent standard of living. Timor-Leste's HDI value for 2018 was 0.626, which ranks it 141 out of 189 countries. This is lower than the average HDI score in South-East Asia (0.735) but higher than the average for SIDS (0.5).

- **Inequality-adjusted Human Development Index (IHDI)**

The Inequality-adjusted Human Development Index (IHDI) adjusts the HDI value according to inequalities in the country relating to health, education and income. The "loss" in human development due to inequality is 18%, which means that Timor-Leste's actual level of human development is lower than its potential.

- **Gender Development Index (GDI)**

The GDI is defined as a ratio of the female to male HDI. The GDI measures gender inequalities in achievement and opportunity, taking into account differences between men and women in their command over economic resources. The 2018 sex-disaggregated HDI values for Timor-Leste are 0.695 (for males) and 0.473 (for females), which is the lowest GDI score of any country, thus it is in GDI Group 5, corresponding to countries with the lowest equity in HDI achievements.

- **Gender Inequality Index (GII)**

The GII is a composite measure that reflects gender-based inequalities in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and economic activity. It can be interpreted as the "loss" in human development due to inequality between female and male achievements in the three GII dimensions. The high GII score has precluded calculation of the GII for Timor-Leste, thus highlighting the existing data gap in this area.

- **Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI)**

The MPI identifies multiple overlapping deprivations that individuals face in their health, education and standard of living. It is based on a set of indicators that are designed to capture the multidimensional nature of poverty. Based on the deprivation score thresholds, people are classified as vulnerable to multidimensional poverty, multidimensionally poor, or in severe multidimensional poverty. In 2018, Timor-Leste's MPI value was 35%, which means that 35% of the population is living in multidimensional poverty. The proportion of the population living below the poverty line (41.8%) and the proportion of people living on less than $1.90 a day (15.2%) are both higher than the MPI value, implying that people living above the income poverty line may still be deprived with regards to health, education and/or standard of living.


---
**Page 151**
---

### 3.4 Health and well-being

Life expectancy at birth remains the overall mortality level of a population in Timor-Leste. The life expectancy for men is 70 years and for women it is 71.1 years for women, which are 3.1 and 3.6 years less than the global average for males and females respectively. High maternal and infant mortality rates are a sign of poor health care services and limited access to reproductive health issues with accessing healthcare services, particularly relating to maternal and child healthcare.

Traditional beliefs and limited economic opportunities encourage women to marry and reproduce at a relatively young age. Lack of education, especially among women, limits their ability to access healthcare, clean water and adequate sanitation facilities increases the risk of maternal and infant deaths. As a result, despite the fact that fertility rate has been rapidly declining, fertility is much higher in rural areas and amongst women with no education.

Timor-Leste has made considerable progress in reducing its maternal mortality ratio (MMR) from one of the highest in the world at 100 per 100,000 live births to 2003 to 74 per 100,000 live births. Despite this progress, Timor-Leste is still a MMR more than three times that of the average for the East Asia and Pacific region


---
**Page 152**
---

## Work and economic empowerment

In Timor-Leste, there is a highly gendered division of responsibilities with regards to work and labour, which is reflected in women's lower levels of values, norms and practices. Women are half as likely as men to participate in the labour force (27% of women compared with 56% of men),\textsuperscript{48} with the largest gender gap observed among rural women who work on their own land or in small-scale agricultural farms but household production activities are often not counted as labour force participation. Women's limited access to productive resources, such as land and credit, further constrain their engagement in the labour market.\textsuperscript{49} Some studies suggest that women in Timor-Leste work an average of 10 hours per day compared with men who work on average 8 hours a day,\textsuperscript{50} although women are often assigned a monetary value. Permission from their husbands to undertake paid work is often only granted if temporary arrangements are made to cover their household responsibilities.\textsuperscript{51}

This represents a significant gender gap in terms of economic empowerment, with men's MMR being 27% and women's at 38%,\textsuperscript{52} respectively; however, this is predominantly due to many Timorese being engaged in vulnerable or informal employment.\textsuperscript{53} Vulnerable employment is characterized by low levels of social protection, poor working conditions, lack of access to training and education opportunities, and a lack of voice at the workplace. Women are more likely than men to be employed in vulnerable employment and are therefore more likely to be affected by economic cycles and lack formal work arrangements.\textsuperscript{54} Vulnerable employment disproportionately affects women in rural areas; 87% of rural women are engaged in informal or vulnerable employment, compared with only 60% of urban women. The gender gap is high amongst rural men (78% versus 37% of urban males).\textsuperscript{55}

Men are the main income providers in Timor-Leste, with only 24% of household income assigned to women.\textsuperscript{56} Indeed, 80% of working women do not receive any payment for their work, which often involves unpaid domestic and care work.\textsuperscript{57}

Microfinance is a promising means to enable the poor, especially rural people, to borrow small amounts of capital at reasonable interest to support income generation (e.g. buying stock or investing in machinery) and improve their livelihoods.11 In Timor-Leste, microfinance has been used by women’s groups since 2005. Around 5% of the population aged 15-64 in Timor-Leste, with more women than men benefiting due to several factors including lack of access to credit and financial services.

Promoting the economic empowerment of women is essential for the realisation of women’s rights, as well as for the achievement of broader development goals such as economic growth, poverty reduction and improved health outcomes.12

### 3.6 Governance, influence and decision making

In Timor-Leste, traditional social and cultural norms assign strict gender roles for men and women, which often leads to discriminatory practices against women.13 At the local level, women have been largely excluded from political participation in elections since independence.

– 98% of the 442 suco and 2,350 aldeia (hamlet) chiefs were men,14 meaning that women had almost no voice in decision making at this level. In 2017, a law was passed to ensure greater representation for women as Hamlet Chiefs or women’s representatives to village councils.

Despite this progress, significant cultural shift is required in the social norms that govern decision making within the family and community. Women need to be encouraged to speak up more freely about their views with confidence and the perception of not being listened to limit women’s participation in public planning processes.15 It is also important to change the norms in which women are expected to be subordinate to men, to be shy and not express their opinions.

At the national level, Timor-Leste has made significant progress towards achieving gender equality in representation at all levels of government. Women now hold 20% of seats in parliament (13 out of 65), with 25 out of the 65 parliamentary seats (38%) held by women. This progress has been aided by the 2006 Law on the Election of National Parliament, which introduces a quota that requires one in every four candidates to be female.

However, women still hold only about 17% of ministerial positions and are represented at only 18% of decision-making roles at the highest levels of government – for example, ministers, vice ministers and deputy ministers. Only two out of ten states held by women.16

### 3.7 Land and assets ownership

Land is one of the most fundamental productive assets in Timor-Leste, given that over 80% of its rural population is reliant on agriculture and natural resources for their livelihoods. Women’s rights to land are highly contested due to a lack of legal recognition of women’s rights to land and property, as well as due to existing patriarchal inheritance systems17 Women are also excluded from land management decision-making processes, including in communal areas where women inherit land. Lack of land rights is one of the main reasons why women are unable to access credit or other financial services.

3.8 Gender-based violence

Violence against women and girls in Timor-Leste is widespread and cuts across the country’s income, culture and ethnicity. Domestic violence is the most common form of violence against women in Timor-Leste, although women are also subject to non-partner rape and sexual assault, sexual harassment and trafficking. Women who have experienced domestic violence report a range of physical consequences affecting their lives, and 59% of women aged 15-49 who have been in a relationship have experienced physical and/or sexual violence by an intimate partner or ex-partner at some point during the relationship (UNFPA, 2018). Of those who have experienced abuse, less than one in four seek help and only 4% of women who experience domestic violence report it to the police. Reasons for not reporting to the police include fear of repercussions, family pressures, economic dependence and self-blame.

Timor-Leste adopted the Law against Domestic Violence (LADV) in 2010, which defined and criminalised domestic violence as a crime under the Criminal Code. The Victim Protection Unit (VPU), a special unit of the national police force, is responsible for receiving and investigating complaints of domestic violence. In 2018, 35% of cases were referred to the VPU and 47% of these cases have since been brought to the formal justice system. However, many domestic violence crimes continue to be addressed through customary justice. Hearings in the customary justice system are commonly held in a woman’s home or at her village chief’s house, where she is often not represented by a lawyer. Nevertheless, there has been an increase in overall public awareness that domestic violence is a crime and that it can be reported to the police.

The 2015 Criminal Code amendments and regulations adopted since 2016 state that domestic violence is a crime requiring investigation in the formal justice system. The law also provides for the establishment of a national anti-domestic violence unit, which points to the role that customary justice systems can play in socialising formal law and acting in favour of condemnation and prevention of such acts at the local level.

### 4.1 Gender-related Political, Legal and Institutional Environment in Timor-Leste

#### Policy environment

The Government of Timor-Leste is committed to the promotion of gender equality under its Constitution, which protects against discrimination based on gender (Article 16) and calls for equality between men and women in economic, social and cultural life (Article 17).

Gender equality as a cross-cutting priority is also reflected in the Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan 2011-2030, which focuses on achieving gender equality goals through:

* Gender mainstreaming systems and mechanisms.
* Gender-sensitive policies at national level and local level.
* Strategies to raise awareness on gender disparities and issues.
* Empowerment of women through livelihood support and education programmes.
* Increased employment of women in the civil service.
* Political training and leadership programmes for increased participation of women in decision-making.


---
**Page 155**
---

### Institutions

The Secretary of State for Equality and Inclusion (SEI) is the overarching national machinery for gender equality. The SEI was formerly known as the Secretary of State for Support and Socio-Economic Promotion of Women (SESPE), before which it was the Secretary of State for the Promotion of Equality (SEP).


---
**Page 156**
---

## Civil society organisations

- **Assistência Legisal de Feto no Labarik (ALFLA)**: ALFLA supports pregnant women and their children to access a fair and formal justice system utilisation through legal aid, legal education and advocacy. It is the only organisation in Timor-Leste in a position to provide legal assistance on civil matters, including cases related to the implementation of Timor-Leste’s first ever law against human trafficking.

- **Fundusana Aloa (Aloa Foundation)**: Fundusana Aloa is a non-profit non-governmental organisation that seeks to nurture women leaders and young people in Timor-Leste. It was established to raise awareness of the widespread sexual violence against women and girls during the militia attacks of September 1999. When sexual violence remains a key issue for the Foundation, it also works on other issues such as health, education and literacy; advocacy; and economic development. Fundusana Aloa is the lead civil society organisation in Timor-Leste to implement Timor-Leste’s first ever law against human trafficking and raising awareness of its content.

- **Covilinha Community Centre**


---
**Page 157**
---

# Covalima Community Centre (CCC)

The Covalima Community Centre (CCC) supports women’s groups with a focus on strengthening livelihoods and leadership, as well as advocacy at district level in Covalima. CCC provides women with training, technical support, and access to credit and support to identify and connect to markets in order to enhance their income-generation opportunities.

## FOKUPERES

FOKUPERES was established in 1997 to address gender-based violence and human rights violations against women and children in Timor-Leste. It is a community-based not-for-profit organisation whose work has expanded to include victim services, non-gender based early childhood development, and economic empowerment.

## Rede Feto

Rede Feto is a network of women’s organisations in Timor-Leste that aim to promote gender equality and women's employment through advocacy, networking and capacity building. Rede Feto was established in 2000 during the first Timor-Leste National Women’s Congress and leads the Congregation of Women’s Organisations (COWO). The network is made up of 15 member organisations that come from across Timor-Leste and include key players in delivering services to women on critical issues of concern.

Rede Feto coordinates social mobilisation, government implementation of the CEDAW recommendations, provides training for the Government and NGOs on gender issues, and represents women’s interests at national level. The network is led by a steering committee that includes representatives from each member organisation.

### 5. Gender Roles, Gaps and Challenges in the GFPCS Priority Sectors

#### 5.1 Agriculture and food security

Agriculture is the main economic activity in Timor-Leste. Women play an important role as cultivators of staple crops such as rice and cassava. In 2018, 68% of employed women were self-employed as farmers, compared with 62% of men. Despite the dominant role of agriculture in the livelihoods of Timorese people, agricultural productivity in Timor-Leste is among the lowest in the East Asia and Pacific region.

The gender gap in land ownership is a major factor that contributes to low agricultural productivity for women farmers, with up to a 31% gain when adjusting for land size within the same sub-district. The gender gap is almost entirely due to women’s disproportionately lower access to land, limited access to credit and inputs, and limited access to extension services.

Given that subsistence agriculture is predominantly the responsibility of Timorese women, their lower productivity of female farmers has significant implications for self-sufficiency and food security. Many households in rural areas are unable to meet their basic nutritional needs due to low agricultural productivity. Resultant low levels of disposable income to offset deficits purchased food compound the issues of subsistence and food security.

#### 5.2 Disaster risk management

The National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) is responsible for providing disaster risk management coordination and technical support in Timor-Leste. It acts as the country’s centre for disaster risk reduction (DRR) activities, which include collecting information, monitoring overviews developments


---
**Page 158**
---

and proposing development for incorporation into the national disaster risk reduction system. The NDMD is amongst others responsible for developing DRR strategies, including preparedness and response plans and assisting in the implementation of these plans at all levels of government in conjunction with relevant partners.

At the municipality (formerly district) level, the District Disaster Coordinator (DDC) is responsible for disaster preparedness planning, response operations and DRR, and is assisted by a District Disaster Management Committee (DDMC). At the sub-district level, the Sub-District Administrator (SDA) is responsible for disaster preparedness planning, response operations and DRR. The SDA is required to within each suct (village), the Suco Chief and village leaders (elders, traditional leaders and village youth) are responsible for disaster preparedness planning and DRR activities. Requests for assistance should be passed through the village chief to the SDA.

The Timor-Leste Red Cross Society (Cru Vermelha de Timor-Leste – CVTL) was highly recognized as an auxiliary organization of the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) with a network of 13 branches across all 13 municipalities in Timor-Leste. As an auxiliary to the Government, CVTL, through its disaster preparedness and response activities, contributes significantly towards disaster, including in the provision of emergency water and sanitation, hygiene promotion, protection shelter, non-food items and livelihood support. CVTL participates in national emergency planning and national disaster risk reduction strategies. The CVTL is committed to strengthening the disaster preparedness and response capacities of nation and district DMCCs and to enable them to deliver a coherent and rapid response.

Integration of a gender perspective in the activities of CVTL lies in the humanitarian mandate of the Red Cross – to prevent and alleviate human suffering without discrimination. The Gender Policy of CVTL sets out its commitment to ensure that all disaster preparedness, response and recovery activities are implemented according to their different needs with the input and equal participation of men and women at all levels within the organization.

Put in place institutional procedures that ensure that the needs of boys, girls, men and women are met equitably in disaster response, vulnerability reduction and the provision of health and other services:

• Formulate measures to ensure that gender-specific vulnerabilities and capacities of men and women are systematically identified and addressed;

• Ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into all requirements for needs assessment and program planning and gender analysis is integrated into program design, delivery, monitoring and evaluation;

• Design strategies for capacity building in gender mainstreaming as part of the institutional development program, with special attention to staff training on gender analysis skills;

• Ensure that reporting and accountability mechanisms for gender mainstreaming in activities and results are established. This includes the establishment of indicators, targets, responsibilities and actions to enable the full participation of women and men on an equal and meaningful basis;

• Develop forms to analyse gender use for all CVTL staff in ensuring equal male and female participation in community projects.

• Ensure that each program has a specific activity line in responding to gender issues.105

### Energy

Energy consumption in Timor-Leste relies on the use of imported fossil fuels, which are used for electricity generation and transportation fuel, and local biomass.106 Approximately 35% of households in Timor-Leste do not have access to electricity.107 Moreover, the Government has acknowledged that one fifth of

## Water

Water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) is a national priority in Timor-Leste. Lack of access to clean water and sanitation facilities remains a significant health risk, particularly in rural areas. 75% of rural households do not have access to safe drinking water from piped or borehole sources, such as unprotected well or spring water. Using water from these sources increases the risk of transmission of water-borne diseases.

Gender inequality is also prevalent; it is predominantly women who collect and use water and provide healthcare and hygiene for the household. Almost 40% of the population lives at least 30 minutes away from their nearest water source, with only half of those having access to improved sanitation facilities. Since 2010, around 780 community water management groups – group maneiga familiasa – have been set up in districts (hameis), of which approximately one third are the members are women. Data from a clean water and sanitation survey conducted by UNICEF in 2014 shows that women represent only 35% of their representation.

**Page 160**

# Gender and Climate Change in Timor-Leste

## 6.1 Gender issues related to climate change impacts and risks

Climate change and extreme disasters have a differential impact on women and men due to the prevalence of gender inequalities that affect the ability of individuals and communities to adapt and respond to climate impacts.

In the stakeholder consultation workshop conducted by WMO in November 2017, the following vulnerability-related events were identified as priority hazards in Timor-Leste:

- **Floods**: Loss of lives and properties are key concerns expressed by women participants. NGO representatives reported that women and children (especially young girls) are among the most vulnerable groups during a flood event. Lack of strength or agility may reduce their ability to quickly remove themselves from danger zones. Furthermore, women, youth and children often do not have access to information on early warning systems.

- **Drought**: Timor-Leste frequently suffers from the impacts of extreme and extended drought. In 2015, the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) event caused a severe drought in the country. The Humanitarian Partnership Agreement (HPA) assessment report on the 2015 drought found that across nine districts, 38% of households were food insecure and 47% had experienced water shortages reporting suffering from the long dry season compared to 57% of men. This is particularly in relation to women who are responsible for household water collection and management.

- **Landslides**: High resolution satellite imagery has recorded 123 landslides in Timor-Leste, a natural resource loss assessment conducted by RMDS in 2015. Susceptibility to landslides is influenced by heavy rainfall, poor land use practices, geology and lithology. Women, elderly people, children and people with disabilities often spend most of the day at home and may be more exposed to landslides than men.

- **Other**: Other gender-related risks include lack of access to information on early warning systems, limited access to financial resources for quick rehabilitation, and poor resource management. Inconsistent or anecdotal trigger information can be costly advice for farmers. More reliable early warnings should be available for farmers in specific regions in a user-friendly format that caters to different users in rural areas.

## 6.2 Gender issues accessing climate information and early warning services

The key gender issues identified by the analysis in relation to access to climate information and early warning services are summarised below:

- **Limited access**: Women have limited access to climate information and early warning systems, which can be attributed to a lack of awareness about these services. This is particularly true for women living in remote areas or who do not speak the national language.

- **Gender-based violence**: Women may face increased risks of gender-based violence during extreme weather events such as floods and landslides due to their limited mobility and access to resources.

- **Limited decision-making power**: Women often have limited decision-making power within households, which can impact their ability to access climate information and early warning services. This is particularly true for women living in rural areas or who are economically dependent on men.

- **Lack of financial resources**: Women may face a lack of financial resources to invest in climate-resilient practices such as constructing toilets and purchasing soap. This can limit their ability to adapt to climate change impacts.

# Social, economic, cultural, political and institutional factors contribute to men and women having differential access to climate information and early warning services.

Climate awareness is generally unevenly distributed across Timor-Leste. There is no systematic early warning system for hydrometeorological hazards. Traditional knowledge is used by men to monitor disaster risks such as flooding. Women are often secondary actors and receivers of information, particularly in rural areas where women have limited access to education and media.

Women's economic roles are also gendered. Women earn less than men and are more likely to be employed in low-paying jobs or informal sectors. Women have lower levels of education and literacy rates compared with men. Therefore, women must rely on men to communicate early warning messages to each household, via personal networks and social ties.

Discriminatory cultural and social norms may limit women's control and right to use and safeguard assets and resources against climate change impacts.

Women's rights on own land are highly restricted and traditional gender roles mean that they are also excluded from decision-making in disaster risk reduction. Women also report that they do not take disaster risk reduction actions until they receive the early warning message from men.

Access to climate information and early warnings within each gender group differs according to age, socioeconomic factors, geographic location and disability.

Early warnings do not reach remote, rural communities due to absent or inadequate observations and communication systems. Women are less likely than men to have access to technology-based systems to transmit and receive information; and lack of capacity of information providers to communicate information tailored for the needs of women with disabilities, particularly those living in remote areas with disability.

Women and men have different information requirements due to gender-based divisions of labour, roles, responsibilities, mobility and socio-cultural norms.

In Timor-Leste, rural women and men often share responsibilities agricultural work; but domestic and reproductive work is entirely the responsibility of women. Different perceptions of climate risk reflect these differences in responsibilities. Women are more concerned about the potential impacts on health, education and access to markets, whereas men are more concerned about damage to crops and livestock. To ensure that early warning messages are effective for both genders it must be communicated in an actionable format targeted to the priority needs of the end-user.

Women and men have different capacities to understand and use climate and early warning information.

Lower literacy levels of women in Timor-Leste limit their ability to read and understand climate and early warning information. Lack of translation into local languages, overly technical presentations and lack of relevance of straightforward; practical advice further present barriers to effective communication.


### Recommendations for a Gender Transformative Approach

Climate information and early warning services have significant potential to reduce risk and vulnerability but only if they are gender-sensitive. Failure to address gender inequalities will reinforce if information products and services fail to understand and effectively target the varying needs of different gender groups. Therefore, it is essential that climate change adaptation strategies include transformative actions to support a paradigm shift in climate information, early warnings and disaster risk management in Timor-Leste. Such actions will increase the effectiveness of Project interventions, whilst also contributing towards achieving gender equality.


---
**Page 162**
---

simultaneously providing an entry point or opportunity to advance or demonstrate gender equality in action. The use of "gender-sensitive", "gender-responsive" and "gender-transformative" terminology in this Assessment is consistent with the definitions provided by the World Health Organization (WHO)10 and The John Hopkins University,7 which are as follows:

* Gender-sensitive programming refers to programmes where gender norms, roles and inequalities have been considered, but no specific actions or measures have been taken to address them. Such programmes may not necessarily have been taken.
* Gender-responsive programming refers to programmes where gender norms, roles and inequalities have been considered, and measures have been taken to actively address them. Such programmes go beyond raising sensitivity and awareness and actually do something about gender-related issues.
* Gender-transformative programming refers to programmes where gender norms, roles and inequalities have been considered, and measures have been taken to actively challenge them, promoting equality between women and men. Such programmes aim at reducing or eliminating inequities between persons of different genders. Such programmes go beyond gender-responsive action by challenging the underlying causes of gender inequality.

The recommended actions that will be undertaken in the Project are outlined in the following sections.

### 7.1 Mainstream gender in climate information, early warnings and disaster risk management

The first step in delivering gender transformative outcomes is the acknowledgement that gender is an integral consideration in disaster risk management, climate resilience building and adaptation. This recognition should be reflected at all levels of project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation assumptions, which can simultaneously lead to marginalisation of women and gender minorities, and identification of opportunities for mainstreaming gender.

Throughout Project development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation, proactive effort is required to reach out to, partner with, and listen to the voices of marginalised groups. In addition, it is essential to recognize that gender equality is not a separate issue but an integral part of all other issues. A truly intersectional approach is critical to ensure that the interaction of gender with other socially excluding factors such as age, ethnicity, race, religion, sexual orientation, language, disability, marital status and sexuality, as well as lack of political rights or social recognition.

### 7.2 Ensure meaningful participation of women

Participation is a human right and a core element of rights-based approaches to addressing discrimination and marginalization. It is also essential for the successful and equal participation of women and gender minorities, both as an essential factor in protecting women's rights as well as ensuring effective and sustainable climate change adaptation strategies. The Project will ensure that all women are included and empowered which will promote meaningful participation of women – in which women are present in decision-making fora and have the agency and voice to effect change - from local to national level. Meaningful participation of women is critical for their empowerment, as well as for the success of the Project.

Capacity building is a critical enabling factor for meaningful participation and is central to women's empowerment. The Project will support this target group by providing training in gender equality roles and responsibilities – with training and workshops scheduled to accommodate limitations to women’s mobility and work and household responsibilities. While the Project places particular emphasis on capacity building at the community level, meaningful participation of women will be concurrently angaged at clinics and


---
**Page 163**
---

# National levels – for example, in sector mainstreaming activities and development of the National Framework for Climate Services

This approach is designed to broaden the gender-responsive impact of the Project by considering the different needs of women and men at national level and the nature of community-based interventions.

Participatory approaches should also be promoted in order to encourage transformative change in gender dynamics. Gender equality and gender inequalities cannot be resolved solely by responding to the differential needs of women and men. Working collaboratively with men as well as women will be critical in this regard. The use of participatory methods, such as focus group discussions and community assessments will facilitate improved understanding of the structural inequalities and power dynamics that influence women’s and men’s vulnerability to climate change.

The gender analysis should also consider how different groups are affected by climate change and support an enabling environment for marginalised groups to voice their needs and priorities. Women and men will be encouraged to participate in, and be actively engaged in the development and implementation of community-based early warning systems (EWS). Other intersectionsalities such as age, ability and socio-economic group should also be considered.

### 7.3 Consider gender norms, behaviour patterns and information preferences in dissemination and communication

Gender differences affect the processing and dissemination of coherent climate information and risk warnings to the general population. Early warnings that lack a gender perspective can exacerbate the negative impacts on women and men. Women are often more vulnerable than men to climate change impacts because their behavioural patterns and communication preferences have not been accounted for, or they are not given sufficient space in decision-making processes.

The following actions will be undertaken as relevant to enhance gender responsiveness in dissemination and communication:

- Ensure that relevant frameworks and climate information products, services and tools are simplified and made available in Tetum or other local languages;
- Develop a gender-responsive communications strategy to ensure the dissemination of understandable messages that take into account the needs of women and men across different ages, abilities, geographies and socioeconomic groups;
- Educate women and men on how warnings will be disseminated, which sources are reliable and how they can access information in case of an emergency or when a warning is received;
- Engage with suco (village) leaders as key contacts to ensure the communication of climate and early warning information to their communities;
- Include women and men in all communication channels to disseminate climate and risk management information and enhance their learning capacity;
- Identify strategies to utilise and integrate traditional knowledge of vulnerable women and men into early warning systems.

The above actions will also identify local information channels and traditional knowledge as valuable means to improve communication at the community level. Local information channels can extend the reach of early warning systems (EWS) to facilitate that information is received at the last mile. Integrating traditional knowledge in EWS can help to build trust between service providers and end users. The IPCC AR5 Report highlights that the integration of local knowledge with scientific knowledge systems and practices with contemporary practices increases the effectiveness of adaptation.


---
**Page 164**
---

7.4 Tailor preparedness and response actions to the differential needs and capacities of vulnerable groups

Women are often categorised merely as victims, which overlooks the fact that women can be powerful agents of change.168 Whilst gender inequalities may increase women’s vulnerability to disasters, it should be recognised that women have different but valuable knowledge, skills, experience and coping mechanisms. It is therefore important for disaster risk management (DRM) practitioners to identify and integrate these unique capacities of women and gender minorities into disaster risk management strategies.

- Develop public awareness and education campaigns tailored to the specific needs and concerns of different gender groups;
- Include gender differentiated variables in hazard and risk maps and use these to develop disaster preparedness and response plans;
- Analyse gender-differentiated responses to previous disasters and incorporate gender-sensitive lessons learned into future capacity building strategies;
- Engage women and gender minorities in DRM capacity building interventions;
- Implement a community-based early warning system (EWS) as parallel with a top-down EWS to reinforce the value of traditional knowledge and as a back-up system to an automated EWS;
- Ensure that Forecast-based Financing actions are gender-responsive and that women and men have equal access to resources.

7.5 Promote equitable power, decision-making and access to resources

Equitable decision-making at all levels supports the development of adaptive capacity and climate resilience that benefits entire communities. Evidence shows that women’s inclusion in positions of leadership is associated with better disaster risk management outcomes than those of men or those of the wider community.169 Active engagement of women and marginalised individuals in disaster risk reduction (DRR) processes can lead to more effective solutions, as these groups have the agency to define their own need and determine potential solutions.170 Such approaches are critical for ensuring that DRR is inclusive and equitable.

In order to fully support women’s empowerment in decision-making processes, targeted resources, knowledge products and training should be provided, particularly at the local level. In addition, the Project should focus on strategies that enhance women’s resilience and strengthen their access to the following livelihood assets:

- **Human capital** – Provide education and training of women to increase their employability and participation in salaried and non-voluntary work.
- **Social capital** – Strengthen and support local women’s networks and groups and promote equal representation in local organisations and community meetings.
- **Natural capital** – Promote access to and ownership of land, and active engagement in decision making related to natural resource management.
- **Physical capital** – Ensure equal access to information, transport and communication assets.
- **Financial capital** – Promote women’s livelihoods through securing access to water, irrigation and agricultural production.

7.6 Minimise the risk of Gender-Based Violence

The risk of increasing gender-based violence (GBV) can be minimised through adopting a gender-sensitive approach to early warning systems (EWS). Based on awareness and understanding of the specific ways in which gender relates to EWS in Timor-Leste and the differential impacts of EWS on gender groups, the Project should ensure that disaster preparedness, response, recovery, and resilience-building activities are informed by the needs, concerns, and capabilities of different gender groups. Where possible, the Project should consult with local GBV service providers and protection services in the development of EWS and related awareness raising initiatives.

The Project should follow Good Practices as defined by the EU-funded Global Crisis response Support Programme (2017).

### 1) Risk Knowledge: Risk assessments should include core questions on what gender-related factors contribute to specific groups of women, men, girls and boys being at greater risk during different types of crisis.

### 2) Monitoring and Warning Service: The Project should assess which specific gender groups have access to which types of observational information and how each group can be leveraged for this information. This will help in the development of a more effective monitoring system.

### 3) Dissemination and Communication: Gender and age differences related to communication of warnings should be assessed and a strategy developed that takes these into account.

### 4) Preparedness and Response: Women, men, girls and boys should not be considered as potential victims or responders rather than just as vulnerable groups. Project planning should take this factor into account and women at the community level should be engaged with to identify what factors need to be addressed in order for their safety needs are also taken care of during different types of crisis and in the post-crisis recovery period.

### B. Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) forms the basis for operationalising the results and recommendations of the Gender Assessment presented in the preceding sections. It contains specific gender-sensitive and responsive elements to be integrated in the Project design and during the implementation of Project activities, in order to ensure that the Project is effective in achieving its objectives. The GAP also provides a framework for monitoring and reporting on progress towards gender-transformative actions that shift the focus from outputs to outcomes—emphasising the effectiveness of climate information and early warning systems (EWS) for marginalised gender groups and improving gender equality within and across the EWS.

The GAP is being developed in close collaboration with key stakeholders. The Gender Expert will be responsible for mainstreaming gender throughout the Project, ensuring quality control, and facilitating a gender-responsive and, where possible, gender-transformative approach to implementation. The Gender Expert will be a key member of the technical working group for EWS and, in this regard, will be responsible for ensuring that gender needs are integrated into the EWS and disaster risk management (DRM) components of the Project. The Gender Expert will also work closely with the Chief Technical Advisor to mainstream gender in the National Framework for Climate Services and in the establishment of climate services for health.

The Gender Expert will work closely with the Project Management Unit (PMU) to ensure that gender aspects are fully incorporated through the Project implementation phases, including project design, planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. The PMU will provide technical guidance and expertise for gender-responsive decision-making, and to support gender mainstreaming by contributing to Technical TORs, as required.

In addition, as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project, UNEP’s PMU will provide an advisory and quality assurance role throughout Project implementation. UNEP will have a key role in ensuring that gender is integrated into all aspects of the Project design and implementation. The Gender Expert will be responsible for supporting the integration of gender, social and economic dimensions into UNEP’s work, in both programmematic and wider organisational priorities. UNEP is committed to promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment as a key element of its mission and strategy. This includes ensuring that gender mainstreaming is embedded within decision making and programming.

At the start of Project implementation, the Gender Expert will conduct an in-depth stakeholder consultation workshop with women (including indigenous women) and other vulnerable and/or marginalised groups (such as youth, elderly people, widows and people with disabilities), which will build on the gender analysis undertaken during the Gender Assessment. This will be a guiding principle throughout the Project.


---
**Page 167**
---

consultations conducted in development of the proposed Project. The workshop will primarily focus on engagement with community-level users of climate information and early warning services, including representatives of scouts (illawara) where the Project will be implemented, women's groups, community-based organisations and a cross-section of other stakeholders involved in the project. The Gender Expert will also conduct additional stakeholder consultations as required to extend the reach and accessibility of the stakeholder consultations and maximise representation of the most vulnerable groups.

The scope of the in-depth stakeholder consultations will include: i) assessment/analysis of women's access to and control over resources (ie, financial, working, land, water, food security, health, disaster risk reduction, water and energy); ii) analysis of the opportunities, challenges and barriers for women-headed households, indigenous women, elderly people, youth, people with disabilities and other vulnerable groups. The objectives of the in-depth stakeholder consultations are outlined below:

- Identify opportunities to strengthen the enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in national service providers who are involved in the Project;
- Explore the gender roles of men and women in areas where the Project will be implemented, their access to and control over resources, including gender-based violence;
- Engage with existing women's groups and community networks that could support the active engagement of women and other vulnerable groups in community-based interventions.

Using a gender analysis framework, explore how the overall Project could contribute to poverty reduction in the community, empowerment, increased awareness of human rights, access to justice, meaningful participation in governance and decision-making processes, and education. Based on the in-depth consultations, the Gender Expert will recommend concrete actions to be undertaken that address barriers (e.g., time, money and literacy constraints, and assestment issues) that have limited women's engagement in the past (particularly those related to decision-making processes and decisions) and barriers to their participation in the Project. The Gender Expert will also identify opportunities for increasing the representation of women in the Project, including a greater number of women technicians and women that are expected to participate in the Climate and Health Working Group. The consultations will also facilitate establishment of a gender focal point within the Project.

The Gender Expert will additionally conduct regular stakeholder consultations throughout the Project to ensure continuous assessment of the gender responsiveness of implementation and collection of lessons learned. This will include an annual stakeholder consultation workshop, as well as additional consultations with women's groups and other stakeholders involved in the project. To ensure that the Project is responsive to the needs and engagement with the most vulnerable and/or marginalised groups, the Gender Expert will engage with representatives of all stakeholders with a role in the climate services value chain (e.g., government, civil society organisations, private sector) throughout the Project implementation phase.

Project interventions have been effective in addressing the gender gaps between women and men; and changing behaviours and practices to increase resilience and reduce vulnerability. Moreover, the Gender Expert will consult regularly with women's groups and organisations representing women's interests operating in Timor-Leste, including those involved in climate change adaptation and disaster risk management programs. This could include ALFIsa, Aloá Community Centre, Covalima Community Centre, FOKUPERS and Rede Feto, which are detailed in section 4.3.

8.3 Monitoring, Evaluation and Reporting

The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan (Annex 11) will be refined on the basis of the inception phase in-depth stakeholder consultations to design gender-specific indicators and collect associated gender-disaggregated data and information that will monitor the gender-responsiveness of Project implementation—including the quality, timeliness, and relevance of the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be responsible for providing guidance on receiving feedback from the Gender Expert. The PSC will include representatives from women’s groups and/or women-focused civil society organizations.

During implementation of the Project, qualitative assessments will be conducted on the gender-responsive benefits that can be directly associated with the Project. These will be incorporated in the Annual Performance Reports and Mid-Term and Terminal Evaluations. Indicators to quantify the achievement of Project outcomes will include: a) Number of women accessing financial services; b) Number of women receiving support and early warning systems; employment in jobs created by the Project, and participation in training and capacity building interventions. Monitoring will also include the number of women participating in gender mainstreaming activities undertaken by the Project, which will be facilitated by the in-depth stakeholder consultations conducted by the Gender Expert.

The Project will undertake a gender mainstreaming approach throughout its development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation to ensure that the Project is fully aligned with national policies and strategies. This includes:

- The Constitution of the Democratic Republic of Timor-Leste, which protects against discrimination on the grounds of gender (Article 16) and states that women and men have the same rights and duties in all areas of political, economic, social, cultural and family life (Article 17).148
- Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan (2011-2030), which includes the following strategies for achieving gender equality goals: a) Gender mainstreaming in national policies and strategies; b) Gender-sensitive budgeting at all levels of government, including at national and local levels; c) Gender awareness raising strategies developed for schools and vocational training institutes, as well as for the general public; d) Strengthening women’s economic empowerment through access to credit, microfinance, and other financial services, both individually and economically through various livelihood support measures; e) Policies, training and leadership programs to support women in taking on decision-making roles at the national and private sector levels.149
- National Action Plan on Gender-based Violence 2017-2021, which details specific actions to reduce and ultimately eradicate gender-based violence.

In particular, the Project will contribute to Pillar I – Prevention of gender-based violence by promoting economic empowerment of women (Outcome 1.4) and gender equality at legal and policy level (Outcome 1.5).

8.4 Stakeholder Response Mechanism

UNEП’s Environmental and Social Sustainability Framework mandates a Stakeholder Response Mechanism (SRM), which provides the opportunity for affected people to seek either compliance review or dispute resolution in regard to the activities that UNEP implements or executes as part of its projects and programmes. Annex 6 sets forth the procedures for submitting a complaint or a dispute. These procedures relate:

- Who can submit a complaint
- How a complaint is communicated
- What information should be included in a complaint
- Information related to the complainant, acknowledgment and tracking
- Information related to maintaining communication with Project-affected communities and status updates
- The SRM’s mandate to build consensus and propose a resolution
- The right of project-affected communities to seek compliance through the SRM

The SRM serves as an independent mechanism for local grievance redress. The local Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) to be established for the Project. The Project Cooperation Agreement (PCA) with the national Executive Entity (EE) will require that the EE puts in place a local GRM, including stakeholder consultations and national procedures relating to grievance redress. The GRM will be gender and age-inclusive and responsive and will address potential access barriers to women (including adolescents), men and youth, as well as other vulnerable groups. The Gender Expert will provide guidance on the development of the GRM and its implementation in a manner that is consistent with UNEP’s Gender Equality Policy. The Gender Expert will provide guidance on the development of the GRM and its implementation in a manner that is consistent with UNEP’s Gender Equality Policy. The Gender Expert will facilitate communications with and between the GRM and potential claimants, the GRM will seek support from local government, civil society organisations and women’s groups. All individuals who are likely to participate in the Project activities will be made aware of the GRM and the means to address it.

### 8.5 Gender-Based Violence

The Project will engage a Gender Expert who amongst others will be responsible for ensuring a gender-responsive approach to the development of early warning systems (EWS) in the Project, which can minimise the risk of gender-based violence. The Gender Expert will consult with local gender-based violence service providers and other relevant stakeholders including civil society organisations who have long-standing local expertise in addressing gender-based violence – including the Abita Foundation, FOKUPERS, ALFULEA and Rede Feito. These organisations have been identified as key partners in the implementation of Timor-Leste’s National Action Plan on Gender Based Violence.
