# Appendix I – Gender Consultation Workshop in Timor-Leste™

## TIMOR-LESTE

Map Source: ESRI, Government of USA, UNICEF


## LIQUÍCA

### ERMERA
AILEU


## 23 November 2017

Preparatory mission to the Liquiça Municipality

A field mission was conducted in the municipality of Liquiça to prepare the community-based gender consultation workshop. The municipality is chosen based on its variety in landscapes from coastal to upland areas. Common climate-related disasters in the municipality include flooding, landslides and droughts.

M. Teresa Ferreira (National Director for Climate Change), M. Ana Maria de Jesus (Deputy Secretary), M. Marcia Guimaraes (National Consultant) and Mrs Zelia A. Maria (National Directorate for Climate Change) took part in the mission to discuss organisation of the planned gender consultation. The field survey identified two villages, Luculai village (coastal area with flooding hazards) and Ulmera village (coastal area with flooding hazards). Meetings with the heads of Luculai village and Ulmera village were cancelled due to bad weather conditions as mentioned on the previous page.


## 24 November 2017

Gender consultation workshop in the Ulmera village

The group comprised Mrs Kristina (Gender Specialist), Mrs Zelia A. Maria (National Directorate for Climate Change) and Mr Leonardo Gonzales dos Santos (Ministry of Environment) and an Interpreter. Unfortunately, due to the bad weather conditions as mentioned above, the proposed consultations in Luculai village were cancelled.

Additional bilateral meetings were organised with Ms Isabel Pereira (Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit – GIZ) and Mr Lorenzo Juarez (FAO).


---
**Page 184**
---

Background

Ulmera village is located near the coast and is 30 minutes by truck from the capital Dili. The village is situated on swampy, waterlogged ground that is highly vulnerable to flooding from the nearby river. During wet seasons, floodwaters can reach up to two meters deep in some areas of the village. Most houses are made of mud walls with thatched roofs. There is a central meeting place with concrete flooring and copper roofing. The village population is estimated to be around 200 households (Census by UNICEF). The village government is led by a male.

A number of women were also present for the question-and-answer session but left to attend another women’s meeting. Therefore, mostly men were left in the meeting towards the end of the session. The question and answer session explored questions on:

- Who benefits?
- Who has what?
- Who does what?
- Who decides?

Who does what?

The community men and women consider themselves more as farmers than fisherfolk, although some fishing activities are also carried out. Division of labour is distinct between men and women. Men do most of the initial preparation for establishment of a farm or garden. For example, men do the vegetation clearance, turning the soil, mounding and preparing the area for planting, as well as the actual planning of fruit trees and other crops to be planted in the garden. Women are responsible for the harvesting of produce. Men are involved in the husbandry of animals such as pigs, buffalo, sheep, goats and chickens.


---
**Page 185**
---

Some of the animals are used for the dowry and so are considered of higher value than the farm or garden produce. Overall, men and women in Ulmera are engaged only in the informal sector. There are no teachers in the village. The community has a strong tradition of farming, fishing, hunting, making sewings and arts and craft. Men spend at least 11 hours a day outdoors doing farm work, fishing, commercial activities and other income generating activities. Women spend most of their time on domestic work including cooking, cleaning the house, caring for children, and unpaid care or community work.

Flooding is considered a major hazard in the area, although drought and strong winds are also a cause of concern. The women have experienced flooding several times over the past 10 years. Based on experience and traditional knowledge, men trigger flood warning if the rain has continued falling for more than three days. They then inform their wives who subsequently mobilize other men to message the women in each household to raise the alarm of potential flooding. The women are also responsible for informing the community about the impending flood from the men before they start mobilizing more water content into higher ground. The men physically relay the message to households. The message must be short and precise: to move higher ground, take all valuable items such as livestock, food stuffs, and other household items. If a warning is received by women, the women collect all valuable household items, including paper documents, and move them to higher ground. They also inform their husbands of the impending flood. In addition, if the flood comes, the women ensure that the house is properly cleaned so as to avoid health problems for members of the family who may be forced to stay in the house temporarily since it experienced two episodes of floods that resulted in the death of children due to drowning.

Disseminating messages alone does not fully protect the community. The men in the village have also constituted a flood warning committee which is made up of young and old men, women and youths. These measures save human lives, but they are often not enough to save livestock, which are valuable assets for many families as they provide food and income.

There is no government-led early warning system for floods in the village. Current weather information is acquired through TV, for those who have access to one. Radios are better than TV because they are cheaper and more portable. However, only a few people can afford radios. The majority of villagers, men and women, consider SMS warnings not to be useful for their community as most do not own mobile phones or it is expensive to purchase credit for the phones. Women think that radios are good, but it is difficult to receive them because they are relayed from far away.

When asked, the community expresses that if they received early warning for flooding from the Government, they recommend that it be one day or two days ahead so that they have sufficient time to save their livestock and other valuable items. However, given that not everyone has access to a TV, radio is the best option. Women consider that TV is the most reliable source of information because it is more accessible than radios for many households with TVs or radio. The youths feel that SMS may have its advantages but very few people own cell phones and those who do are unable to afford credit.

Therefore, use of TV and radio is still the better option to receive early warning messages.

Apart from the traditional early warning practice, there is no other early warning early for the community. The impact of flooding on the community is that it causes loss of life and property, makes it difficult to save the lives of valuable animals such as pigs, buffalos, sheep and goats. The community could save more assets if they had better information about impending floods.

Land tenure in Ulmera is customary and is passed from generation to generation through the men as leaders. Men

Who has what?

Men raise livestock, which fetch more money than handmade crafts and vegetables that women have a gender role (collecting and harvesting) in to earn money. Men therefore have access to more cash than women do.

Land tenure is customary and is passed from generation to generation through the men as leaders.


---
**Page 186**
---

also have access to traditional knowledge about the land and resources on the land. Women do not have rights over the land or the productive resources of the land.

A household spends approximately $10 per week on food and $5 per week on other expenses. The household mostly spends money on cultural obligations such as on dowry and funerals. The community is a subsistence community and so produce their own food for consumption. Healthcare services are free, and people do not spend money to go to the hospital.

Few men, women and youths have access to mobile phones. Some households have access to TV and radios, and very few read the newspaper. The most preferred media for men is radio; for women it is TV and for young people it is both TV and radio. Mobile phones are not a common communication device in the community as they are expensive.

No training has been provided by the government on early warning systems or disaster risk reduction. They have no access to services at municipality level, including early warning systems. The community would like to have relevant disaster preparation training provided to them to improve their knowledge and understanding of threats and to avoid associated risks.


Who decides?
Men decide on household resources, assets and finances. Women have limited direct influence in decision making; although in community women’s groups they are very active and have the space to make decisions. Young people do not participate in decision-making as they belong to a weather warning organisation or network in the community or outside of the community.


Who benefits?
The following needs with regards to climate impacts were expressed by men, women, young people and people with a disability:

Men:
- More lead time (days) to save livestock
- Improve the store wall to divert water from the community
- Access to radios for early warnings; and
- Access to relevant training.

Women:
- At least two days lead time to save household content;
- Receive messages through TV;
- Ensure that women take part in relaying early warning messages to the community; and
- Access to relevant training.

Young people:
- Access to radio for information; and
- Access to relevant training in preparedness for better responses.

People with a disability:
- More lead time to save lives;
- Participate in contact; and
- Relevant training for people with a disability.


Conclusion
For an effective early warning system for flooding, both men and women consider saving lives as their primary goal. Secondary to that, men consider saving livestock as important; and women consider saving valuable household content during floods and ensuring the health of household members after floods as also important. Young people are not considered in decision-making processes by the community or in disaster preparedness training. Men do not consider the training of young boys in preparedness for flood events. Women with a disability. No specific mention was made for the preparedness of young girls, although it is clear that girls would be


---
**Page 187**
---

more vulnerable in floods than boys. Furthermore, for an effective early warning system for floods in the area, both men and women consider an early warning system with a lead time of at least two days would be better suited to specific needs, priorities, and perceived benefits. Use of auto leaders as champions is also considered important by women, who also value person-person contact in information dissemination.
